\begin{abstract}
\begin{itemize}\setlength\itemsep{0.35em}
\item \textbf{The question.} Five companies --- Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Oracle --- are spending about \$780B this year on AI data centers, up from \$448B in 2025~\cite{ref:epoch_capex,ref:mlq2026}; Musk's SpaceX/xAI and the GPU-rental firms add about \$140B more. Will the revenue arrive fast enough to pay for them?
\item \textbf{The method.} A Fermi estimate: the answer is built from many small, bounded, sourced factors, and the uncertainty in each is carried through to the result. Every input sits in a public register anyone can change.
\item \textbf{The cost.} The buildings and chips cost about \$890B a year to carry by 2030 --- wear, running costs and a return on the money --- even if spending merely stays flat, because each year's spending is still on the books when the next arrives.
\item \textbf{The revenue.} AI revenue in 2026 is about \$179B. To cover the cost by 2030 it must grow about 57\% a year, every year.
\item \textbf{What the sectors deliver.} Counted sector by sector --- fifteen of them, each with its own ceiling --- it grows about 33\% a year, and only \$480--490B of it reaches the owners of the data centers.
\item \textbf{The gap.} The most likely outcome is a shortfall of about \$420B a year in 2030, more than five standard deviations from break-even; more if spending keeps rising as the companies now say it will. 2027--29 is a loss on every path.
\item \textbf{New in this version: every builder counted.} SpaceX/xAI, CoreWeave, Nebius and others now build at hyperscaler scale. The rent they earn was already in the revenue the paper counts; their costs were not. With both, the gap is \$420B, not the \$260B the five largest alone would show.
\item \textbf{How long investors wait.} Longer than the horizon. Even spending cut back to follow the revenue that actually arrives turns a yearly profit only from 2032, and by 2035 it has not earned an ordinary return (about 9.5\% a year) on the money, counting the early losses.

  On a flat path the money has lost about 4\% a year on average by 2035. If spending keeps rising, about 10\%. And the losses are not free: they are paid for by borrowing (Alphabet's long-term debt rose from \$21.6B to \$125.1B in a year~\cite{ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029}) or by giving up other earnings, so the hole grows with interest.

  In today's money it is worse: after inflation (about 2.6\% a year, counted once) flat spending is losing about 6.5\% a year on average in 2035, while fixed-price contracts lose value and power and labor costs rise. Tax, too, widens the gap rather than closing it.
\item \textbf{What would prove it wrong.} AI revenue above \$440B in calendar 2028. This is a probability assessment, not a forecast.
\item \textbf{Why growth slows.} Time saved is not money saved; checking AI's work takes time; much of the value goes to buyers and integrators rather than the cloud; countries carve out their own clouds; and the grid and local consent hold projects back.
\item \textbf{The way out.} Not a crash: spending that tracks the revenue actually booked turns a yearly profit around 2032. A flat path does not reach a yearly profit by 2035; an arms race never does.
\item \textbf{Cross-checks.} Goldman Sachs's September 2026 framework needs 27 cents of revenue a year per dollar of capital; this paper's carry is 28. The two differ on scope and on one input, facility operating cost.
\end{itemize}

\noindent\small Model, data and code are open. Every input is appraised in Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}, the update protocol is in Appendix~\ref{app:update}, and a glossary of terms is in Appendix~\ref{app:glossary}.
\end{abstract}

They are about nine-tenths of 2026 spending by the world's nine largest cloud operators; the Chinese four (\$50--110B a year) are tracked but kept outside the model~\cite{ref:trendforce_csp9}. \section*{What Is New in Version 28 (29 October 2026): the Q3 Earnings}\label{sec:v28}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What Is New in Version 28}
Version 28 is the post-earnings rebuild promised in Appendix~\ref{app:update}. Every input that moved is listed below with its source; the delta log in Appendix~\ref{app:update} gives old and new values. Nvidia (19 November) and Oracle (December) report later and will enter as v28.1, with Anthropic's amended S-1 if it is filed by then. An expandable version of this list, with the detail behind each bullet, is in the repository (\texttt{CHANGES.md}).

\subsection*{The headline}\label{ag:headline}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The market asked the paper's question.} On 29 October Alphabet had its worst day as a public company --- down nearly 13\%, about \$480--500B of value~\cite{ref:cnbc_goog_1029,ref:yahoo_goog_1029} --- on results in which Google Cloud revenue \emph{doubled}. Investors sold two things: the spending --- capex of \$51.4B in the quarter, free cash flow of $-$\$9.1B, long-term debt up from \$21.6B to \$125.1B in a year, and the CFO's statement that ``we continue to expect our capex to increase significantly in 2027''~\cite{ref:alphabet_q3_2026,ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029} --- and a slowdown in search advertising (next subsection). Whether the revenue can carry the build-out is now being priced, at the largest scale yet.
\item \textbf{The result moves, because the count is now complete: $-$\$422B/yr in 2030, $z=-5.56$.} For the five largest builders alone nothing moved: calendar-2026 capex is unchanged at $\sim$\$780B, the 2026 revenue base, re-derived from Anthropic's filed financials, is \$179B (P10--P90 \$168--191B), and the 2030 shortfall is \$259B ($z=-3.54$). What changed is the count: the builders outside the five whose rent the model already counted --- SpaceX/xAI, CoreWeave, Nebius, IREN, Applied Digital, about \$140B in 2026 --- are now in the cost as well (next pages). Break-even growth on a plateau is 57\%/yr to 2030; the sector chains still deliver about 33\%. Under the re-issue rule the change is \emph{material} (Appendix~\ref{app:update}).
\item \textbf{Flat spending is now the conservative case.} No company guided down. Alphabet (``increase significantly''), Amazon (``spend meaningfully more in 2027''), Meta (``continue investing aggressively'') and Microsoft (fiscal-2027 capex to grow, Q2 above \$50B) all point up~\cite{ref:alphabet_q3_2026,ref:amzn_q3_2026,ref:meta_q3_2026,ref:msft_q1fy27}. The street's 2027 figure is about \$1.1T~\cite{ref:gs_capex_bbg}. The paper's arms-race path, not its plateau, is the one the companies describe.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{What spooked investors: AI and the search business}\label{ag:search}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{More searches, less money per search.} Google Search revenue grew 12\% (\$63.4B), down from 17\% in the second quarter, in the same quarter that Google said search query growth was the highest in five years~\cite{ref:alphabet_q3_2026,ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029}. Faster query growth with slower revenue growth means revenue per query grew more slowly, or fell.

  The paper's reading: AI answers are replacing some of the ad-bearing results they sit beside. Analysts called the search slowdown the point that ``outweighs all other points in the print''~\cite{ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029}.
\item \textbf{The paper already carries this.} AI enters the revenue base only as an \emph{uplift} to advertising (25 / 40 / 55 \$B, Table~\ref{tab:basedecomp}), and the search chain assumes AI answers ``defend share rather than expand it'', adding only 5--15\% of search revenue (Appendix~\ref{app:sectors}). If AI answers take revenue from search rather than add to it, the uplift belongs at the low end, and the gap widens.
\item \textbf{No input changed for it yet.} One quarter is a signal, not a measurement, and the register moves only on a sourced figure. Two more quarters of falling revenue per query would move the advertising uplift to its low end, worth about \$15B a year of 2026 base and more by 2030. It is on the watch list for v28.1.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{How long investors wait: the hole, the payback, the return}\label{ag:cum}
The yearly cost in this paper already includes a return on the money: about 9--10\% a year (Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}). So ``break-even'' here means earning an ordinary investor's return, not merely covering costs; a positive cumulative figure means the shortfall in that return has been made up.

Table~\ref{tab:cumyear} adds up each year's surplus or deficit from 2027, with revenue growing as the fifteen sector chains say it will (about 33\% a year on average to 2030, slowing after).
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Cumulative surplus ($+$) or deficit ($-$) from 2027, by spending path, all builders (\$B, median, bottom-up revenue, rounded to \$10B)}
\label{tab:cumyear}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Path} & \textbf{2027} & \textbf{2028} & \textbf{2029} & \textbf{2030} & \textbf{2031} & \textbf{2032} & \textbf{2033} & \textbf{2034} & \textbf{2035} \\
\midrule
Flat spending (plateau) & $-$230 & $-$540 & $-$920 & $-$1{,}340 & $-$1{,}750 & $-$2{,}130 & $-$2{,}480 & $-$2{,}790 & $-$3{,}050 \\
Arms race (street path) & $-$270 & $-$710 & $-$1{,}320 & $-$2{,}060 & $-$2{,}860 & $-$3{,}670 & $-$4{,}480 & $-$5{,}300 & $-$6{,}100 \\
35\% cut (rationalize) & $-$180 & $-$370 & $-$530 & $-$640 & $-$680 & $-$660 & $-$580 & $-$420 & $-$190 \\
Spending tracks revenue & $-$230 & $-$500 & $-$740 & $-$900 & $-$920 & $-$830 & $-$650 & $-$410 & $-$140 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Flat spending} digs a hole of about \$1.34T by 2030 and \$3.05T by 2035. Each year's loss shrinks after 2030, but every year to 2035 is a loss.
\item \textbf{An arms race} --- the path the companies now describe --- never earns its return: the hole passes \$6T by 2035.
\item \textbf{Cutting back} is the only way the losses stop growing. A 35\% cut is profitable year by year from 2032; its hole bottoms out near \$680B in 2031 and is still about \$190B in 2035. Spending that simply tracks booked revenue bottoms near \$920B in 2031 and is still about \$140B short in 2035. Neither is repaid within the horizon.
\item \textbf{The losses cost money: interest or lost earnings.} Spending ahead of revenue has to be financed --- by borrowing, as Alphabet now is (long-term debt \$21.6B $\to$ \$125.1B in a year~\cite{ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029}), or by giving up earnings the money would have made elsewhere. Either way the hole compounds. Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc} charges the cost of money ($\sim$9.5\% a year) on the losses themselves.
\item \textbf{The losses are not part of capex.} Capex is the money spent on buildings and chips. The yearly cost above is what that capex costs to hold. The losses are revenue falling short of that cost, and they come out of the owners' profits and cash --- lower earnings, then write-downs. With the cost of money charged on them, the deepest point for spending that tracks revenue is about \$1.18T rather than \$920B, and in 2035 it is still about \$800B short.
\item \textbf{The return on the money, with the early losses included.} Table~\ref{tab:roiyear} gives the average return earned on the money still invested, from 2027 to each year, before tax. An ordinary investor's return is about 9.5\%. By 2035 spending that tracks revenue has averaged about 9\% and a 35\% cut about 8\% --- just short of it; flat spending averages about $-$4\%; an arms race about $-$10\%. No path earns an ordinary return on the money, early losses included, within the horizon.
\item \textbf{Inflation, counted once.} Over a nine-year wait, inflation matters --- but it must not be counted twice.
  \begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Already in:} the cost of money (9.45\%) is a nominal rate, so it already contains the inflation investors expect. Whether and when the hole closes therefore stands as computed; deflating them again would double-count. Stated in 2026 dollars, both sides shrink together and the crossover years, where there are any, do not move (Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc}, bottom).
  \item \emph{Not added to revenue:} receipts are counted in today's prices --- multi-year contracts are fixed in dollars when signed, AI unit prices are falling, and the sector ceilings are built from today's prices. So no inflation uplift is given to revenue.
  \item \emph{What it changes:} the returns investors actually keep. Inflation is taken at 2.0 / 2.6 / 3.7\% a year --- the Federal Reserve's 2\% goal, September core CPI (2.6\%) and headline CPI (3.7\%)~\cite{ref:fed_goal,ref:bls_cpi_sep26}. After it, an ordinary return is about 6.5\%; flat spending averages about $-$6.5\% a year in 2035 and an arms race about $-$12\% (Table~\ref{tab:roiyear}, bottom).
  \item \emph{Fixed prices, rising costs:} a fixed-price contract signed in 2026 is worth about a fifth less in real terms by 2035, while power, labor and site costs rise with prices. The model holds facility opex at its 2026 share of gross stock; letting it rise with inflation would add about \$16B to the 2030 carry and \$50B by 2035 on the flat path. That is left out of the base (no source yet for the escalation clauses) and shown here as a sensitivity: it makes the result worse, not better.
  \end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Worked example: one dollar lost in 2027, recovered in 2035.} Every step can be checked on a calculator.
  \begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{The cost of waiting, in dollars.} The dollar lost in 2027 is borrowed, or taken from other investments. At the cost of money, 9.45\% a year, the owner needs $1.0945^{8}=2.06$ dollars back in 2035 just to be even. This is what Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc} charges.
  \item \emph{How much of that is inflation.} Prices rising 2.6\% a year for eight years multiply by $1.026^{8}=1.23$. A 2035 dollar buys what $1/1.23=0.81$ of a 2026 dollar buys.
  \item \emph{The rest is real earnings given up.} $2.06/1.23=1.67$, which is $1.067^{8}$: about 6.7\% a year in today's money. (Across the Monte Carlo draws the median is about 6.5\%, because the inflation range leans high.) So of the \$1.06 the waiting costs, about a fifth is inflation and four-fifths is real return forgone.
  \item \emph{The revenue side gets no uplift.} A contract signed in 2026 to pay \$1 in 2035 still pays \$1 --- worth 81 cents in today's money. The model already counts it as \$1 and never inflates it, so nothing is added here and nothing needs removing.
  \item \emph{Why the payback year does not move.} To state everything in today's money, each year's hole and each year's recovery are divided by the same price level. Dividing a number by a positive number never changes its sign, so whether and when the hole closes is the same either way. On the full count of builders it does not close by 2035 on any path, in either kind of dollar (Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc}, both halves). Deflating the 9.45\% rate a second time would have pushed payback later for no reason: that is the double count avoided.
  \item \emph{What does change.} The return investors keep. In 2035 the flat path's average return is about $-$4\% in dollars of each year, $(0.960/1.027)-1\approx-6.5\%$ in today's money: losing purchasing power every year for nine years.
  \end{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Tax, counted honestly.} The returns above are before tax, by design: the 9.45\% cost of money is an after-tax rate, and the paper compares it with pre-tax profit. Does tax change the picture? The 2025 tax law made 100\% first-year write-off of equipment permanent for property acquired after 19 January 2025~\cite{ref:irs_notice_2026_11}, and the builders' tax bills fell sharply in 2025 --- Amazon's from about \$9B to \$1.2B, Meta's from \$9.6B to \$2.8B, Alphabet's from \$21.1B to \$13.8B~\cite{ref:yahoo_tax_2026}. It does not rescue the build-out, for two reasons, shown step by step (\texttt{model/tax\_check.py}, flat path):
  \begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{The write-off delays tax; it does not cancel it.} Chips written off at once for tax are still depreciated over their lives in the books; the tax not yet paid is an interest-free loan from the Treasury, repaid as the books catch up. Amazon says so itself: the policy ``ultimately doesn't change the amount of tax we pay''~\cite{ref:yahoo_tax_2026}. Its value is the interest saved on that loan: about \$11--33B a year by 2030 (tax rate 15--21\%; chips only, or chips plus half of the power and cooling plant).
  \item \emph{Tax on the return itself.} To keep 9.45\% after a tax rate of 15--21\%, the owners must earn about \$42--63B a year more profit by 2030 than the carry charges. The base leaves this out.
  \item \emph{Net.} Tax makes the 2030 gap \emph{wider} by about \$20--45B a year, not narrower. The base stays before tax, as stated throughout, so that the comparison is with the profit the companies report; the effect of tax is given here so that no reader has to guess its sign.
  \end{enumerate}
\item \textbf{What average growth would do it on a flat path:} about 57\% a year to break even in 2030, and about 74\% a year to have made up the 2027--29 losses by then (Table~\ref{tab:breakeven}). The sector chains deliver about 33\%.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Beyond the Big-5: the other builders, and why they are added}\label{ag:spacex}
\claim{Musk's SpaceX/xAI and the GPU-rental firms now build at hyperscaler scale. Their rent was already in the revenue the model counts; from v28 their cost is in the carry too --- and the 2030 shortfall rises from about \$260B to about \$420B.}

\inbrief{
\item Five builders outside the Big-5 will spend about \$140B on AI in 2026: SpaceX/xAI ($\sim$\$75B), CoreWeave ($\sim$\$37B), Nebius ($\sim$\$22B), IREN and Applied Digital ($\sim$\$6B) --- company-stated figures.
\item The rent they earn from labs and clouds was already inside the model's revenue; their buildings and chips were not in its cost. Revenue in, cost out flattered the build-out.
\item With them in the base, the 2030 shortfall on a flat path is about \$420B a year ($z=-5.6$), break-even growth 57\% a year, and no path earns an ordinary return on the money within the horizon.
\item Builders that publish no figure --- Meta's off-balance-sheet Hyperion venture, Anthropic's own sites, Crusoe, Lambda, Nscale, Tesla, the Gulf --- are still out. Each would widen the gap.
}

\lead{Why they were left out.} The paper counted the builders whose spending is reported in SEC filings and reconstructed quarter by quarter by Epoch AI --- Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Oracle~\cite{ref:epoch_capex}. Until 2026 the others were private, small, or both: xAI spent \$5.6B on AI in 2024 and \$12.7B in 2025~\cite{ref:tc_spacex_s1}; CoreWeave \$8.7B and \$10.3B~\cite{ref:crwv_q4_2025}.

\lead{Why they are added now.} Two reasons: scale, and symmetry.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Scale.} SpaceX's AI capex was \$7.7B in the first quarter of 2026, \$15.8B in the second and \$16.4B in the third; its fourth quarter is guided to \$35--40B in total, ``almost entirely'' for compute --- about \$75B for 2026 and a run rate of \$140--160B a year~\cite{ref:baxtel_spacex_q2,ref:il_spacex_q3,ref:hl_spacex_q3,ref:basenor_spacex_q4}. CoreWeave guides \$35--39B for 2026~\cite{ref:crwv_q2_2026}; Nebius \$20--25B~\cite{ref:nebius_2026}; IREN and Applied Digital about \$6B between them~\cite{ref:iren_2026,ref:apld_fy26}. Together, about \$140B --- 18\% on top of the Big-5's \$780B.
\item \emph{Symmetry --- the main reason.} The model counts revenue reaching \emph{any} owner of AI data centers: the labs' compute bills sit inside the cloud-capture share whoever is paid. Anthropic pays SpaceX \$1.25B a month to May 2029 for Colossus~1~\cite{ref:baxtel_spacex_q2}; Microsoft provides about two-thirds of CoreWeave's revenue, and OpenAI and Meta most of the rest~\cite{ref:crwv_customers}. That rent was counted as receipts; the cost of the buildings and chips that earn it was not. A model that wants to be checked cannot keep revenue in and cost out.
\item \emph{No double count.} The Big-5 buy this capacity as a service, not as property: it is not in their cash capex or their finance leases, which is what the paper counts. Where a Big-5 company books a finance lease on a neocloud site, it would appear in both; no such lease has been found, and the register flags the check for each filing.
\end{itemize}

\lead{Does a new builder take revenue from the others?} Yes --- and that is why adding one widens the gap. The revenue side of the model is set by what end customers pay: enterprises, consumers, advertisers. A new builder creates no new customers; it divides the same revenue among more owners.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Rent moves, it does not grow.} The \$15B a year Anthropic pays SpaceX is \$15B that Azure, AWS or Google Cloud did not receive for the same work.
\item \emph{Captive uses are still paid for by end customers.} Grok, robotaxis and robots earn money only when subscribers, riders or advertisers pay; that revenue is already in the sector chains (consumer, autonomous fleets, coding tools). If SpaceX's computers serve it, the other clouds do not.
\item \emph{Compute in orbit.} If SpaceX builds data centers in space, that spending appears in its capex like any other, probably with a shorter life (a satellite's years, not a building's decades) and so a higher yearly carry; its customers are the same end customers.
\item \emph{So:} the industry's cost rises by the new builders' cohorts; its revenue does not. The Big-5 are squeezed twice --- by the lost rent and by the lower prices more capacity brings.
\end{itemize}

\lead{How they enter the model.} Their AI capex is added to the Big-5's AI-attributable capex year by year (register entry \texttt{other\_builders\_ai\_capex}: 0.5, 14.3, 27.8 and 140.4 \$B for 2023--26) and goes through the same vintage model --- same lives, energization lags, grid cap, facility opex and 9.45\% cost of money. From 2027 they follow the same path as the Big-5: flat on a plateau, up with an arms race, down 35\% when the Big-5 cut. All of it is AI spending, so no non-AI baseline is subtracted. The management claim that SpaceX's compute earns ``less than a one-year payback''~\cite{ref:baxtel_spacex_q2} is recorded as an interested party's judgment (grade J), like the Big-5's own claims (Section~\ref{sec:gs}).

\lead{What it does to the result.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{2030, flat path:} carry \$725B $\to$ \$890B; shortfall \$260B $\to$ \$420B a year; $z$ $-3.5\to-5.6$; break-even growth 49\% $\to$ 57\% a year. The sector chains still deliver about 33\%.
\item \emph{A 35\% cut:} was about even in 2030; now \$110B short in 2030 and profitable year by year from 2032.
\item \emph{The wait:} with the cost of money, no path is repaid by 2035 (Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc}).
\item \emph{The Big-5 alone} are unchanged: about \$260B short in 2030. The difference is the other builders' cost, which their share of the same revenue does not cover.
\end{itemize}

\lead{Who is still out.} Meta's Hyperion venture with Blue Owl (\$27B and more, built off Meta's balance sheet: its CFO said the construction ``will not be recorded in capex''~\cite{ref:meta_hyperion}); Anthropic's own sites with Fluidstack (\$50B over several years); Crusoe, Lambda and Nscale, which publish no capex; Tesla's training clusters (about 400 MW by end-2026, no dollar figure); and the Gulf builders. Each would add cost and no revenue. They are left out only because no stated figure exists yet; the register lists them, and the next filings will bring some in.

\subsection*{New sources}\label{ag:sources}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Anthropic's S-1 (public 26 October): the first filed lab financials.} First-half 2026 revenue \$16.3B (\$1.2B a year earlier); operating loss \$0.7B; net loss \$80.7B, of which \$72.4B is a non-cash change in the fair value of convertible notes and similar instruments~\cite{ref:anthropic_s1}. The \$80.7B is therefore \emph{not} cash burn and is not used as such.
\item \textbf{The paper's Anthropic estimate held.} v27 put Anthropic's 2026 bookings at 50--65 (mode 57), implying a third quarter of \$16--19B. The third quarter, reported to investors but not yet filed, was \$16.8B~\cite{ref:cnbc_anthropic_q3}. The component is narrowed to 53--60 (mode 56).
\item \textbf{The register's September revision is reversed.} On 29 September the base had been raised to \$187B on press reports of Anthropic's expected \$100B+ exit run-rate. The filed numbers do not support it; the base returns to \$179B. This is the paper's warning --- run-rates overstate bookings --- confirmed by the first filed figure.
\item \textbf{Cloud growth accelerated again.} Google Cloud \$30.7B (+103\%), cloud backlog \$561B~\cite{ref:alphabet_q3_2026}; Azure +48\%, commercial remaining performance obligation \$677B~\cite{ref:msft_q1fy27}; AWS \$46.0B (+39\%), backlog \$578B, growth guided above 40\% in 2027 and 2028~\cite{ref:amzn_q3_2026}. This is the bull case realized, and is already inside the sector chains' 2027 growth of 40--80\%.
\item \textbf{Oracle: the first signal of the rationalize path.} At its 28 October Investor Day Oracle said that ``following a period of peak capex in FY27--FY28, capital expenditures are expected to step-down in FY29'', with positive free cash flow from the second half of FY28~\cite{ref:orcl_investor_day}. That is the paper's 35\%-cut path described by one of the five.
\item \textbf{Anthropic's compute commitments.} The S-1 refers to committed spend ``substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure''; press reports of the draft give noncancelable commitments of \$111B to Google, \$110B to AWS and \$31B to Microsoft, and up to \$750B in all~\cite{ref:anthropic_s1,ref:dealroom_s1}. A large part of the hyperscalers' future ``AI revenue'' is one lab's investor capital returning to them --- the circular flow of Section~\ref{sec:circ}, now in a filing.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Accounting changes that make the carry look smaller}\label{ag:acct}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Microsoft lengthened building lives from 15 to 25 years} from fiscal 2027, and books new data-center leases as operating rather than finance leases~\cite{ref:cfodive_msft}. Both lower \emph{reported} capex and depreciation; neither changes what the buildings cost or how long the chips inside them last. The paper's carry is economic and keeps shell lives at 15/20/25 years.
\item \textbf{Three-quarters of Microsoft's capex is now chips.} ``Roughly 3/4 of our CapEx was for short-lived assets, primarily CPUs and GPUs''~\cite{ref:msft_q1fy27}. The paper's silicon share (45--65\%) is a five-company average; Microsoft's mix supports the upper half, which shortens the blended life and front-loads the carry.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Easier to read}\label{ag:read}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{A plain-language front.} The abstract is eleven short points, followed by an at-a-glance box and a reading guide with page numbers.
\item \textbf{The argument in the titles.} Every section and subsection opens with a one-line claim in blue, and the longest open with an \emph{In brief} box: the simple argument first, the detail below for readers who want to dig in.
\item \textbf{Short paragraphs with headlines.} Long paragraphs are split, with a blank line between them, and most open with a short blue headline, so the argument can be followed by scanning.
\item \textbf{Symbols named first.} Quantities are named in words before their symbols are used.
\item \textbf{Colour figures and a cleaner page.} All figures are redrawn in colour; the flow diagram labels every box and arrow; every page fits its margins.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Errata: where earlier versions erred}\label{ag:errata}
Errors in published versions are listed here and kept, version by version, in the repository's \texttt{ERRATA.md}; nothing is corrected silently.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{All versions to v27.1, scope (the largest).} Revenue reaching \emph{every} owner of AI data centers was counted, but the cost of owning them was counted only for the five largest. The rent SpaceX/xAI, CoreWeave, Nebius and other builders earn from labs and clouds was in; their buildings and chips were not. That understated the gap: the 2030 plateau shortfall is $\sim$\$420B, not $\sim$\$260B, and no path is repaid with the cost of money by 2035 (it was 2034--35 at best). Corrected in v28 (p.~\pageref{ag:spacex}). The error was in the direction of the build-out.
\item \textbf{v26.x--v27.1, abstract.} The abstract quoted a calendar-2026 revenue base of \$187B, a 2030 plateau deficit of \$255B and $g^{*}=48\%$, from a register run in which the base had been raised on 29 September on a press report of Anthropic's expected \$100B+ exit run-rate.

The body tables (Tables~\ref{tab:breakeven} and~\ref{tab:bu_results}) were computed on \$179B and showed $\sim$\$260B and 49\%. The filed S-1 supports \$179B. Effect on the conclusion: none (both inside the propagated uncertainty), but the abstract and the tables disagreed.
\item \textbf{v26.x--v27.1, register.} Raising the base on a run-rate report broke the paper's own rule that run-rates overstate bookings. From v28 a lab component moves only on a filing or a booked-revenue figure.
\end{itemize}
\subsection*{Not an erratum, but worth flagging}
\begin{itemize}
\item A figure of ``\$300B of 2027 capex'' for Alphabet circulated widely after the call. It is a press estimate, not company guidance, and the paper does not use it.
\item The revenue base is now stated consistently as \$179B throughout; v27's abstract carried a register run on the superseded \$187B base (\$255B for the five largest). On the same base, the five largest alone are $\sim$\$260B short in 2030; every builder, $\sim$\$420B.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Still waiting on}\label{ag:waiting}
Anthropic's amended S-1 with filed third-quarter figures (expected in November; its first-half statements go stale around 11--12 November); Nvidia's third quarter (19 November); Oracle's fiscal second quarter (December); a post-earnings consensus for 2027--28 capex; CoreWeave's third quarter (10 November) and SpaceX's fourth quarter (late January); lease notes in the Big-5's 10-Ks, to confirm that no neocloud capacity is booked as a finance lease.
\newpage

\section*{Executive Summary}\label{ag:exec}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{The wait is the investor's problem.} The money spent in 2025--27 does not earn an ordinary return within the horizon. Counting the early losses and a cost of money of $\sim$9.5\%, spending that follows revenue, and a 35\% cut, both turn a yearly profit only from 2032 and have averaged 8--9\% by 2035 --- just short; flat spending averages $\sim-4$\%/yr by 2035; an arms race $\sim-10$\% (Tables~\ref{tab:cumyear} and~\ref{tab:roiyear}). After inflation ($\sim$2.6\%/yr, counted once) the flat path is losing $\sim$6.5\% a year on average in 2035.

    The plain losses bottom out near \$700--900B on the paths that recover and pass \$3T on the flat path. That delay, not whether AI is useful, is what investors are pricing.
    \item \textbf{Every builder counted.} SpaceX/xAI ($\sim$\$75B of AI capex in 2026), CoreWeave ($\sim$\$37B), Nebius ($\sim$\$22B), IREN and Applied Digital ($\sim$\$6B) build at hyperscaler scale. The model already counted the rent they earn from labs and clouds but not their cost. With both, the 2030 flat-path shortfall is $\sim$\$420B a year, not $\sim$\$260B; a new builder adds cost and divides the same customers' money among more owners (p.~\pageref{ag:spacex}). Off-balance-sheet sites (Meta's Hyperion), Anthropic's own build, Crusoe, Lambda, Nscale and Tesla publish no figure and are still out; each would widen the gap.
    \item \textbf{What the October earnings said.} Every company that gave a direction pointed 2027 spending up; Alphabet lost $\sim$\$500B of value in a day on its spending and a slowdown in search advertising, as AI answers replace ad-bearing results; Anthropic's first filed numbers matched the paper's estimate; and Oracle said its spending peaks in 2027--28 and steps down after --- the first builder to describe the way out (What Is New in Version 28).
    \item \textbf{Labs and landlords.} The labs and the infrastructure owners fund, supply and compete with one another; Google and Meta are both. Who wins the squeeze moves the gap between them, not the gap with the customer: the carry is paid from the same end-customer dollar either way (Section~\ref{sec:labslandlords}).
    \item \textbf{The losses cost money too.} A company that spends ahead of revenue pays for the shortfall, either in interest on debt or in earnings it gives up elsewhere. Alphabet's long-term debt rose from \$21.6B to \$125.1B in the year to September~\cite{ref:yahoo_nasdaq_1029}.

    Charging $\sim$9.5\% a year on the losses, the flat path's hole is $\sim$\$1.5T by 2030 and $\sim$\$4.5T by 2035, against \$1.34T and \$3.05T added up plainly; an arms race reaches $\sim$\$8.5T by 2035; and even spending that follows revenue is still $\sim$\$800B short in 2035 (Table~\ref{tab:cumwacc}).
    \item \textbf{The question is the growth rate $g$, not the demand level $D$.} A stock built ahead of demand is rational if demand compounds fast enough. Comparing a stock to a demand level lets each side pick its year. The vintage model instead asks: at what sustained growth rate does the installed base earn its carry, and by when? (Figures~\ref{fig:scissors} and~\ref{fig:breakeven}.)
    \item \textbf{The carry ramps.} Net of a non-AI baseline that the flat 2023 capex total pins at $\sim$\$130B for that year, growing 2--10\%/yr since, and with the other builders' AI capex added, the AI-attributable stock is $\sim$\$1.3T entering 2027 and $\sim$\$2.8T by 2029. Its carry is $\sim$\$465B in 2027, $\sim$\$645B in 2028, $\sim$\$890B in 2030 on a plateau (Figure~\ref{fig:carry}) --- siting friction defers some of it a year and then adds cost. A 35\% capex cut flattens it near \$580B; an arms race pushes it to \$1.2T.
    \item \textbf{Break-even growth is 41--70\%/yr sustained to 2030, depending on spending.} From a calendar-2026 base of $\sim$\$179B, plateau needs $g^{*}\approx57\%$ (2030) and $\sim$74\% to recover the 2027--29 deficits cumulatively; rationalization needs $\sim$41\%; arms race $\sim$70\%. Across the low and high ends of the decomposed base (\$163--194B) the plateau figure runs 61\% to 54\%.
    \item \textbf{The sectors deliver about 33\%, not 57\%.} Fifteen independent sector chains (Section~\ref{sec:bottomup}, Figure~\ref{fig:sectors}) put 2030 end-customer AI revenue at $\sim$\$625B and infrastructure receipts at $\sim$\$480B --- office assistants and agents ($\sim$\$135B), advertising uplift ($\sim$\$118B), consumer ($\sim$\$60B) and coding tools ($\sim$\$60B) dominate; everything else is under \$50B each. Growth decays as sectors approach seat-, budget-, user- and yield-set ceilings. The 2030 shortfall against a plateau carry is $\sim$\$420B/yr, a 5.6$\sigma$ result (Figure~\ref{fig:reconcile}).
    \item \textbf{The observed growth rate is above the threshold today, unevenly.} Anthropic's revenue went from \$1.2B in the first half of 2025 to \$16.3B in the first half of 2026 (filed, S-1) and \$16.8B in the third quarter alone (reported)~\cite{ref:anthropic_s1,ref:cnbc_anthropic_q3}; OpenAI's run-rate went from \$21B to \$40B~\cite{ref:epoch_revenue}; Google Cloud grew 103\% in the third quarter~\cite{ref:alphabet_q3_2026}; Microsoft's consumer assistant and Meta's non-advertising AI products have no comparable disclosures.

    Two years of the labs' pace would clear the plateau path by 2029. The sector chains below are the reasons the paper expects deceleration; whether it comes is the 2027--28 test (Section~\ref{sec:falsify}).
    \item \textbf{Where the remaining doubt lives, and whether profit can overlap.} After nineteen decompositions (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}) the residual uncertainty sits in two axes: an adoption/capability factor and the assistant ceiling. Figure~\ref{fig:probmap} maps P(receipts $>$ carry) over both, with a feasibility band built from the enterprise software and services budget --- itself decomposed, because that budget is being torn up: seat-priced SaaS is deflating under agents, European and Chinese buyers are displacing US vendors, and the money is moving from seats to AI solutions.

    On a plateau the 50\% contour sits at a residual factor of $\sim$1.9 --- 2030 AI revenue of $\sim$\$1.18T, near the top of the feasible band (\$920B--\$1.31T), which requires AI to take about a third of a pool that is not growing. Under a 35\% capex cut P(profit) is $\sim$7\% in 2030 and $\sim$58\% by 2032 at the paper's modes.

    That is the paper's result stated as a probability rather than a sign: not a prediction of loss, but a distribution over outcomes and the growth it takes to move it.
    \item \textbf{Economic depreciation and the commodity floor.} Straight-line lives are the accounting view. The economic view (reviewer comment) is that an installed accelerator is worth what it can earn against the newest chip's marginal cost per token, which falls 50--90\% a year; on that schedule the 2023--26 silicon carries an unrecognized impairment of \$100--250B today for the five largest alone (more with the other builders), and the carry is front-loaded into years the paper already calls a sunk deficit. Doubling inference utilization (batching, quantization, open models) serves twice the tokens from the same stock but, if prices fall in step, no more dollars.

    And if prices converge on marginal cash cost --- $\sim$\$0.03--0.05 per million tokens against realized prices of \$0.10--1.0 --- receipts converge on the opex line, $\sim$\$150B in 2028 and $\sim$\$220B in 2030, against a \$890B carry. Section~\ref{sec:commodity}; the metric to watch is realized price per million tokens against that floor.
    \item \textbf{Readiness is a constraint in its own right.} Data Center Watch counts \$130B of US projects blocked or delayed in Q1 2026 alone --- matching all of 2025 --- with 833 opposition groups in 49 states, a New York moratorium, and Ipsos polling near 70\% local opposition~\cite{ref:dcw_q1_2026,ref:forbes_consent,ref:tomshw_dcw}.

    Water, ratepayer bills, noise and diesel are the stated grounds; Dublin and Amsterdam were the European template. The model now defers 10--35\% of each cohort a year for consent and siting, adds 2--9\% to shell cost for community benefits, water mitigation and relocation, and 0--6\% to cooling opex where evaporative cooling is refused. The net effect is to shift the carry later, not to remove it.
    \item \textbf{Why $g$ decays.} Nominal task savings convert to cash at roughly 0.28$\times$ (fractional time, verification, Parkinson--Jevons); realized savings leak to buyers and outcome-priced integrators at 10--50:1 against cloud tokens (Nordhaus); routine inference migrates to 1.45B NPUs a year; sovereignty removes 15--35\% of demand while duplicating capital; grid queues strand cohorts before they earn; and a material share of 2026 lab compute is paid with investor capital (\$122B and \$65B raised this year~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai,ref:techcrunch_anthropic}), not customer revenue.
    \item \textbf{Scale.} A \$920B/yr build-out and a \$420B/yr shortfall sound enormous and are not, against the economies that host them: world GDP is $\sim$\$115T, the US $\sim$\$30T, China $\sim$\$19T, the EU $\sim$\$20T\verify. The Big-5 build-out alone is 1.5--2.5\% of US GDP per year depending on definition (gross, or lease-adjusted as in BofA's cycle comparison~\cite{ref:a16z_som}) --- at the peaks of the railway booms of the 1840s and 1870s and of telecoms in 1999--2001, each of which ended in impairment without ending the technology.
    \item \textbf{The street's path is the arms race.} The street has Big-5 capex at $\sim$\$1.1T in 2027 and $\sim$\$1.15T in 2028~\cite{ref:gs_capex_bbg,ref:a16z_som}, and on the October calls every company that gave a direction for 2027 pointed up (What Is New in Version 28). The paper's plateau is therefore below consensus and its arms-race path is consensus; on that path the 2030 deficit is $\sim$\$745B and no year through 2035 is profitable.

    The strongest statement of the bull case, a16z's September deck, is taken point by point in Section~\ref{sec:a16z}; the two documents agree on nearly every observable and disagree on two judgments --- whether AI is paid from labor budgets as well as IT budgets, and whether today's rising GPU rental prices are a permanent state or a cycle top. The question here is who carries the write-down and when, not whether AI is affordable.
    \item \textbf{The sell-side hurdle agrees with the carry.} Goldman Sachs's September 2026 framework~\cite{ref:gs_roic2026} --- \$1.42T of 2028--30 revenue to earn 15\% on the \$1.73T 2026--27 cohort --- is 27.4 cents per dollar of capital per year; the paper's carry is 28.4 (Section~\ref{sec:gs}, Table~\ref{tab:gs_reconcile}).

    The note isolates one cohort and benchmarks to backlog; the paper stacks the cohorts and forecasts receipts. The one material input difference is facility operating cost (2\% versus 6.65\% of capital), now carried as two anchors in the register.
    \item \textbf{Beyond 2030.} Ending the analysis in 2030 would invite the objection that 2032 makes it all back. Appendix~\ref{app:horizon} runs the model to 2035: on a plateau the annual deficit narrows only to about \$265B by 2035 and the cumulative deficit passes \$3T; under an arms race it stays near \$800B a year; under a 35\% cut, or under an adaptive path in which capex tracks realized receipts, annual profit arrives in 2032, but by 2035 the plain cumulative is still \$140--190B short and, with the cost of money, \$700--800B (Tables~\ref{tab:cumyear} and~\ref{tab:cumwacc}).
    \item \textbf{Consequence, either way.} The 2025--27 cohorts will not earn their carry in 2027--28 under any growth rate. ASC 360-10 recoverability~\cite{ref:fasb_asc360} is tested on projected cash flows, so impairment turns on whether management can still credibly forecast $g\ge g^{*}$ in 2028. That is the observable this paper puts its name on.
\end{itemize}
\newpage

\section{Method: What a Fermi Estimate Can and Cannot Say}\label{sec:method}
\claim{Build the answer from many bounded factors; the few loose ones carry the uncertainty, and those are the only ones worth arguing about.}

\inbrief{\item A Fermi estimate multiplies many bounded factors; errors add in quadrature, so the few loose factors dominate.\item Inputs here are known to tens of percent, not orders of magnitude; the result to a factor of about 1.3.\item The paper therefore decomposes the loose inputs and reports which two or three carry the doubt.\item A conclusion is called robust only when the gap is larger than the propagated uncertainty.}

On July 16, 1945, ten miles from the Trinity tower, Enrico Fermi dropped scraps of paper as the blast wave passed and, from their $\sim$2.5\,m displacement, estimated a yield of about 10 kilotons of TNT~\cite{ref:fermi_trinity}; radiochemistry later gave $21\pm2$ kilotons~\cite{ref:katz2021}. He had the order of magnitude before the instruments were read.

At Chicago he institutionalized the method with the piano-tuner question~\cite{ref:morrison1963,ref:vonbaeyer1993}: chain population, household size, ownership density, tuning frequency and tuner throughput, and the product lands within a factor of two of the directory count.

Why does this work? If the estimate is a product of $n$ independent factors, $Y=\prod X_i$, then $\ln Y=\sum\ln X_i$ and, for independent errors,
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\ln Y}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{\ln X_i}^{2}.
\label{eq:var}
\end{equation}
Errors add in quadrature, and --- the point that matters in practice --- they add \emph{weighted}. Most factors in a well-built chain are known to $\pm$10--20\% ($\sigma_{\ln X}\approx0.1$--0.2); only one or two are loose. The total is then dominated by the loose ones and barely moved by the tight ones: nine factors at $\sigma=0.1$ contribute less than one factor at $\sigma=0.3$. Fermi's paper-scraps got within a factor of two because he had one loose factor and knew it.

\lead{Precision of tens of percent.} A corporate budget is not an order-of-magnitude exercise either: people know what they spend to within 5--20\%. This paper is in that regime. Every input below is bounded by a filing, a disclosure, a physical relation or a budget, and the reader should expect --- and hold the paper to --- precision of tens of percent on inputs and a factor of about 1.3 on the result, not powers of ten.

\lead{What ``Fermi'' means here.} ``Fermi'' here names the method of decomposition and propagation, not a claim that only the order of magnitude is known. Adding tight factors does not accumulate error; it partitions the estimate so that the loose factors are exposed and can be attacked individually.

That is why this paper decomposes demand into fifteen sectors rather than one growth rate, and why Section~\ref{sec:results} reports a variance budget: the reader should see which two or three inputs carry the uncertainty, because those are the only ones worth arguing about.

\lead{Three rules for the uncertainty.} Every numbered equation in the paper is restated and explained term by term, with a worked number, in Appendix~\ref{app:equations}. The full propagation uses three different rules, set out and checked numerically in Appendix~\ref{app:errors}: (1)~\emph{within a chain}, where factors multiply, relative uncertainties add in quadrature ($\sigma^2_{\ln}$ add); (2)~\emph{across chains that are summed} --- sectors, or capex cohorts --- absolute uncertainties add in quadrature, which in relative terms weights each chain by the square of its share of the total, plus any factor the chains have in common, which adds once and does not average away; (3)~\emph{between supply and demand}, which are subtracted, absolute uncertainties again add in quadrature and the result is judged by the gap divided by that combined uncertainty (the $z$-score).

Two disciplines follow: report one or two significant figures, and call a conclusion robust only when the gap exceeds the propagated uncertainty. Where the gap is inside the noise, say so.

\lead{Marked figures.} A red \verify{} after a figure marks a number that rests on a single unaudited or secondary source and is still to be verified against a filing.

\noindent\lead{Open model.} Parameters, distributions, the Epoch AI capex series and the Monte Carlo code are archived with this paper on Zenodo, the CERN-hosted open research repository; the permanent digital object identifier (DOI) for all versions is \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23406475}, which always resolves to the latest.

\section{Prior Literature}
\claim{Others framed the gap as a flow or a task count; this paper turns it into a stock, a growth rate and a probability.}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{The capex--revenue gap.} Cahn~\cite{ref:cahn2024} framed the ``\$600B question'' as a flow comparison; Covello~\cite{ref:covello2024} questioned whether generative models solve problems valuable enough to earn back the stock. We extend both from flow to stock and from level to growth rate.
    \item \textbf{Task-based macro limits.} Acemoglu~\cite{ref:acemoglu2024} bounds AI's contribution to total factor productivity --- output growth not explained by more labor or capital --- at roughly 0.66\% over ten years, by counting the share of tasks AI can do and the cost saving on each. Section~\ref{sec:slack} does the same accounting at the level of a firm's payroll: how much of the nominal saving on tasks becomes cash the firm can actually book.
    \item \textbf{The productivity J-curve.} Brynjolfsson, Rock and Syverson~\cite{ref:brynjolfsson2021} show that general-purpose technologies require complementary intangible investment before measured productivity rises. That lag is why enterprise adoption cycles (8--12 years) are longer than silicon lives (3--5), and it is also the strongest argument for building early: if the productivity is coming, capacity must be there first.

    Section~\ref{sec:demand} engages it directly. Solow's 1987 remark that computers were visible everywhere but in the productivity statistics~\cite{ref:solow1987} is the same phenomenon a generation earlier. Practitioners at the last mile report the same thing from experience: putting AI onto a poorly run process automates the process's defects, and the time goes into fixing the process, not the model.
    \item \textbf{Surplus capture.} Nordhaus~\cite{ref:nordhaus2004} estimates that innovators captured on the order of 2\% of the social surplus from their innovations over 1948--2001, the rest going to customers as lower prices and better products. A 2\% share is a 50:1 ratio of customer benefit to innovator revenue. Section~\ref{sec:lastmile} finds 10--35:1 for AI contact-center automation --- the same phenomenon, measured on one sector.
    \item \textbf{Scaling and spending data.} Epoch AI's capex~\cite{ref:epoch_capex} and revenue~\cite{ref:epoch_revenue} series, and Villalobos et al.\ on the human-text data wall~\cite{ref:epoch_data2024}, supply the empirical spine.
\end{enumerate}
The evidence for the build-out is strong and the paper uses it as input: Google Cloud's contracted backlog reached \$514B in Q2 2026, up from \$462B a quarter earlier, on 82\% revenue growth~\cite{ref:alphabet_q2_2026} (how much of it is AI-specific is not disclosed); the 2026 capex guidance has been revised upward all year~\cite{ref:mlq2026,ref:statista2026}; Goldman models \$5.3T of hyperscaler capex over 2025--2030~\cite{ref:yahoo_goldman2026}; and frontier-lab revenue has grown at multiples per year~\cite{ref:epoch_revenue}.

Those are the observables the model must reconcile with, and the next section lists them with their provenance --- audited filing, company statement, or press report --- because they are not all of the same quality.

\section{What We Observe (2025--2026)}\label{sec:observe}
\claim{Capex has doubled, cloud growth is accelerating and lab revenue is multiplying --- the bull case is real; the question is whether it is fast enough.}

Provenance matters here. The capex series and Nvidia's revenue are audited filings; the lab run-rates are company statements or press reports citing them; the Microsoft, AWS and OpenAI figures are earnings-call remarks and leaked financials, not audited disclosures, and are marked accordingly. Meta's AI spending is monetized through advertising rather than sold as a product, so it has no ``AI revenue'' line at all; the paper treats it as an advertising-uplift channel (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}).

\lead{What the observables say.} Three things follow. Nvidia's \$300B data-center run-rate is consistent with the capex series and with a silicon share near half. Frontier-lab revenue is growing at multiples per year, which is the bull case realized so far. And roughly \$190B of lab equity was raised in 2026 alone, a sum comparable to the labs' combined revenue --- so a material share of the compute they buy from hyperscalers this year is paid with investor capital, not customer cash.

\subsection{Do the five, and the Chinese four, tell the same story?}
\claim{US and Chinese operators are on the same curve.}

They do, and the consistency is itself evidence. Across Alphabet, Microsoft, Amazon and Meta the Q2 2026 pattern is identical: capital expenditure roughly doubling year on year, guidance raised during the year, cloud revenue growth accelerating (Google Cloud +82\%, Azure +43\%, AWS +37\%), and free cash flow turning negative at the two heaviest spenders~\cite{ref:alphabet_q2_2026,ref:futurex_q2}.

The Chinese hyperscalers are on the same curve a year behind and at smaller scale: in Q2 2026 Alibaba's capex was RMB~67.7B (+75\%) and Tencent's RMB~52.8B (+176\%), each about a quarter of quarterly revenue; Alibaba's AI cloud and compute revenue grew 45\% while adjusted profit fell 38\%; Tencent's free cash flow went negative for the first time in its history on RMB~51B of prepayments for computing power; Baidu's capex reached 36\% of revenue with negative free cash flow~\cite{ref:china_q2_capex,ref:36kr_china}.

\lead{The nine largest cloud operators.} TrendForce puts 2026 capex for the nine largest cloud operators --- the Big-5 plus ByteDance, Tencent, Alibaba and Baidu --- at \$830--890B, up 79--90\%, with the Chinese four raising AI capex $\sim$80\%~\cite{ref:trendforce_csp9,ref:taiwannews_csp9}. The same source notes Chinese cloud returns on invested capital of 13--20\% against 25--50\% for US peers~\cite{ref:china_q2_capex}.

\lead{Two implications.}
\begin{itemize}
\item The growth rates the paper's near-term chains assume (40--80\% into 2027) are what every large operator on both sides of the Pacific is reporting, so the base case is not built on a US anomaly.
\item The Chinese four add \$50--110B/yr of capex that is outside the Big-5 register and outside its demand pools; the model is a Big-5 model, and the Chinese stock competes for the same tokens with cheaper power and less efficient silicon (Section~\ref{sec:commodity}).
\end{itemize}

\section{The Supply-Side Carry: A Vintage Model}\label{sec:supply}
\claim{Each year's spending stays on the books for years, so the yearly cost keeps climbing even if spending stops growing.}

\inbrief{\item Each year's spending is a cohort that stays on the books: chips for about four years, buildings for about twenty.\item The yearly cost of the whole stack --- wear, running costs and a return on the money --- is the carry.\item On flat spending the carry climbs from about \$465B in 2027 to about \$890B in 2030, because old cohorts are still being paid for as new ones arrive.\item AI chips run hot and wear out faster than the accounting lives assume, and they cannot be cheaply reused.}

\subsection{Cohorts, not a snapshot}
\claim{Spending is counted by the year it was made, net of the old non-AI baseline, and each year's cohort is carried until it wears out.}

Let $I_c$ be Big-5 capex in cohort year $c$ (Table~\ref{tab:observe}; Figure~\ref{fig:capex}) and $A_c=\max(I_c-N_c,0)$ the AI-attributable part after subtracting the non-AI baseline $N_c=N_{2023}(1+g_N)^{c-2023}$. The baseline is constrained by data: total capex was flat at \$161B in 2023 while AI purchases began~\cite{ref:epoch_capex}, so $N_{2023}=161-A_{2023}$ with $A_{2023}\sim$Triang(20, 30, 40), and it grows at $g_N\sim$Triang(2, 6, 10)\%/yr for ordinary cloud refresh and logistics (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}).

AI-attributable capex above a grid energization ceiling of Triang(1{,}150, 1{,}400, 1{,}680) \$B/yr --- 22--40 GW of new IT load at \$38--55M per MW --- is deferred to the following year and carries return but not depreciation (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}); the ceiling does not bind on a plateau and binds in 5\% of arms-race draws.

\lead{Siting, consent and water.} Separately, a fraction $f_{\text{delay}}\sim$Triang(0.10, 0.20, 0.35) of each cohort from 2026 is deferred a year by siting, consent and water constraints; shell cost carries an uplift $\kappa_{\text{site}}\sim$Triang(1.02, 1.05, 1.09); and facility opex carries $(1+u)$ with a cooling-uplift $u\sim$Triang(0, 0.03, 0.06) for water-constrained cooling (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}).

Each cohort splits into a silicon share $\phi\sim$Triang(0.45, 0.52, 0.60) --- anchored on Nvidia's data-center revenue (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}) --- with an economic life $L_{\text{si}}$ that is the fleet-weighted harmonic mean of the training-cluster life $L_{\text{tr}}\sim$Triang(2.5, 3.5, 4.5) yr and the inference-fleet life $L_{\text{inf}}\sim$Triang(4, 5, 6) yr at a training share $f_{\text{tr}}\sim$Triang(0.35, 0.45, 0.55) --- effective $L_{\text{si}}\approx4.2$ yr (P10--P90 3.75--4.6) --- and a shell/power/cooling share with life $L_{\text{sh}}\sim$Triang(15, 20, 25) yr.

Silicon is energized in its spend year at an energization fraction $\ell_{\text{si}}\sim$Triang(0.5, 0.7, 0.9) and shell at $\ell_{\text{sh}}\sim$Triang(0.2, 0.4, 0.6), the rest the year after (lower-case $\ell$ is a fraction; upper-case $L$ is a life in years). With straight-line depreciation and a mid-year convention, the carry $H_t$ in year $t$ --- the annual cost of owning the installed base, summed over cohorts $c$ and over the two components $j$ (silicon, shell) --- is
\begin{equation}
H_t=\sum_{c\le t}\sum_{j\in\{\text{si},\text{sh}\}}\left[\frac{G_{c,j}}{L_j}+c_{\text{opex}}\,G_{c,j}+r\,G_{c,j}\!\left(1-\frac{t-c+\tfrac12}{L_j}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}\!\left[t-c+\tfrac12<L_j\right]+r\,\text{Deferred}_t,
\label{eq:Ht}
\end{equation}
where $G_{c,j}$ is the in-service gross value of component $j$ of cohort $c$, $L_j$ its life, the facility-opex share $c_{\text{opex}}\sim$Triang(0.059, 0.0665, 0.074) of in-service gross (decomposed in Section~\ref{sec:decomp}), the cost of capital $r\sim$Triang(0.090, 0.0945, 0.099) from CAPM components (Section~\ref{sec:decomp})~\cite{ref:damodaran2026}, and $\mathbf{1}[\cdot]$ an indicator that is 1 while the cohort is younger than its life. Forward paths: \textbf{Plateau} (780 flat), \textbf{Arms race} (1{,}000, then 1{,}100; consistent with the \$1T+ 2027 projections in~\cite{ref:mlq2026}), \textbf{Rationalize} (550, then 500).

\caption{Big-5 capex by company, 2022--2025 (Epoch AI, SEC XBRL~\cite{ref:epoch_capex}) and 2026 guidance~\cite{ref:mlq2026,ref:statista2026}. The dashed line marks the 2022 level; the model's non-AI baseline is pinned from 2023 at about \$130B (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}).}\label{fig:capex}\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{f2_carry.pdf}}

In 2028 under Plateau the median carry decomposes as depreciation $\sim$\$325B, facility opex $\sim$\$150B, return on net book and on deferred capital $\sim$\$170B. The carry ramps because 2027 is the cheapest year --- the 2026 cohort is half energized and the 2027 cohort barely --- and rationalization flattens rather than cuts because the 2025--26 cohorts are on the books whatever happens next.

\subsection{Silicon life: accounting versus economics}
\claim{The books say five to six years for a chip; the economics say about four, and the paper shows the result both ways.}

The operators' own accounting lives for servers are 5 years at Amazon (cut from 6 in January 2025, explicitly citing the pace of AI hardware change~\cite{ref:amzn_10k_life}), 5.5 at Meta~\cite{ref:hudson_meta}, and 6 at Microsoft and Alphabet~\cite{ref:hudson_msft,ref:hudson_policy}. We use an economic life of 3--5.5 years because power, not silicon health, is the binding constraint: where interconnection is queued~\cite{ref:pjm_queue,ref:eirgrid_cru,ref:amsterdam}, a 700\,W prior-generation part cannot hold a substation slot against a successor with several times the throughput per watt; secondary-market volume cannibalizes new cluster pricing; and 24/7 thermal duty is an accelerated-aging regime for stacked memory.

\lead{Shown both ways.} Against this, 2020-vintage A100s remained rented through 2026, and Alphabet and Microsoft have not shortened their lives. So we show the result both ways: with accounting lives (4.5--6.5 yr) the plateau break-even for 2030 moves from 57\% to 58\%, and with an 8-year life it is 57\% --- longer lives lower annual depreciation but keep every cohort on the books longer, and the two effects nearly cancel by 2030 (Table~\ref{tab:breakeven}).

The dual-use defense --- repurposing 40--100\,kW/rack liquid-cooled halls for databases and web hosting --- does not recover the carry; the capital is committed to dense tensor math.

\subsection{Thermal acceleration: high failure rates are the intrinsic norm}
\claim{AI chips run hot all the time, so they wear out faster than the servers the accounting lives were set on.}

The accounting lives of 5--6 years were set on the experience of CPU servers at ASHRAE-class inlet temperatures. AI accelerators are a different thermal regime: 700--1{,}000\,W parts at junction temperatures of 80--95$^{\circ}$C under sustained duty, with stacked HBM and through-silicon vias in the same envelope. Three standard reliability relations bear on their economic life:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Arrhenius acceleration.} For temperature-driven wear-out mechanisms the acceleration factor is $AF=\exp\!\left[\frac{E_a}{k}\left(\frac{1}{T_1}-\frac{1}{T_2}\right)\right]$; with the JEDEC default activation energy $E_a\approx0.7$\,eV~\cite{ref:jedec_jep122} this is close to the industry rule of thumb that failure rates double for every 10$^{\circ}$C~\cite{ref:arrhenius_rule}. A part held 20$^{\circ}$C hotter than the population its accounting life was calibrated on has roughly a quarter of that life.
    \item \textbf{Electromigration.} Black's equation, MTTF $\propto J^{-n}e^{E_a/kT}$~\cite{ref:black1969}, couples the same exponential to current density; accelerators run both high.
    \item \textbf{Thermal cycling.} HBM stacks and TSV interconnects fail by solder and micro-bump fatigue under thermal cycling (Coffin--Manson), which is driven by load swings rather than steady temperature; inference fleets with bursty duty see both mechanisms at once.
\end{itemize}
Field data agree. Meta's Llama~3 training run on 16{,}384 H100s recorded 419 unexpected interruptions in 54 days, 58\% attributed to GPU faults including HBM3 errors --- about one accelerator-related interruption every three hours at a scale that is now routine~\cite{ref:llama3}. High failure is not an anomaly of early hardware; it is the expected behavior of silicon run at the top of its thermal envelope.

\lead{Three consequences for the model.}
\begin{itemize}
\item First, the economic-life distribution Triang(3, 4, 5.5) is, if anything, generous; a thermally motivated Triang(2.5, 3.5, 5) raises the 2027--28 carry by \$10--15B/yr and lowers 2029--30 slightly as cohorts roll off faster (2030 break-even $g^{*}$ stays at about 57\%).
\item Second, and more important, shorter life converts a growing share of a flat \$780B/yr into \emph{replacement} rather than \emph{growth}: at a 3.5-year life the plateau path is buying back roughly a third of its own stock every year, so capacity --- and therefore revenue potential --- rises much more slowly than cumulative spend.
\item Third, the salvage and second-life defenses that follow are weaker than their proponents assume, because the parts being down-cycled are the ones that have already consumed most of their Arrhenius budget.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The salvage fallacy in detail}
\claim{Old chips cannot simply be moved to cheaper work: power, not silicon, is the scarce input, and a new chip earns more from the same power slot.}

Corporate disclosures have extended server useful lives to six years~\cite{ref:hudson_policy,ref:amzn_10k_life}, and a common defense holds that frontier accelerators can be down-cycled into batch inference for 6--8 years at meaningful residual value. Three constraints bound this:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Power is the binding input.} Where interconnection is queued (PJM, ERCOT, Dublin, Amsterdam)~\cite{ref:pjm_queue,ref:eirgrid_cru,ref:amsterdam}, a 700W prior-generation part cannot hold a substation allocation against a successor delivering several times the throughput per watt. Older parts are de-racked for the grid slot, not for the silicon.
    \item \textbf{Competing with one's own ghost.} Secondary-market volume of prior-generation parts cannibalizes new cluster pricing, as it did for mainframes in IBM's own history.
    \item \textbf{Thermal duty.} 24/7 operation at peak thermal load is a known accelerated-aging regime for stacked memory and TSV interconnect; a multi-year second life at full utilization is not the default assumption an engineer would make. We have not found a public, peer-reviewed failure-rate study specific to HBM3e/HBM4 under hyperscale duty and do not cite one\verify.
\end{itemize}
Against this, the empirical record is mixed: 2020-vintage A100s remained fully utilized in rental markets through 2026. We therefore use $L_{\text{si}}\sim$Triang(3, 4, 5.5) rather than the 2.5--3.5 years of harsher analyses, and note that even $L_{\text{si}}=8$ does not by itself close the gap by 2030 (Table~\ref{tab:breakeven}).

\subsection{The dual-use fallacy in detail}
\claim{An AI hall cannot be reused for ordinary computing at a price that recovers its cost.}

AI halls are engineered for 40--100 kW/rack with direct-to-chip liquid cooling and non-blocking fabrics, at several times the cost per megawatt of 8--12 kW enterprise halls. Repurposing them for relational databases and web hosting does not recover the carry; the capital is physically committed to dense tensor math.



\section{Demand: Base, Growth, Quality}\label{sec:demand}
\claim{Revenue is about \$179B in 2026, and it must be counted carefully: run-rates overstate it and some of it is investors' money.}

\inbrief{\item AI revenue in 2026 is about \$179B, counted from seven sourced parts.\item Only part of it reaches the data-center owners: about 82 cents of each customer dollar.\item Run-rates overstate what is booked, and some lab spending is investors' money, not customers'.}

\subsection{Anchor to what is observed}
\claim{Three flows are easy to confuse --- what customers pay, what reaches the cloud, and what leaks away --- so the paper names each.}

Terms, first, because three flows are easy to confuse. \emph{End-customer revenue} $D_t$ is what buyers --- enterprises, consumers, advertisers --- pay for AI products and services in year $t$; $R_s(t)$ is the same thing for one sector $s$, and $D_t=\sum_s R_s(t)$. \emph{Infrastructure receipts} are the part of that money that reaches the owners of the data centers, after the frontier labs keep their gross margin and after the part that runs on devices, on customers' own premises or on integrators' small models --- collectively, \emph{leakage} --- never reaches a cloud meter at all.

The \emph{cloud-capture share} $\theta_s$ is the fraction of a sector's end-customer revenue that becomes infrastructure receipts. Figure~\ref{fig:flow} draws the flows; the carry test compares infrastructure receipts, not end-customer revenue, with $H_t$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f18_flow.pdf}}
\caption{Where an AI customer's dollar goes (each arrow is labelled with the share of the dollar it carries). End-customer revenue enters through three channels; the frontier labs keep a margin and spend the rest on compute; part of every channel leaks to devices, premises and integrators; what remains is the infrastructure receipts that must cover the carry.}\label{fig:flow}\end{figure}

Calendar-2026 end-customer AI revenue across all channels is $D_{2026}\sim$Triang(161, 179, 198) \$B, the sum of seven sourced components, the hyperscaler component itself decomposed from Microsoft's disclosures (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}). Not all of it reaches the infrastructure layer: frontier labs keep a margin and spend the majority of their costs on compute~\cite{ref:epoch_spending}, so we apply an infrastructure share $\theta\sim$Triang(0.77, 0.815, 0.86), a channel-weighted decomposition with the lab channel itself decomposed by lab (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}). Demand then grows at a sustained rate $g$:
\begin{equation}
D_t=\theta\,D_{t_0}(1+g)^{t-t_0},\qquad t_0=2026,
\end{equation}
where $D_t$ here denotes the infrastructure receipts of the top-down path (end-customer revenue times $\theta$), $\theta$ is the revenue-weighted cloud-capture share, $D_{t_0}$ the 2026 base, and $g$ the sustained growth rate in percent per year (a $g$ of 0.57 means 57\%/yr). Does $g$ itself follow a curve? In the bottom-up estimate it does: the sectors' implied growth falls from $\sim$57\% in 2027 to $\sim$14\% in 2030 (Table~\ref{tab:bottomup}), the shape of a logistic.

The top-down sweep deliberately holds $g$ constant so that a single number answers ``how fast must it grow'' --- a sustained rate is easier to test against the labs' disclosures than a decaying one. This makes $g$ the single parameter the debate turns on, and it answers the J-curve objection directly: if productivity arrives late but arrives~\cite{ref:brynjolfsson2021}, then building early is right --- and the bull must name the $g$ at which it pays.

\subsection{Revenue quality}
\claim{Run-rates overstate what is booked, and part of the labs' spending is investor capital, not customer revenue.}

Two caveats on the base.
\begin{itemize}
\item First, run-rates overstate booked revenue: OpenAI's reported 2025 booked revenue was \$13.1B against an end-2025 run-rate of \$21B~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai,ref:epoch_revenue}; we use calendar-year figures.
\item Second, a material share of 2026 lab compute purchases is funded by the $\sim$\$190B of equity raised this year rather than by customer revenue~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai,ref:techcrunch_anthropic}.
\end{itemize}

OpenAI's 2025 operating loss is reported to have exceeded its revenue~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai}; the figure is leaked and unaudited, and the paper treats it with corresponding skepticism, using only its direction.

\lead{The cost of opacity.} That skepticism is itself a cost of the disclosure regime: a company at this scale, with these commitments, that publishes run-rates in the months of its choosing and lets its losses reach the market by leak, generates churn --- in its counterparties' recoverability forecasts, in its lenders' covenants, and in analyses like this one --- that a single audited statement would remove.

The same applies to every lab and to the hyperscalers' ``AI revenue'' lines: partial disclosure does not protect the discloser, it adds noise to everyone's estimate of it, including the register this paper maintains.

\lead{Investor money is not customer revenue.} Those receipts are real cash to hyperscalers and are counted in $\theta D$; but they are bridging, not durable, and the model's $g$ is a growth rate of customer revenue, not of investor capital. Related-party round trips --- Microsoft--OpenAI, Nvidia--CoreWeave, Amazon/Alphabet--Anthropic --- are of order \$10B/yr\verify and are small against this; the investor-funded channel is the circularity that matters in 2026.

\section{Bottom-Up Demand: Fifteen Sector Fermi Chains}\label{sec:bottomup}
\claim{A second, independent estimate: fifteen sectors, each growing toward its own ceiling. Together they grow about 33\% a year from their own 2026 base, not 57\%.}

\inbrief{\item A second estimate that does not assume a growth rate: fifteen sectors, each growing toward a ceiling set by seats, budgets or users.\item Together they reach about \$625B of customer revenue in 2030, growing about 33\% a year on average and slowing as they near their ceilings.\item Four sectors carry three-quarters of it: office assistants and agents, advertising, consumer, and coding tools.\item Startups failing, chips moving onto devices and routine work moving to small models all cap what the cloud collects.}

The top-down path in Section~\ref{sec:demand} has one free parameter, $g$. A Fermi estimate is only as good as the number of independent factors in it, so we build a second estimate that does not use $g$ at all. Each sector $s$ is a chain of four estimates: a calendar-2026 base $R_s$ (the sector bases sum to about \$205B, above the \$179B channel count of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} because sectors count end-customer spend that the channel count nets out; if anything this is generous to the build-out, and the base is not independent evidence --- the ceilings and ramps are), a 2030 ceiling $C_s$ derived from a physical or budgetary constraint (Appendix~\ref{app:sectors}), a logistic ramp rate $k_s$, and a cloud-capture share $\theta_s$ (the fraction of end-customer revenue that reaches the infrastructure layer after lab margins, on-device execution, on-premise deployment and integrator capture). End-customer revenue follows
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}R_s(t)=\frac{C_s}{1+\left(C_s/R_{s,0}-1\right)e^{-k_s(t-t_0)}},\qquad t_0=2026,\\ D^{\text{BU}}_t=\sum_s R_s(t),\qquad \text{Receipts}_t=\sum_s\theta_s R_s(t),\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
where $R_{s,0}$ is the sector's 2026 revenue (\$B), $C_s$ its ceiling (\$B), and $k_s$ its ramp rate in units of per year: at $k=0.8$ the sector covers half the distance to its ceiling in about $\ln 2/0.8\approx0.9$ years. The curve starts at $R_{s,0}$, rises fastest early, and flattens as it approaches $C_s$; the sector's growth rate is not an input but falls out of the curve.
Two common factors --- $M_C$ on ceilings ($\sim$Triang(0.7, 1.0, 1.4)) and $M_k$ on ramp rates ($\sim$Triang(0.7, 1.0, 1.3)) --- are multipliers drawn once per trial and applied to every sector. They represent the macroeconomic background the sectors share --- a recession, an interest-rate shock, a regulatory turn, or a capability step that makes agents trustworthy everywhere at once --- and they keep fifteen independent draws from averaging to an implausibly narrow band.

Sectors are listed in order of their 2030 ceiling. Sectors exposed to sovereign procurement carry the sovereignty carve-out $\gamma_{\text{sov}}$ (the share of demand kept for domestic providers, Section~8) in the sovereignty scenario, and in every scenario a European displacement ramp $g_{\text{EU}}\sim$Triang(3, 5, 8)\% by 2030 for the movement of European buyers away from US vendors documented in Section~\ref{sec:decomp}.

\lead{Growth as a consequence, not an input.} Growth is high while the large sectors are far from their ceilings and falls as they approach them. Three readings.
\begin{itemize}
\item First, the sectors are consistent with the observed 2026--27 pace: 57\% growth into 2027 is in line with the labs' guided deceleration~\cite{ref:epoch_revenue_x}, so the bottom-up model is not a bear case in the near term.
\item Second, growth decays fast because the four large sectors approach ceilings set by seat counts, IT budgets and advertising yields, not by model capability.
\item Third, the cloud-capture share matters as much as the ceilings: a quarter of end-customer revenue never reaches the infrastructure layer --- it stays with labs as margin, runs on-device, or accrues to integrators.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Structural limits on the ceilings}
\claim{Startups fail, chips move onto phones and laptops, and routine work moves to small models: each caps what the cloud collects.}


\lead{Startup mortality.} Of the 2024--25 funded cohort, roughly 40\% had failed within 24 months and the trajectory points to $\sim$80\% by end-2026\verify~\cite{ref:cbinsights}. When labs ship native features, single-feature wrappers collapse; their API spend is the low tail of the enterprise pool.

\lead{The local-silicon siphon.} About 1.2B smartphones and 250M PCs ship annually with 40--80 TOPS NPUs. If 350M knowledge workers issue $\sim$15 routine text tasks per day at $\sim$500 tokens each, routine volume is
\begin{equation*}
350\text{M}\times 15 \times 250 \times 500 \approx 6.6\times 10^{14}\ \text{tokens/yr},
\end{equation*}
worth of order \$70--330B/yr at routine-model prices of \$0.10--0.50 per million tokens. If 60--85\% of that runs on-device or on private small models, the cloud loses \$40--280B/yr of potential base-load. On-device inference is not free (device cost, power, governance), but its marginal price to the cloud is zero.

\lead{Frontier versus routine.} Multi-step reasoning and complex synthesis are under $\sim$5\% of enterprise token volume; the 90\%+ that is summarization, drafting and formatting is exactly the share that distilled open-weight models and NPUs absorb.


\section{Decomposing the Dominant Uncertainties}\label{sec:decomp}
\claim{Nineteen loose inputs are broken into sourced parts, which shrinks the uncertainty and moves the answer against the build-out.}

\inbrief{\item Breaking nineteen loose inputs into sourced parts cuts the spread of the result by more than half, and moves the answer against the build-out rather than for it.\item What remains uncertain is mainly three things: the labs' compute-cost share, how fast firms adopt AI, and how big the assistant market gets.\item Budgets bound the answer, and those budgets are themselves being reshaped by AI.}

Left as single judgment ranges, three inputs would carry most of the model's uncertainty: the 2026 revenue base (64\% of top-down variance at a Triang(160, 210, 300) range), the cloud-capture share $\theta$ (18\%), and the advertising ceiling (51\% of bottom-up sector variance).

An estimate improves in exactly one way --- by decomposing its loosest factor into tighter, separately sourced ones --- so this section does that for those three and for a further sixteen, and then draws what-if curves over what is left.

\lead{Every decomposition, both directions.} Each decomposition is reported with the undecomposed range it replaces, and with the direction it moved the central estimate, because a decomposition that only ever tightens in one direction would be a sign of motivated choices. Appendix~\ref{app:inputs} appraises every input in turn --- what is known, the range chosen, the likely direction of bias in its source, and what would improve it --- so that any reader can substitute a better-informed range.

\subsection{The 2026 revenue base}
\claim{Built from seven sourced components, the 2026 base is about \$179B, well below the \$210B a single guess would give.}

Other labs (xAI's Grok, Mistral, Cohere, Perplexity, non-US) & 4 / 7 / 12 & Sparse disclosure\verify \\
Hyperscaler AI services excluding lab hosting and reseller pass-through & 25 / 35 / 45 & Microsoft AI run-rate $>$\$37B less Azure-OpenAI hosting; AWS AI $>$\$15B less Bedrock pass-through; Google Cloud AI less Vertex pass-through~\cite{ref:yahoo2026,ref:traderinsights2026}\verify \\
AI-attributable advertising uplift (Meta + Google) & 25 / 40 / 55 & Meta: 8--12\% relevance/pricing lift disclosed~\cite{ref:meta_q2_2026} on $\sim$\$230B ads $=$ \$18--28B; Google assumed comparable magnitude with less disclosure, \$10--25B \\
Consumer, search and internal use not already in lab revenue & 8 / 15 / 25 & Gemini, Microsoft Copilot consumer, AI search monetization, internal productivity\verify \\
Overlap correction & $-15$ / $-8$ / 0 & Anthropic books Bedrock and Vertex end-customer spend as gross revenue~\cite{ref:sacra_anthropic}, so a portion of ``AWS AI'' and ``Google Cloud AI'' is the same dollar counted twice \\
\midrule
\textbf{Sum} & \textbf{179 (P10 168, P90 191)} & $\sigma_{\ln}=0.051$, versus 0.13 for the undecomposed Triang(160, 210, 300) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}
Two things happen relative to a single judgment range of Triang(160, 210, 300). The uncertainty falls by a factor of 2.5, because the two largest components are public quarterly figures rather than a guess. And the central value falls from \$210B to \$179B, because run-rates overstate calendar bookings and because gross-basis reseller accounting double-counts. Decomposition does not only tighten an estimate; it can move it, and here it moves against the build-out.

\subsection{The cloud-capture share}
\claim{About 82 cents of each customer dollar reaches the infrastructure owners; the labs keep the most, and their margin is the number to watch.}
