The cloud-capture share, $\theta$ --- the fraction of end-customer revenue that reaches the infrastructure owners --- is a channel-weighted average of four shares that are each better known than the aggregate: the lab channel ($\sim$50\% of the base), where infrastructure receipts are the labs' compute cost as a fraction of revenue --- OpenAI at $\sim$39\% gross margin and $\sim$\$13B of 2026 Azure spend on $\sim$\$29B of bookings~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai}, Anthropic's margin undisclosed\verify --- giving $\theta_{\text{lab}}\sim$Triang(0.50, 0.65, 0.85); hyperscaler direct services ($\sim$20\%), $\theta\sim$Triang(0.9, 1.0, 1.0); advertising ($\sim$22\%), $\theta=1$; and consumer/other ($\sim$8\%), Triang(0.75, 0.85, 0.95).

The weighted result is $\theta\sim$0.82 (P10--P90 0.77--0.87), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.045$ versus 0.072 before. What remains uncertain is one number --- the labs' compute-cost share --- and Figure~\ref{fig:whatif3} sweeps it.

\subsection{The advertising ceiling}
\claim{AI lifts advertising by roughly 8--18\% of a \$700--950B pool --- large, but a lift, not a new market.}

The 2030 ceiling decomposes as (Meta ads 2030 $\sim$Triang(330, 380, 430)) $+$ (Google ads 2030 $\sim$Triang(380, 450, 520)), both from 2026 levels~\cite{ref:meta_q2_2026b} at 10--15\%/yr, times an AI-attributable lift $\sim$Triang(0.08, 0.12, 0.18) anchored on Meta's disclosed 8\% click and 15.7\% conversion lifts and 12\% price-per-ad growth~\cite{ref:meta_q2_2026}, plus AI-native formats $\sim$Triang(10, 25, 60).

Result: \$136B (P10--P90 \$111--164B), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.15$ versus 0.22 before. Meta's Advantage+ run-rate of \$75B~\cite{ref:meta_q2_2026} is not an uplift figure --- it is revenue flowing through an AI-managed product --- but it bounds the base on which any lift can act, and it is consistent with the range.

\subsection{The common adoption factor}
\claim{One shared factor --- how fast firms really adopt --- moves every sector together, and is why the errors do not cancel.}

The factor that correlates the sectors ($\sigma_{\ln}=0.14$) is not one thing. It splits into an IT-budget cycle component --- enterprise IT spend growth of 8--10\%/yr with a well-forecast variance, $\sigma\approx0.05$~\cite{ref:gartner_it}\verify; an inference price-deflation component --- revenue is volume times price, and price per token for fixed capability has fallen $\sim$10$\times$/yr~\cite{ref:epoch_spending} while volume has risen faster, net $\sigma\approx0.08$; and a residual --- whether agentic systems work well enough to be trusted with whole workflows, and how fast enterprises reorganize around them --- $\sigma\approx0.10$.

The first two are improvable with data and are largely observable by 2027. The third is the irreducible core of the whole debate, and it is the axis of Figure~\ref{fig:whatif1}.

\subsection{Facility opex}
\claim{Running costs are about 6.65\% of capital a year: power, staff, tax, insurance and network, each sourced.}

The supply side's loosest input after the base was facility opex, at Triang(0.05, 0.08, 0.11) of in-service gross. It decomposes into power --- PUE $\sim$Triang(1.12, 1.22, 1.35) $\times$ 8{,}760\,h $\times$ utilization Triang(0.65, 0.80, 0.92) $\times$ tariff Triang(\$0.075, 0.10, 0.13)/kWh~\cite{ref:eia_epm}, divided by all-in capex of Triang(38, 45, 55) \$M per MW of IT load --- giving $\sim$1.9\% of gross per year; staffing and maintenance Triang(1.2, 2.0, 3.0)\%; property tax and insurance Triang(0.7, 1.0, 1.4)\%; network and bandwidth Triang(0.5, 1.0, 1.6)\%; other Triang(0.3, 0.6, 1.0)\%.

Sum: 6.65\% (P10--P90 5.9--7.4\%), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.085$ versus 0.157. The central value falls as well as the spread --- a decomposition that moves in the build-out's favor, as roughly half of them should if the choices are not motivated.

\subsection{The assistant ceiling}
\claim{Paid AI office seats, not hype, cap the largest sector: about \$150B by 2030, with the widest range of any input.}

\lead{What the ceiling means.} The most that enterprises could spend in 2030 on AI assistants and the agentic workloads built on them, given seats, prices and IT budgets.

\lead{The seat count is growing fast.} Microsoft disclosed 15M paid M365 Copilot seats in January 2026, 20M in April and more than 30M by July, with the largest single deal at 740{,}000 seats~\cite{ref:msft_copilot_seats,ref:q2_scorecard}.

\lead{But a paid seat is not a used seat.} Three qualifications keep the number honest:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Bundling.} The 10M seats added in one quarter coincided with the May launch of the \$99 E7 suite that bundles Copilot into E5, the July base-price increases, and usage-based billing alongside per-seat licences~\cite{ref:ai2_copilot,ref:windowsforum_copilot}.
\item \textbf{Use.} Microsoft reports weekly engagement matching Outlook and Teams and satisfaction doubling over three quarters; independent estimates put weekly active use at 20--30\% of assigned seats in some large deployments~\cite{ref:ai2_copilot}.
\item \textbf{Price, not seats.} The commercial seat base grew 6\% while Microsoft 365 commercial cloud revenue grew 17\%: the growth is price and mix~\cite{ref:msft_8k_fy27,ref:fool_copilot}.
\end{itemize}
So the model treats paid seats as the upper bound on adoption, and carries the \emph{active share} --- Triang(0.25, 0.45, 0.70) of paid seats --- as a secondary variable. A usage disclosure would settle it.

\lead{The ceiling, step by step.} Five factors, each bounded:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Seats today:} paid seats across all vendors, mid-2026, Triang(35, 42, 50)M.
\item \textbf{Seat growth:} Triang(1.3, 1.6, 2.0)$\times$ a year in 2027--28, slowing after, and capped at 250M --- about 55\% of the Microsoft 365 and Google Workspace commercial base.
\item \textbf{Price:} Triang(\$25, 30, 36) per seat per month.
\item \textbf{Agents on top:} agentic and workflow consumption at Triang(1.2, 2.0, 3.0)$\times$ the seat revenue.
\item \textbf{Budget cap:} the whole limited to an AI share of Triang(3, 4, 5.5)\% of $\sim$\$6.5T of 2030 IT spending, about \$270B.
\end{enumerate}

\lead{The result: about \$150B, with a wide range.} The median is \$150B (P10--P90 \$100--229B).

\lead{Why the range stays wide.} This decomposition does not tighten the estimate --- its spread ($\sigma_{\ln}$) is 0.31, against 0.20 for a single judgment range --- because seat growth is genuinely uncertain, and a judgment range would understate that. It is the right outcome: the assistant ceiling is the loose axis, and it now has a floor anchored in data and a hard cap anchored in budgets.

\subsection{Silicon life: the cutoffs}
\claim{Physics sets the floor and accounting the ceiling: a working life between about 2.5 and 6.5 years.}

Silicon life does not decompose further, but it is bounded on both sides by evidence: below by the thermal-acceleration physics of Section~\ref{sec:supply} (an economic life under 2.5 years would imply failure rates no operator has reported), above by the operators' own accounting lives of 5--6 years (an economic life beyond the accounting life would mean the companies are over-depreciating, which none has claimed). The training/inference fleet mix (Section~\ref{sec:supply}) gives an effective 4.2 years, inside those cutoffs.

\subsection{The feasibility cutoff: a budget that is itself being torn up}
\claim{Enterprise software and services budgets bound what AI can earn --- and those budgets are themselves shrinking under AI.}

\inbrief{\item AI revenue has to come out of budgets; the enterprise software and IT services budgets set the outer limit.\item Those budgets are being reshaped by AI itself: seat-priced software is deflating, agents replace seats, and buyers outside the US are leaving US vendors.\item The money is not lost but moved --- to outcome-priced AI solutions, agents and integrators; the model moves 12--32\% of the pool by 2030.\item Incumbents can raise prices to hold revenue, but only converting seats to paid AI use offsets seat loss.\item Result: a feasible band for 2030 AI revenue of about \$920B--\$1.31T, against which the paper's probability map is drawn.}

A first-pass cutoff --- 8\% of total IT spend plus the advertising and consumer caps, $\sim$\$810B, drawn as a line --- would rest on an assumed share. A second pass would take Gartner's 2026 enterprise software (\$1.44T) and IT services (\$1.87T)~\cite{ref:gartner_it_jul2026}, grow them at 7--13\%/yr, and apply an AI share; that treats the pool as stable and Gartner as reliable, and neither holds.

\subsubsection*{Why a forecast budget is not enough}
Gartner revised its 2026 IT-growth forecast from 10.8\% in February to 14.2\% in July~\cite{ref:gartner_it_feb2026,ref:gartner_it_jul2026} --- a 3.4-point swing in five months on the same year --- which is the precision such forecasts deserve, and the pool itself is being reorganized by the very technology the paper is pricing. Three things are happening to it at once.

\subsubsection*{Three forces reshaping the budget}
\lead{Seat-priced software is deflating.} Between January and April 2026 the enterprise software sector lost roughly \$2T of market value --- \$285B in one 48-hour stretch after Anthropic's Claude Cowork launch --- in what JPMorgan called the largest non-recessionary software drawdown in over thirty years~\cite{ref:saaspocalypse_ti,ref:saaspocalypse_nx}.

Atlassian reported its first-ever decline in enterprise seat counts; Salesforce fell $\sim$30\% and Adobe $\sim$36\% year-to-date~\cite{ref:saaspocalypse_fc}. The market's reading is the paper's: if an agent does the work of ten people, the customer buys one seat, not ten.

\lead{Agents replace seats.} Gartner itself projects 40\% of enterprise applications carrying AI agents by end-2026, from under 5\% in 2025~\cite{ref:saaspocalypse_arbi}. Per-seat application software is $\sim$55\% of the software pool; the model gives it 2026--30 growth of Triang($-6$, 0, $+5$)\%/yr, against Triang(4, 8, 12)\% for infrastructure software, databases and security.

Customers paying Microsoft for extended security updates to \emph{avoid} Windows 11 are the small-scale version of the same fact: incumbents are collecting rent on products their customers would rather not use, and rent is the first thing a budget cut removes.

\lead{Buyers outside the US are displacing US vendors.} In April 2026 France's DINUM announced a national migration of government workstations from Windows to Linux; Schleswig-Holstein is mid-way through a full exit from Microsoft Office, Exchange and Windows; Denmark is following; France's national health fund is moving 80{,}000 staff to sovereign tools~\cite{ref:eu_sovereignty}.

The trigger was not price but law: Microsoft's disabling of the International Criminal Court prosecutor's account on a US order made the CLOUD Act's reach concrete, and a 2026 survey of 1{,}818 IT decision-makers in Germany, France and the Nordics found the share actively implementing European software doubled from 20\% to 40\% in a year, with 87.5\% of German respondents favoring European products in critical infrastructure~\cite{ref:tuta_sovereignty}.

\lead{Sovereignty: Europe and China.} Europe is $\sim$23\% of the global pool; the model moves Triang(10, 20, 35)\% of it away from US vendors by 2030. China is $\sim$10\% and is already 70--95\% domestic. The IT services pool, the largest single component, grows at Triang($-2$, 3, 7)\%/yr as AI removes consulting hours faster than implementation work adds them.

\subsubsection*{Where the money goes}
\lead{The money moves from seats to solutions.} What the seat and services deflation does not do is disappear. The dollars are reallocated: to outcome-priced AI solutions, to agents that replace the seats, to the labs and integrators that sell them. The model puts the reallocated share of the accessible pool at Triang(12, 20, 32)\% by 2030, plus Triang(2, 4, 7)\% of genuinely new AI spend.

\subsubsection*{The incumbent's dilemma}
\inbrief{\item A software vendor that charges per seat earns seats $\times$ price.
\item AI agents and buyers leaving for European or domestic tools cut the seats, so the vendor raises the price.
\item But every price rise pushes some customers to leave: at typical rates, an 8\% a year price rise only holds revenue flat.
\item Only one thing really offsets lost seats: getting customers to pay for AI on top. That is the same adoption question the whole paper turns on.}

\lead{The move: raise the price, bundle the AI.} On July 1, 2026 Microsoft raised Microsoft 365 commercial prices worldwide --- up to 43\% on some plans, E3 to about \$39 per user per month, Windows Enterprise up --- and bundled Copilot Chat and Security Copilot into the suites as ``value expansion''~\cite{ref:m365_price_wl,ref:m365_price_uscloud,ref:m365_price_ow}. Standalone Copilot seats, which customers had shown little appetite for, were folded into the base. Microsoft is the example because it discloses the most; Salesforce, Adobe, ServiceNow and SAP face the same arithmetic.

\lead{Why it only holds ground.} Revenue is seats times price. If prices rise by $p$ a year and each step of price rise loses a share $\epsilon$ of seats (the seat-loss elasticity), revenue after four years is $(1+p)^4\,[1-\epsilon((1+p)^4-1)]$ times today's, before any other seat loss. With $\epsilon=0.35$ and seats already falling 3\% a year, an 8\% annual price rise keeps revenue roughly flat, and no more (Figure~\ref{fig:incumbent}, left).

\lead{Microsoft 365, worked through.} The inputs are widely quoted but unverified\verify:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Today:} about 450M paid seats at about \$21 a month --- some \$113B in 2026.
\item \textbf{Seats:} Microsoft's own figures show seats still growing 6--7\% a year~\cite{ref:msft_8k_fy27}; the model slows that to between $-4$\% and $+4$\% a year to 2030 as agents and sovereignty exits bite, with 3--15\% of seats lost to sovereignty by 2030.
\item \textbf{Price and response:} prices up 5--12\% a year; seat-loss elasticity 0.15--0.60.
\item \textbf{Result:} base-suite revenue of about \$110--140B in 2030 on 350--420M seats --- near or a little above 2026, held up mainly by today's seat growth.
\end{itemize}

\lead{The number to beat.} The observed 6--7\% seat growth is what the paper's seat-deflation argument has to overcome, and it has not yet. What would change the picture is paid AI on top: if 12--35\% of remaining seats pay for Copilot by 2030 (from about 6.5\% today, 30M of some 460M), total Microsoft 365 revenue is roughly \$130--170B --- above 2026, but short of an 8\% a year trend in most draws (Figure~\ref{fig:incumbent}, right).

\lead{The lesson for every seat-priced vendor.} Raising the bill for AI features does not offset lost seats; only converting seats into paid AI use does. That conversion is the same adoption question this paper cannot settle from 2026 data, which is why the argument lives in the assistant ceiling and the adoption factor, not in the size of the budget.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f21_incumbent.pdf}}
\caption{Left: seat revenue in 2030 relative to 2026 for a seat-priced vendor, by seat decline and price growth, at a seat-loss elasticity of 0.35. Right: Microsoft 365 commercial as an illustration on unverified inputs --- the base suite is more likely than not below 2026 revenue by 2030; the total holds up only if Copilot attach rises fivefold.}\label{fig:incumbent}\end{figure}

\subsubsection*{The resulting band}
\lead{Result.} The 2030 pool is \$3.75T (P10--P90 \$3.5--4.0T), not the \$4.85T that 7--13\% growth would give; the part accessible to US vendors is \$3.25T; the AI share of it is $\sim$25\% (20--32\%); the enterprise AI cap is \$825B (\$650B--\$1.03T); with advertising (\$140--200B) and consumer (\$80--130B) caps the total feasibility band is \$1.10T (P10--P90 \$920B--\$1.31T).

That is almost exactly the band a stable, growing pool with a 15\% AI share would give --- the two errors cancel --- but the composition is entirely different, and the composition is the point.

\lead{The total holds; who receives the dollar changes.} Seat-priced incumbents lose it; European and Chinese providers keep more of it at home (the displacement enters the demand chains as a ramp on the sovereign-exposed sectors, Section~\ref{sec:bottomup}); and the largest single loser is the company that is both the largest seat-priced vendor and the most exposed to the sovereignty exit --- Microsoft, whose AI receipts grow while its conventional Office, Windows and Azure base is being cut from both sides. The band is drawn as a band, not a line, in Figure~\ref{fig:probmap}.

\subsubsection*{What is still loose}
\lead{Two secondary variables.} The reallocation share (12--32\% of the accessible pool moving to AI solutions) and the seat-priced SaaS growth rate ($-6$ to $+5$\%/yr) are the loosest terms in the band and neither can be pinned from 2026 data: the first will be visible in Gartner's and the vendors' 2027 category reporting, the second in seat-count disclosures over the next four quarters.

\lead{Held at the centre, swept separately.} Rather than let them hide inside the band they are treated as \emph{secondary variables} --- held at their central ranges in the base case and swept explicitly in Figure~\ref{fig:secondary}. The reading, with every builder counted: the plateau's 50\% contour ($\sim$\$1.18T) lies above the cutoff at the central secondary values ($\sim$\$1.10T). It is financeable only if the reallocation share is above about 24\%, or if seat software grows while European displacement stays low; the composition changes far more than the total.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f22_secondary.pdf}}
\caption{The two secondary variables in the feasibility band. Left: the cutoff versus the share of the accessible pool reallocated to AI, for three seat-software growth rates. Right: the cutoff versus seat-software growth, for three levels of European displacement of US vendors. The dash-dot line is the plateau's 50\%-probability contour.}\label{fig:secondary}\end{figure}

\subsection{The non-AI capex baseline}
\claim{About \$130B of the 2023 spending was not AI, and the model subtracts it, growing it slowly.}

The \$162B the five companies spent in 2022~\cite{ref:epoch_capex} decomposes by company --- Amazon fulfillment and transport $\sim$\$36B, AWS non-AI $\sim$\$28B, Microsoft $\sim$\$24B, Alphabet $\sim$\$31B, Meta $\sim$\$32B, Oracle $\sim$\$4B\verify. Growing that figure forward at an assumed 0--8\%/yr would give $\sim$\$190B for 2026. The filings constrain it lower (below); the by-company split is what the constraint is applied to.

\subsection{Energization lag, by component}
\claim{Chips are installed within months; buildings and power take one to two years, so cost arrives in two waves.}

A single lag Triang(0.3, 0.5, 0.7) hid two different physical processes. Accelerators ship and rack within one to three quarters of purchase ($\ell_{\text{si}}\sim$Triang(0.5, 0.7, 0.9) in the spend year); shell, power and cooling take twelve to twenty-four months ($\ell_{\text{sh}}\sim$Triang(0.2, 0.4, 0.6)). The effective lag is 0.56 with $\sigma_{\ln}=0.11$ versus 0.17, and its variance share in Table~\ref{tab:varbudget} fell from 2\% to under 1\%.

\subsection{Silicon life, by fleet}
\claim{Training chips are retired after three to four years; inference chips last longer.}

A single economic life hid the training/inference split. Training clusters are retired when a successor's throughput per watt makes the power slot worth more than the silicon (Section~\ref{sec:supply}): Triang(2.5, 3.5, 4.5) years. Inference fleets serve fixed models and run longer: Triang(4, 5, 6). At a training share of Triang(0.35, 0.45, 0.55) the fleet's effective life (harmonic mean, since depreciation is $1/L$) is 4.2 years (P10--P90 3.75--4.6), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.077$ versus 0.124 for the undecomposed Triang(3, 4, 5.5).

\subsection{The consumer ceiling}
\claim{Hundreds of millions use AI apps, but few pay: consumer revenue is about \$60B by 2030.}

Gemini's app reached 950M monthly users and Meta AI 1.2B by mid-2026~\cite{ref:consumer_mau}; with ChatGPT and others, unique consumer users are Triang(1.3, 1.6, 2.0)B. Paid conversion Triang(4, 6, 8)\% at Triang(\$20, 23, 26)/month gives \$27B of subscriptions; free-tier advertising on Triang(0.8, 1.0, 1.3)B ad-eligible users at Triang(\$15, 25, 40)/user-year gives \$27B; premium tiers Triang(5, 10, 15). Ceiling \$64B (P10--P90 \$55--75B), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.12$ versus 0.18, and lower than the earlier \$80B mode.

\subsection{Six further decompositions, and a grid variable}
\claim{Six more inputs are broken down, from the labs' compute share to the cost of capital, and a cap on how much new power the grid can connect.}

\textbf{Hyperscaler AI services excluding labs.} Microsoft's $>$\$37B AI run-rate~\cite{ref:yahoo2026} decomposes into Azure hosting of OpenAI ($\sim$\$13B, already in the OpenAI channel~\cite{ref:valueadd_openai}), M365 Copilot seats (20M $\times$ \$25--36 $\times$ 12 $\approx$ \$7B~\cite{ref:msft_copilot_seats}), GitHub Copilot ($\sim$\$2B), and other Azure AI services (remainder). AWS AI ($>$\$15B) less Bedrock pass-through, and Google Cloud AI less Vertex pass-through, add \$8--12B each. Calendar-2026 hyperscaler ex-lab revenue: \$39B (P10--P90 \$35--42B), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.067$ versus 0.118. The 2026 base becomes Triang(161, 179, 198) after the v28 lab revisions.

\lead{The lab channel's infrastructure share, by lab.} OpenAI's compute purchases --- $\sim$\$13B on Azure plus \$6--12B with Oracle and CoreWeave --- against $\sim$\$29B of bookings give 0.7--1.0; Anthropic's margin is undisclosed but bracketed by reported 2024--25 figures\verify at a compute share of Triang(0.45, 0.55, 0.70); other labs Triang(0.6, 0.7, 0.85). Revenue-weighted: $\theta_{\text{lab}}=0.635$ (P10--P90 0.59--0.69), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.058$ versus 0.108. Overall $\theta$ becomes Triang(0.77, 0.815, 0.86).

\lead{The non-AI baseline, constrained by 2023.} Total capex was flat at \$161B in 2023 while AI purchases of \$20--40B began~\cite{ref:epoch_capex}, so the non-AI baseline must have \emph{fallen} to $\sim$\$130B in 2023 (Amazon cut fulfillment capex that year). Growing from there at Triang(2, 6, 10)\%/yr gives \$156B for 2026 (P10--P90 \$145--168B) and $\sim$\$197B by 2030 --- lower than the $\sim$\$190B an assumed growth from 2022 would give. The AI-attributable stock is correspondingly larger. A constraint from a filing beats a growth assumption, whichever way it cuts.

\lead{Silicon share, from Nvidia.} Nvidia's \$300B data-center run-rate~\cite{ref:nvda_q1fy27} at a Big-5 share of Triang(0.45, 0.55, 0.65) is \$165B; server integration, custom accelerators (TPU, Trainium, MTIA), memory, networking and storage add roughly as much again. Against AI-attributable capex of $\sim$\$620B --- the share must be taken against AI capex, not the \$780B total, or it comes out implausibly low --- the silicon share is Triang(0.45, 0.52, 0.60).

\lead{Cost of capital, by CAPM.} Risk-free Triang(4.0, 4.3, 4.6)\%, equity premium Triang(4.5, 5.0, 5.5)\%, levered beta Triang(1.1, 1.2, 1.35), pre-tax debt cost Triang(5.5, 6.5, 7.5)\%, equity weight Triang(0.75, 0.82, 0.90), tax 21\%~\cite{ref:damodaran2026}: $r=9.45\%$ (P10--P90 9.0--9.9\%), $\sigma_{\ln}=0.038$ versus 0.058. The central cost of capital fell about a point, from 10.5\% to 9.45\%, and the 2030 carry fell $\sim$\$10B.

\lead{Grid energization capacity.} Capital can only earn once it is \emph{energized}, and the grid limits how much can be connected in a year. Global AI data-center interconnection capacity of Triang(22, 30, 40) GW/yr of IT load~\cite{ref:pjm_queue,ref:eirgrid_cru}\verify at Triang(\$38, 45, 55)M per MW is \$1.15--1.68T/yr of energizable capex.

Capex above it is deferred a year and carries return but no depreciation or opex. It does not bind on a plateau; it binds in $\sim$5\% of arms-race draws, where it \emph{lowers} the near-term carry by delaying depreciation while the capital sits idle. It is in the model because a physical ceiling should be, and because it becomes decisive if capex exceeds $\sim$\$1.3T/yr.

\subsection{Readiness: consent, water, and siting}
\claim{Local opposition, water and power now delay and cancel projects: about \$130B blocked or delayed in the first quarter of 2026 alone.}

\inbrief{\item Water and electricity bills are the stated grounds; the record on bills so far cuts the other way.\item The model defers 10--35\% of each year's build by a year and adds 2--9\% to building cost.\item The effect is to shift the cost later, not to remove it.}

A funded, grid-connected project must also be \emph{allowed} to be built, and the record says that constraint now binds. Data Center Watch counts \$156B of US data-center projects blocked or delayed by local opposition in 2025 and \$130B in the first quarter of 2026 alone --- 75 projects, roughly the whole of 2025 in three months~\cite{ref:dcw_q1_2026,ref:tomshw_dcw}.

Active opposition groups more than doubled to 833 across 49 states; more than 300 state bills were filed in six weeks; statewide moratoria were proposed in 14 states and New York enacted a halt on large new facilities in July 2026~\cite{ref:dcw_q1_2026,ref:kucoin_dcw}.

\lead{Cancellations and polling.} Cancellations rose from 6 in 2024 to 25 in 2025 and more than 20 in early 2026, including a \$14B Arizona site withdrawn and a \$24.7B Virginia site in litigation~\cite{ref:kucoin_dcw}. Ipsos polling moved from about half of Americans opposing a data center near them in late 2025 to roughly 70\% by mid-2026~\cite{ref:tomshw_dcw,ref:forbes_consent}.

The stated grounds are the ones this paper's supply section already prices in other forms: electricity and ratepayer bills, water, noise, diesel backup, and opaque permitting.

\lead{Electricity bills, so far.} On bills specifically the record cuts the other way so far: Watten, Bistline and Blanford find that data-center load \emph{lowered} average residential rates by about 6\% over 2019--24 by spreading the grid's fixed costs~\cite{ref:watten2026}; the opposition is nonetheless real and growing, and the deferral variable is calibrated on what communities do, not on what rates did.

Europe reached this point first: Dublin~\cite{ref:eirgrid_cru} and Amsterdam~\cite{ref:amsterdam} imposed connection and zoning limits in 2021--22, Frankfurt and Paris face grid and land constraints, and the Netherlands has restricted hyperscale sites nationally.

\lead{China is the exception.} Siting is a state decision, and the \emph{East Data, West Computing} program directs capacity to the western provinces where power and land are cheap and local objection is not a factor. The deferral variable below is calibrated on US data and applied to the whole Big-5 stock, which is conservative for the Chinese portion (small for these five companies) and roughly right for Europe.

\lead{Water, as a Fermi chain.} A 2030 AI fleet of Triang(60, 80, 110) GW at Triang(0.65, 0.80, 0.92) utilization draws $\sim$570 TWh/yr; at a water-usage effectiveness of Triang(0.3, 1.0, 1.8) L/kWh and an evaporative share of Triang(0.4, 0.6, 0.8), direct consumptive water is $\sim$90 billion gallons/yr (P10--P90 53--141), before the 3--4$\times$ larger indirect draw of the power generation behind it.

Why is the water not simply recycled? Evaporative cooling works \emph{by} evaporating it: the heat leaves as vapor, and that water is consumed, not returned.

\lead{Water and heat.} Closed-loop and dry cooling return the water but reject the heat to air instead, which takes 5--10\% more power. For scale: US freshwater withdrawals are about 320 billion gallons per \emph{day}, so 90 billion gallons per \emph{year} is under 0.1\% of the national total, and far less than agriculture or thermoelectric power plants.

The issue is local: a 500\,MW campus draws like a town of 50{,}000 people, from one aquifer, in the dry regions where land and power are cheapest.

\lead{Where water decides.} Arizona, Aragon and the Dutch Randstad refuse evaporative cooling for this reason. Operators are not exaggerating their efficiency work --- WUE has fallen steadily --- but the concentration, not the total, is what communities object to.

\lead{Three variables.} (i) A siting and consent deferral $f_{\text{delay}}\sim$Triang(0.10, 0.20, 0.35): the fraction of each cohort from 2026 pushed a year by permitting, litigation or relocation, treated like the grid deferral --- it carries return but not depreciation. The mode is set from the ratio of disrupted project value to annual pipeline (\$130B/quarter against a US pipeline of order \$1T), discounted because most delayed projects eventually proceed elsewhere.

(ii) A shell cost uplift $\kappa_{\text{site}}\sim$Triang(1.02, 1.05, 1.09) for community-benefit agreements, water mitigation, dry cooling, private generation and relocation to friendlier jurisdictions~\cite{ref:forbes_consent}. (iii) A cooling opex uplift $u\sim$Triang(0, 0.03, 0.06) where evaporative cooling is refused.

\lead{Effect.} The three variables shift the carry rather than remove it: 2027 falls by $\sim$\$25B (deferred cohorts), 2030 rises by $\sim$\$17B (deferred cohorts arriving with higher shell cost and opex). Break-even $g^{*}$ moves by about one point (56\% to 57\%); the bottom-up 2030 deficit is $\sim$\$15B larger and $z$ about 0.4 more negative than without the friction; rationalization's 2030 P(profit) is roughly six points lower (13\% to 7\%; \texttt{model/variants\_check.py}), because the cut path's late cohorts are the ones deferred into 2029--30. Readiness friction is, on net, a bear variable for the carry test and a bear variable for the revenue test, since the capacity it delays cannot serve demand in the year it was meant to.

\subsection{What remains, and the what-if curves}
\claim{After all the decompositions, the doubt sits in three places: how fast firms adopt, how big the assistant market gets, and the labs' compute-cost share.}

After nineteen decompositions the top-down root-sum-square falls from 286 to 104 (Table~\ref{tab:varbudget}), and the residual uncertainty sits in three places: the residual adoption/capability factor, the assistant ceiling, and the labs' compute-cost share. Each has a cutoff of its own --- the IT-budget share for the first two, the labs' reported gross margins for the third --- and Section~\ref{sec:overlap} maps the probability of profit over the two that matter most.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f13_whatif_mcap.pdf}}
\caption{What-if 1. The 2030 $z$-score and P(deficit) as the residual adoption/capability factor scales bottom-up receipts from 0.6 to 1.6, by capex path. The plateau path crosses $z=0$ only above $\sim$1.5 --- receipts 50\% above the sector chains' central estimate.}\label{fig:whatif1}\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{f14_whatif_ceilings.pdf}}
\caption{What-if 2. Median 2030 deficit (Plateau) across the two ceilings that could close the gap. The dot is the paper's modes. No combination in the plotted range reaches break-even; even the largest ceilings in the plotted range leave a median deficit near $-\$60$B, because by 2030 the logistic ramps have reached only 60--80\% of ceiling and assistant receipts are $\sim$60\% of assistant revenue.}\label{fig:whatif2}\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f15_whatif_theta_base.pdf}}
\caption{What-if 3. Left: 2030 deficit versus the labs' compute-cost share (the only loose part of $\theta$). Right: top-down 2030 deficit at $g=42\%$ versus the 2026 base across and beyond its decomposed range.}\label{fig:whatif3}\end{figure}

\lead{How robust is the sign?} The sign survives each residual variable moved alone across its plausible range. What it does not survive is the residual factor above $\sim$1.5 on a plateau --- the sector chains being wrong by half in the same direction at once.

No pair of ceilings does it alone (Figure~\ref{fig:whatif2}); it takes ceilings, ramp rates and cloud-capture shares moving together, which is a single statement about agentic adoption in the enterprise. That is where this paper's argument and the bull case actually disagree.

\section{Digital Sovereignty and Grid Friction}
\claim{Countries keeping data at home duplicate capital and carve demand out of the US clouds; grid queues strand chips before they earn.}

\inbrief{\item Rules that keep data at home force duplicate data centers that run below capacity.\item Countries building their own AI carve demand out of the US clouds: 15--35\% in the sovereignty scenario.\item Grid connection queues of five to seven years strand chips before they can earn.}

\textbf{Capital duplication ($\kappa_{\text{sov}}$).} Data-residency regimes (GDPR Chapter~V, the EU AI Act, BSI C5, India's DPDP Act)~\cite{ref:gdpr,ref:aiact,ref:dpdp} require in-jurisdiction processing for large classes of data, forcing smaller, under-utilized facilities in Frankfurt, Paris, Dublin, London, Tokyo and Riyadh in place of a few gigawatt sites in cheap-power regions. We model the resulting carry multiplier as $\kappa_{\text{sov}}\sim$Triang(1.1, 1.2, 1.3). This is an engineering judgment about utilization and scale, not a published measurement.

\lead{Demand carve-out ($\gamma_{\text{sov}}$).} Procurement preferences for domestic champions (Mistral, Falcon, Bhashini) and air-gapped defense enclaves remove a fraction $\gamma_{\text{sov}}\sim$Triang(0.15, 0.25, 0.35) of the sovereign-exposed sectors (office assistants, CX, financial, healthcare, legal, public sector, science) from US hyperscalers.

\lead{Grid queues.} Multi-year interconnection backlogs in PJM~\cite{ref:pjm_queue}, Dublin's large-user connection constraint through 2028~\cite{ref:eirgrid_cru}, and Amsterdam's establishment restrictions~\cite{ref:amsterdam} cap the physical velocity of deployment. Capital committed to sites that cannot energize is cash drag: it is in the installed base but produces no revenue. We do not model this separately; it cuts against the bull case.

\lead{Effect.} Applying $\kappa=1.2$, $\gamma=0.25$ to the bottom-up model raises the 2030 plateau deficit by about half, to $\sim$\$645B (Table~\ref{tab:bu_results}); in the top-down sweep it raises the plateau break-even for 2030 from 57\% to $\sim$75\%; the siting friction of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} is the domestic form of the same constraint.

\section{Circular Capital Flows}\label{sec:circ}
\claim{Some cloud revenue is the clouds' own investment coming back; the round trips are small, but the \$190B of lab funding is not.}

\inbrief{\item The strict round trips are small, about \$12B a year; the \$190B of 2026 lab funding is the larger issue.\item The labs and the clouds fund, supply and compete with one another; the squeeze moves money between them, not from the customer.}

Reported cloud revenue is not all exogenous demand. Where a hyperscaler's equity investment in a lab returns as that lab's cloud spend, the revenue is a round trip. Modeling the ecosystem as a directed graph, the circular revenue on a cycle is bounded by its minimum edge weight $\omega(e)$ (the dollars flowing along that edge), $F(C)=\min_{e\in C}\omega(e)$.
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{Circular versus merely non-recurring flows (annual, order of magnitude)}
\label{tab:circular}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{L{4.3cm}L{1.8cm}L{2.2cm}X}
\toprule
\textbf{Flow} & \textbf{Class} & \textbf{Order} & \textbf{Basis} \\
\midrule
Microsoft $\leftrightarrow$ OpenAI & Circular & $\sim$\$4B & Equity/credits returning as Azure consumption~\cite{ref:hudson_policy}\verify \\
Nvidia $\leftrightarrow$ CoreWeave & Circular & $\sim$\$3B & Equity and capacity backstops returning as GPU orders~\cite{ref:coreweave_s1}\verify \\
Amazon/Alphabet $\leftrightarrow$ Anthropic & Circular & $\sim$\$3B & Convertible notes returning as Trainium/TPU consumption~\cite{ref:techcrunch_anthropic}\verify \\
Multi-party (Oracle, CoreWeave) & Partly circular & $\sim$\$2--5B & Colocation and capacity swaps; edge weights not disclosed \\
\midrule
\textbf{Genuinely circular} & & \textbf{$\sim$\$1.2$\times$10$^{10}$} & Triang(8, 12, 18) in the model \\
\midrule
VC-funded startup API spend & Non-recurring & $\sim$\$1.5--2$\times$10$^{10}$ & Real cash, but $\sim$80\% of the cohort fails within 2--3 years\verify~\cite{ref:cbinsights}; modeled as the low tail of enterprise demand, not as circular \\
Oracle RPO backlog & Backlog & \$3$\times$10$^{11}$+ & A contracted future obligation to non-investment-grade counterparties~\cite{ref:oracle_10q}; a collectability risk, not a loop \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}
Only the first three rows are round trips in the strict sense; the rest are real but fragile. The circularity that matters in 2026 is larger and sits outside this table: the $\sim$\$190B of lab equity raised this year, a large share of which is spent on hyperscaler compute (Section~\ref{sec:demand}).

\lead{Off-balance-sheet structures.} As in the 1999--2001 telecom buildout, capacity is increasingly financed through SPVs with infrastructure funds (Blackstone, Brookfield, Blue Owl) and vendor-backstopped debt. This does not reduce the carry; it converts equity capex into debt service with DSCR tripwires that accelerate distressed liquidation when revenue undershoots.



\subsection{Labs and landlords: partners, rivals, and sometimes the same company}\label{sec:labslandlords}
\claim{The labs and the clouds fund, supply and compete with one another; who wins the squeeze moves the margin between them, not the gap with the customer.}

\lead{Three relationships, not one.} The frontier labs and the infrastructure owners are customers and suppliers, investors and investees, and competitors, often all at once. Which relationship dominates decides who keeps the margin on an AI dollar, and so decides the cloud-capture share $\theta$.

\lead{Where they play nicely.} The hyperscalers fund the labs, and the labs spend the money on the hyperscalers' compute (Table~\ref{tab:circular}). Anthropic's S-1 now puts this in a filing: committed spend ``substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure'', reported as \$111B to Google, \$110B to AWS and \$31B to Microsoft, noncancelable~\cite{ref:anthropic_s1,ref:dealroom_s1}.

The labs also resell through the clouds --- Bedrock, Vertex --- and book the end-customer spend gross~\cite{ref:sacra_anthropic}. For the infrastructure owner this is the comfortable case: guaranteed demand, partly paid with its own investment.

\lead{Where they squeeze each other.} Each side is trying to take the other's margin.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The labs squeeze the landlords} by spreading their compute across several clouds and buyers' terms, so that no landlord can price as a monopolist; by building or leasing their own capacity; and by improving their own margins. Every point of lab gross margin gained is a point of $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ lost to the owners (Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}).
\item \textbf{The landlords squeeze the labs} by building their own chips (TPU, Trainium, MTIA), which lowers their cost and caps what labs can charge; by training their own models and selling them on the same shelf as the labs'; and by bundling AI into products the labs cannot reach, such as search, office suites and advertising.
\end{itemize}

\lead{Where they are one and the same.} Google and Meta are both lab and landlord: Gemini and Meta's models run on their own data centers, so for them $\theta\approx1$ and there is no margin to argue over --- but there is also no outside customer paying the carry. Their AI must earn its return through advertising and subscriptions, which is why the search slowdown of the third quarter matters (What Is New in Version 28).

\lead{What it does to the model.} The paper carries the tension in one number, the lab channel's share $\theta_{\text{lab}}\approx0.64$ (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}). If the labs win the squeeze, $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ falls and receipts fall with it; if the landlords win, the labs' margins stay thin and the labs need more investor capital, which is the circular flow above.

Either way the carry is paid from the same end-customer dollar: the squeeze moves the gap between labs and landlords, it does not close it. Noncancelable commitments shift the risk: if lab revenue disappoints, the landlords are paid until the labs cannot pay. That is a credit question for 2028--30, not a revenue question for 2026.

\section{Enterprise ROI and the Slack Trap}\label{sec:slack}
\claim{Time saved is not money saved: firms realize a fraction of AI's nominal savings, and that fraction is what pays the carry.}

\inbrief{\item Enterprises would need about \$1.1T of real benefit in 2028 to justify their share of the carry.\item AI's savings come in fragments, need checking and get absorbed, so only about 28 cents of each dollar of nominal savings becomes cash.\item Controlled studies show real gains at the task level; the leak is between the task and the profit.}

\subsection{What enterprises must realize}
\claim{In 2028 enterprises would need to realize about \$1.35T of benefit to justify their share of the carry.}

Take 2028 under Plateau: carry $\sim$\$645B, all builders (Table~\ref{tab:carry}). Enterprise buyers supply roughly 70\% of AI revenue, so they must pay $\sim$\$450B to the owners of the data centers and the labs. Every dollar of software or token spend carries $\mu_{\text{integ}}\sim$Triang(1.0, 1.5, 2.5) dollars of integration, so total enterprise outlay is $\sim$\$1.13T, and with a required benefit margin $m\approx0.2$ over cost for an unproven technology~\cite{ref:koller2020,ref:brealey2023} --- every firm applies a hurdle to new spending, and the hurdle is higher, not lower, for a technology whose payback is uncertain --- enterprises must realize $B_{\text{req}}\approx\$1.35\times10^{12}$/yr of auditable benefit by 2028.

\subsection{The labor-substitution floor}
\claim{The \$1.35T of benefit enterprises need is about 4.5\% of world knowledge-worker pay --- possible, but only if savings turn into cash.}

Against a global knowledge-worker payroll of $\sim$\$30T (1.05B workers at $\sim$\$28.5k fully loaded) and a US pool of $\sim$\$9T (62M at $\sim$\$145k)~\cite{ref:bls_oes,ref:bls_ecec}, $B_{\text{req}}$ is $\sim$4.5\% of global payroll or $\sim$15\% of US payroll if the US bore it alone. Benefits need not be layoffs --- revenue growth, avoided hiring through attrition, faster cycle times all count --- but they must be auditable cash flow.

\subsection{Four reasons realized yield is a fraction of nominal savings}
\claim{Savings come in fragments, need checking, get absorbed, and managers protect their teams --- so only about 28 cents per dollar becomes cash.}

\inbrief{\item Time saved comes in fragments that cannot be turned into fewer staff.\item Someone has to check AI's work; freed time gets absorbed; managers protect their teams.\item Together only about 28 cents of each dollar of nominal savings becomes cash.\item Controlled studies show real task-level gains; the leak is between the task and the profit and loss.}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Indivisibility.} Tools save fragments of tasks; thirty minutes a day across six workflows does not aggregate across ten employees into one dismissal. Avoided hiring through attrition is the realistic path, and it is slow.
    \item \textbf{Verification.} While liability (SEC, FINRA, HIPAA, professional conduct) rests with human officers, human review is an irreducible fixed cost; labor shifts from generation to verification.
    \item \textbf{Parkinson--Jevons rebound.} Cheaper output inflates the demand for output: three proposal variants, sixty-page reports, more meetings to digest synthetic overflow. Gross volume rises; cash flow does not.
    \item \textbf{The manager's dilemma.} Compensation, prestige and political capital are indexed to headcount. Departments report ``bandwidth expansion,'' not surplus payroll.
\end{enumerate}
The lag has precedent, and the precedent is measured. Paul David's study of the electric dynamo found that factories took roughly forty years, from the 1880s to the 1920s, to reorganize around electric power before productivity responded, because the gain came from rebuilding the factory floor, not from the motor~\cite{ref:david1990}.

The personal computer arrived in 1981; US labor productivity growth ran at 1.4\%/yr from 1973 to 1995 --- Solow's 1987 paradox --- and accelerated to 2.9\%/yr only in 1995--2004, a fifteen-year lag that Jorgenson, Ho and Stiroh attribute to information technology once complementary investment had been made~\cite{ref:bls_productivity,ref:jorgenson2008}; Brynjolfsson and Hitt's firm-level work found returns to computers appearing over five to seven years and only where organization changed with them~\cite{ref:brynjolfsson_hitt2000}.

\lead{The adoption curve.} The internet's contribution followed the same shape. The present wave is, by the aggregate data, at the start of that curve: the Census Bureau's business survey put AI use in production at roughly a tenth of US firms in 2025\verify~\cite{ref:census_btos}, and no acceleration is yet visible in the productivity statistics.

What the evidence says at the firm level so far: an MIT study of enterprise generative-AI deployments in 2025 found that about 95\% of pilots produced no measurable profit-and-loss impact, the gains concentrating in a small minority that reorganized workflows around the tool~\cite{ref:mit_nanda}; McKinsey's 2025 survey found most firms using AI somewhere but only $\sim$1\% describing their deployments as mature~\cite{ref:mckinsey_ai2025}.

\lead{Real gains at the task level.} Where controlled studies exist the task-level gains are real --- 14\% more issues resolved per hour in a call center, larger for novices~\cite{ref:brynjolfsson_callcenter}; 26\% more completed tasks with an AI coding assistant in a randomized trial~\cite{ref:cui_copilot}; 12\% more tasks and 25\% faster in a consulting-task experiment~\cite{ref:dellacqua_bcg} --- and none of those studies reports the headcount that was removed as a result.

The gap between task-level gains and firm-level cash is the slack trap's footprint, and it is the same footprint the ERP and office-software waves left: fractional time savings on the desktop, no matching line in the payroll.

Companies that do cut do it wholesale, in restructurings attributed to AI in the press release, and those show up in the labor data as ordinary restructurings; the paper's 1.5\% displacement marker (Section~\ref{sec:falsify}) is what would distinguish the two. We formalize realized benefit as
\begin{equation}
B_{\text{realized}}=W\,\tau\,(1-\sigma_{\text{slack}})(1-\nu_{\text{verify}})-C_{\text{severance}},
\end{equation}
where $W$ is the payroll pool, $\tau\sim$Triang(0.10, 0.15, 0.25) the nominal share of work time AI saves, $\sigma_{\text{slack}}\approx0.65$ the share of that saving absorbed as slack, $\nu_{\text{verify}}\approx0.20$ the share consumed by verification, and $C_{\text{severance}}$ one-time separation costs. These two coefficients are the paper's most consequential and least measured assumptions; they are judgments consistent with the J-curve literature~\cite{ref:brynjolfsson2021}, not measurements.

At the modes, realized yield is $0.28\,\tau$: a 15\% time saving converts to $\sim$4\% of payroll --- the global requirement, but only over a full J-curve cycle of 8--12 years, against silicon lives of 3--5.

\section{Last-Mile Services and Value Capture}\label{sec:lastmile}
\claim{Much of AI's value goes to buyers and to the integrators who fix the process, not to the cloud.}

\inbrief{\item A dollar of cloud tokens can produce 10 to 50 dollars of savings for the buyer: good for the buyer, little for the cloud.\item Most in-house AI projects fail, so firms turn to outside operators paid on results.}

\subsection{Where the surplus goes}
\claim{Professional-services firms lose billings to AI; little of what they lose shows up as cloud revenue.}

The \$2.65T professional-services pool (consulting $\sim$\$1.05T, accounting/audit $\sim$\$0.65T, legal $\sim$\$0.95T)\verify faces compression of billable hours (tax prep, audit workpapers, contract review) that puts of order 30\% of billings ($\sim$\$0.8T) under deflation. Outcome-priced last-mile operators capturing 10--18\% of realized savings under gain-share terms address a \$80--140B/yr market, at high gross margins, using small fine-tuned open-weight models on modest hardware. Upstream token intensity of 1.5--3\% leaves the hyperscalers \$1--4B of that pool.

\subsection{The 10--50:1 disconnect}
\claim{A dollar of cloud tokens produces 10 to 50 dollars of savings for the buyer --- good for the buyer, little for the cloud.}

Contact centers illustrate the ratio. Global contact-center labor is $\sim$\$350B. A plausible 2026--27 displacement is 5--10\%, or \$20--35B/yr (a figure of \$80B, sometimes quoted, would imply a quarter of the global pool already gone, which the labor data do not show). The RAG and speech inference behind that displacement consumes \$1--2B/yr of tokens.

The ratio is 10--35:1; Nordhaus's economy-wide 2.2\% innovator share~\cite{ref:nordhaus2004} corresponds to $\sim$45:1. Freight routing and travel administration show the same pattern but are mostly classical optimization, not AI tokens, and we no longer count them.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{The value-capture disconnect: realized savings versus upstream tokens (order of magnitude)}
\label{tab:disconnect}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{L{3.3cm}L{3.0cm}L{3.0cm}X}
\toprule
\textbf{Workflow} & \textbf{Billing base} & \textbf{Realized savings, 2026--27} & \textbf{Upstream cloud tokens} \\
\midrule
Contact centers \& support & $\sim$\$350B global labor & \$20--35B/yr & \$1--2B/yr (RAG, speech) \\
Corporate travel \& admin & $\sim$\$1.4T business travel & \$10--30B/yr overhead & Fractional (database triggers) \\
Tax prep, audit workpapers, contract review & $\sim$\$0.8T of billings under deflation & \$40--80B/yr by 2028 & \$1--4B/yr (small fine-tuned models) \\
Freight \& logistics routing & $\sim$\$2.6T US logistics & \$50--100B/yr & Near zero (classical optimization, not LLM tokens; excluded from AI demand) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{The talent trap and the timing chasm}
\claim{Most in-house AI projects fail, so firms turn to outside operators paid on results, which delays cloud demand.}

An in-house MLOps and agentic pod costs \$3.5--5M/yr, and most internal proofs-of-concept do not reach production (surveys put the figure above 70--80\%\verify). CFOs are shifting to outcome-aligned external operators. The cycle --- failed internal experiment, procurement, compliance validation --- delays material revenue by 3--5 years, against a silicon life of 3--5 years. That is the timing chasm in one sentence.

\subsection{Reconciling the slack trap with the leakage story}
\claim{Slack dominates in high-liability work, leakage in high-volume work; both keep money away from the cloud.}

These are not contradictory; they dominate in different places. In high-liability knowledge work (legal, finance, medicine, engineering) the slack trap dominates: savings are real but do not reach cash flow. In high-volume, low-liability process work (contact centers, claims intake, document routing) savings do reach cash flow, and there the leakage story dominates: the buyer and the integrator keep it. Neither regime routes much to the cloud meter.


\section{Hardware Cycle and Commoditization}
\claim{Cheaper chips, smaller numbers and open models keep cutting the price of AI work, so the same revenue needs less cloud.}

\inbrief{\item Cheaper chips, lower-precision maths and open models keep cutting the cost of AI work.\item Memory supply swings and on-device chips move routine work off the cloud.\item The same revenue therefore needs less cloud, which caps what the installed base can earn.}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Precision shifts.} FP4 microscaling delivers several times the throughput per watt of FP8/FP16 generations, and ternary-weight architectures remove floating-point multiply from the critical path. In power-constrained facilities this strands the prior generation regardless of its silicon health.
    \item \textbf{HBM bullwhip.} Lower-precision inference cuts memory-bandwidth demand per token. Capacity additions by SK Hynix, Samsung and Micron sized to 2025 demand risk oversupply by 2027--28.
    \item \textbf{Open weights as a deflationary wedge.} Llama, Mistral, DeepSeek and Nvidia's own open models commoditize the model layer. When a 70B-class model runs on a workstation, API pricing converges toward the electricity floor, removing the platform margin the hurdle needs. The low-cost invasion is already under way: Chinese open-weight models trained at a fraction of frontier cost are run on-premise by Western enterprises for routine work even where cloud access to Chinese providers is restricted or prohibited, because the weights, once published, are not a service that can be blocked.

    Distilled and quantized derivatives of those models are what the last-mile operators of Section~\ref{sec:lastmile} deploy. \emph{Microscaling} --- a block floating-point scheme in which groups of values share one exponent, standardized by the Open Compute Project in 2023 --- is what makes FP4 usable for inference and is expected to be adopted fastest where hardware is scarcest.
    \item \textbf{Edge and on-premise.} Two different things reduce cloud receipts: inference on the user's own device (phones and PCs with NPUs), and inference on a customer's own servers (on-premise clusters running open-weight models). The paper's cloud-capture share $\theta_s$ combines both; neither reaches a hyperscaler meter.
    \item \textbf{Quantum computing} is not modeled. Gate rates, error correction and the absence of scalable quantum memory keep it outside the 2026--2035 window for commercial inference or training; it is mentioned only to say so.
\end{itemize}

\section{Geopolitical Divergence}
\claim{The US, Europe, China and others are building separate AI stacks; each duplication adds capital without adding customers.}

\inbrief{\item The US, Europe, China and others are building separate AI stacks with different power costs and rules.\item Each duplication adds capital without adding customers.\item China's cheaper power and state siting make it a low-cost competitor for the same work.}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{United States:} carries most of the global hyperscale debt and capex; industrial power \$0.10--0.14/kWh in PJM and rising; 5--7 year interconnection queues~\cite{ref:eia_epm,ref:pjm_queue}. No federal carbon price; California's cap-and-trade and the northeastern RGGI scheme cover a minority of load.
    \item \textbf{Europe (FLAPD):} power \$0.145--0.195/kWh, ETS carbon at $\sim$EUR~70/t, Dublin frozen to 2028~\cite{ref:eirgrid_cru}, Amsterdam capped~\cite{ref:amsterdam}.
    \item \textbf{China:} interconnection is a planning decision rather than a queue, and siting objection is not a constraint; curtailed western renewables at \$0.02--0.035/kWh~\verify under \emph{East Data, West Computing}; state-subsidized training for industrial and humanoid robotics; open-weight export. Embodied AI is bandwidth-heavy: a four-camera robot at 30 fps and $\sim$60 spatial tokens per frame produces $\sim$2.7$\times$10$^{7}$ tokens per camera-hour, which favors cheap power over cheap chips.
    \item \textbf{Middle powers:} India (DPI), the Gulf, Korea --- sovereign models on stranded power and open weights, bypassing US hyperscalers for domestic demand.
    \item \textbf{Atoms.} Transformers on 36--48 month lead times, electronic-grade polysilicon, copper and neon set the deployment velocity; integrated supply chains with cheap state power hold a structural advantage over permitting-bound Western sites.
\end{itemize}


\section{Economic Depreciation, Utilization, and the Commodity Floor}\label{sec:commodity}
\claim{A commodities reviewer's three points: chips lose value faster than the books say, utilization gains go to buyers, and prices fall toward cash cost.}

\inbrief{\item An AI chip is worth what it can earn against the newest chip, so the silicon the five largest bought through 2026 is already worth \$100--250B less than the books show.\item Running chips harder serves more work, but if prices fall in step it brings in no more money.\item If prices fall to the cash cost of running the chips --- about a tenth of today's --- the capital is never recovered.\item The cheapest producers set the price; the GPU renters are squeezed first.}

Three points raised by a reviewer with a commodities background~\cite{ref:mehta_2026} change how the supply side should be read, and each is now in the model as a secondary variable.

\subsection{Economic versus accounting depreciation}
\claim{An AI chip is worth what it can earn against the newest one, so the silicon the five largest bought through 2026 is already worth \$100--250B less than the books show.}

The carry in Section~\ref{sec:supply} depreciates silicon straight-line over an effective 4.2 years --- 20--30\%/yr, close to the operators' accounting. The economic view is different: an installed accelerator is worth what it can earn against the newest chip, and the marginal cost of inference per token on the newest chip falls 50--90\% with each generation.

On that view the value of a cohort follows a declining balance, $\text{NBV}_t=G(1-\delta)^{\text{age}}$, with $\delta\sim$Triang(0.5, 0.7, 0.9) per year~\cite{ref:mehta_2026,ref:epoch_spending}, and most of a cohort's value is gone within two years whether or not the silicon still runs --- the direction Amazon's own 2025 shortening of server lives points~\cite{ref:amzn_10k_life}.

\lead{The carry moves earlier.} Two consequences.
\begin{itemize}
\item First, the carry is front-loaded: at $\delta=0.7$ the 2027 carry is $\sim$\$500B rather than \$465B and the 2030 carry $\sim$\$800B rather than \$890B, because value written off early is not written off later (Figure~\ref{fig:econdep}, left). The annual deficits from 2027 are similar in size but flatter; what changes is timing, and the timing runs against the operators, since the losses fall in the years the paper already calls a sunk deficit.
\item Second, and more immediately, the silicon bought in 2023--26 is already impaired on the economic schedule: its straight-line net book value at end-2026 is $\sim$\$330B, its economic value \$75--230B, an unrecognized gap of \$100B ($\delta=0.5$), \$175B ($\delta=0.7$) or \$250B ($\delta=0.9$).
\end{itemize}

That gap is what ASC 360-10's fair-value step would recognize if the recoverability test failed; it is the mechanism, not the useful-life note, by which the impairment wave of Section~\ref{sec:falsify} would arrive. The economic depreciation rate, $\delta$, is a secondary variable, and the evidence on it currently points both ways: the cost-per-token series that Epoch AI maintains falls at the rates assumed, while Silicon Data's rental index has H100 and A100 rental prices \emph{rising} through 2026 (\$2.63 and \$1.59 per GPU-hour in September), with residual values inferred from them rising too~\cite{ref:silicon_data,ref:a16z_som}.

Rising rentals are what scarcity looks like before the 2027--28 cohorts energize; a decline toward \$1 per H100-hour would be the floor arriving. The variable stays swept until that series moves.

\subsection{Utilization}
\claim{Running chips harder serves more work, but if prices fall in step, it brings in no more money.}

Inference throughput is modeled by operators at 40--50\% utilization of nominal capacity, the rest held as latency and peak buffer~\cite{ref:mehta_2026}. Batching, quantization and the proliferation of open-weight models could double useful utilization for the heaviest workloads (register entry \texttt{inference\_utilization}, Triang(0.40, 0.45, 0.50) today; doubling case as a sweep).

The same stock then serves twice the tokens. But receipts in this paper are dollars, not tokens, and doubling tokens raises dollars only if the price per token holds --- which is precisely what the next subsection says it will not. Higher utilization is a Jevons effect: more inference, cheaper, from the same capital, with no relief for the carry.

\subsection{Batch share, and what four decades of computing already taught}
\claim{Computing has been here before: when work moves to batch, the buyer, not the owner, takes the gain.}

Utilization does not rise on its own; it rises when work is moved into batch. The labs now sell asynchronous batch processing at 50--75\% below interactive rates with 24-hour windows, and prompt caching at $\sim$90\% off repeated context~\cite{ref:batch_pricing}; the register carries a \emph{batch share} $b\sim$Triang(0.3, 0.5, 0.7) of 2030 tokens processed this way (a secondary variable).

At the mode, effective fleet utilization rises from 45\% to $\sim$64\% and the volume-weighted effective price falls to $\sim$70\% of list; for heavy pipelines that stack batch and caching the effective price is 5--10 times below list, i.e.\ within one to three times the marginal cash cost.

\lead{Utilization: an old lesson.} This is not new. Mainframe operators learned in the 1970s that a machine paid for by the shift is only economic if the night is filled with batch, and ran them at 90\%-plus; the grid- and distributed-computing decades made the same trade at the price of latency; and Bitcoin mining is the closest precedent of all --- a commodity computation on special-purpose silicon, where within two years of each ASIC generation the hashprice converged to the marginal cost of the cheapest power, the equipment was obsolete in 12--24 months, and capacity migrated to wherever electricity was cheapest.

\lead{Gains go to the buyer.} Inference has every one of those properties. The lesson of the precedents is that utilization gains are always taken by the buyer, not the producer: they lower the floor rather than lift the receipts. One caution on the numbers: Oracle reports GPU \emph{allocation} utilization of 97.9\%~\cite{ref:orcl_q1fy27} --- the share of its fleet rented out --- which is a different quantity from the 40--50\% \emph{throughput} utilization within a rented job that the batch argument concerns; both can be true at once.

\subsection{Who is the low-cost producer}
\claim{The cheapest producers --- own-chip clouds and open models --- set the price; the GPU renters are the first squeezed.}

In a commodity the survivors at the floor are the producers with the lowest full cost --- the BYDs, not the incumbents --- and the paper's carry should be allocated to them by tier rather than spread across the industry. Figure~\ref{fig:coststack} builds a cost curve for five producer types from the same chain as the marginal cash cost (power, PUE, tariff~\cite{ref:eia_epm}, opex, tokens per hour) plus each tier's capital cost per token (capex per accelerator slot~\cite{ref:nvda_q1fy27}, life~\cite{ref:amzn_10k_life,ref:hudson_policy}, cost of capital~\cite{ref:damodaran2026}, utilization~\cite{ref:orcl_q1fy27}).

\lead{The producer cost curve.} At the modes: on-premise open-weight small models for routine work cost \$0.03 per million tokens all-in; in-house-silicon hyperscalers (TPU, Trainium, MTIA) \$0.14; Chinese western-corridor capacity on domestic silicon \$0.27 (cheap power offset by less efficient chips); Nvidia-based US hyperscalers \$0.29; debt-funded neo-clouds \$0.40. Against a blended list price near \$0.30 the two Nvidia-based tiers are at or above break-even on full cost; against the effective prices heavy users already pay (\$0.03--0.06), only the on-premise and in-house-silicon tiers cover their carry.

\lead{Neo-clouds go first.} That ordering is the exposure table of Section~\ref{sec:falsify} derived rather than asserted: the neo-cloud is the marginal producer the floor eliminates first; the Nvidia-based hyperscaler is next unless it moves to its own silicon; Google's TPU fleet is the industry's lowest-cost large producer; and the Chinese corridor competes on power while its chip efficiency lags.

The ``BYD of AI'' is not one company but a tier --- in-house silicon on cheap power running open-weight models --- and the carry the paper computes falls, in the end, on whoever is not in it.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f24_coststack.pdf}}
\caption{The inference cost curve. Producers ordered by full cost per million tokens (bar width is share of 2030 capacity); dark is marginal cash cost, light adds the carry (depreciation and return). Dotted: blended list price; dashed: effective price paid by batch-and-cache heavy users. At effective prices only the two lowest tiers cover their carry.}\label{fig:coststack}\end{figure}

\subsection{The commodity floor}
\claim{If prices fall to the cash cost of running the chips --- a tenth of today's --- the capital is never recovered.}

In a commodity, price converges to marginal cash cost and the return on fixed capital goes to whoever built cheapest; uneconomic capital is written off or replaced by lighter structures~\cite{ref:mehta_2026}. Inference has the shape of a commodity: an undifferentiated unit (the token), many producers, falling costs, open-weight models removing the product moat.

The marginal cash cost of a token is the cash it takes to produce one more --- power and facility opex, not depreciation or return.

\lead{The marginal cash cost of inference.} A chain: an accelerator drawing Triang(0.7, 0.9, 1.2) kW at PUE Triang(1.12, 1.22, 1.35) and a tariff of Triang(\$0.075, 0.10, 0.13)/kWh, plus non-power opex of Triang(\$0.05, 0.10, 0.18) per accelerator-hour, costs Triang-derived $\sim$\$0.20--0.35 per hour in cash; at Triang(3, 6, 10) million tokens per hour at 45\% utilization~\cite{ref:eia_epm,ref:batch_pricing} it produces tokens at a marginal cash cost of \$0.036 per million (P10--P90 \$0.026--0.053), or half that if utilization doubles (Figure~\ref{fig:econdep}, right).

\lead{Realized prices against the floor.} Realized \emph{list} prices are \$0.10--0.30 per million for small and mid models and \$1--5 for frontier models --- roughly ten times the floor, blended --- but effective prices for batch-and-cache pipelines are \$0.03--0.06, within one to three times the floor (register entry \texttt{token\_price\_index}, effective tier).

If prices converge to the floor while volume rises to meet it, receipts converge on the fleet's cash cost --- the opex line, $\sim$\$150B in 2028 and $\sim$\$220B in 2030 --- against a carry of \$645B then and \$890B in 2030. On the commodity view the depreciation and return terms are never recovered by anyone but the lowest-cost producer, and the paper's deficit is not a shortfall to be grown out of but the ordinary fate of fixed capital in a commodity whose price found its floor.

\lead{The metric to watch.} It is not revenue but the ratio of realized price per million tokens to marginal cash cost: the paper's register now carries both (\texttt{token\_price\_index}, \texttt{marginal\_cash\_cost}), and a ratio approaching one from above is the signal that the commodity view has won.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f23_econdep_floor.pdf}}
\caption{Left: the plateau carry under straight-line depreciation (base) and under declining-balance economic obsolescence at 50, 70 and 90\%/yr, against bottom-up receipts; economic depreciation front-loads the carry. Right: the marginal cash cost of inference per million tokens at current and doubled utilization, against realized price points; prices are about ten times the floor.}\label{fig:econdep}\end{figure}

\subsection{Market context}
\claim{Short sellers already bet against the pure GPU renters.}

The commodities reviewer also notes that the pure-play GPU renters --- the neo-clouds of the exposure table --- are among the most heavily shorted positions in public markets in 2026, and that the uneconomic nature of the projects is argued publicly by short sellers including Jim Chanos~\cite{ref:mehta_2026}.

This is corroboration of the paper's neo-cloud row, not evidence for it: a crowded short is a market opinion, and market opinions are inputs to nothing in the register. It is recorded because it locates the paper's argument in a live debate and because the short interest itself is one more observable to watch.

\section{The Bull Case at Its Strongest: a16z's \textit{State of Markets}, September 2026}\label{sec:a16z}
\claim{The best bull case agrees with the paper on nearly every fact; it differs on two judgments.}

\inbrief{\item a16z's deck is the strongest case for the build-out, so the paper answers it point by point.\item The two agree on nearly every fact: capex, backlogs, rising GPU rents, returns on existing businesses.\item They disagree on two judgments: whether AI is paid from labor budgets as well as IT budgets, and whether scarce compute is permanent or a cycle top.\item The paper's arms-race path is the street's central case, not a stress test.}

Andreessen Horowitz's September 2026 \textit{State of Markets} deck~\cite{ref:a16z_som} is the most complete statement of the case for the build-out, assembled by an investor with every reason to make it well. It is also the best test of this paper, because most of its charts bear directly on a register entry. Each of its claims is taken in turn: the evidence it offers, and what the register does with it.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{``We keep underestimating the capex.''} Street consensus for Big-5 capex has been revised upward every year; the September 2026 consensus is about \$1.05--1.1T for 2027 and \$1.15T for 2028, and on the October calls every company that gave a direction pointed 2027 up~\cite{ref:gs_capex_bbg}, and J.P.~Morgan's figures are \$791B, \$1,066B and \$1,179B for 2026--28~\cite{ref:a16z_som,ref:jpmam_gtm}.

\emph{What the register does with it:} the paper's ``arms race'' path (\$1.0--1.1T) is not a stress case --- it is the street's central expectation --- and the ``plateau'' is below consensus. Readers who take consensus at face value should read the arms-race rows of every table: a 2030 deficit of $\sim$\$745B and no year of profit through 2035. The paper labels the path accordingly.

\item \textbf{``Capex is eating cash flow, for now.''} Hyperscaler free cash flow is near zero in 2026--27; the consensus has it rebounding past \$500B by 2030~\cite{ref:a16z_som,ref:jpmam_gtm}. \emph{Register:} a rebound of that size on a capex path that keeps rising to \$1.18T requires revenue above \$1T by 2029--30 --- precisely the top-down break-even test.

The two consensus series (capex up, free cash flow up) are consistent with each other only on the paper's bull case; one of them will be revised.

\item \textbf{``Reports of GPU obsolescence are exaggerated.''} Silicon Data's September 2026 rental index puts the H100 at \$2.63 per GPU-hour, the A100 at \$1.59, the H200 at \$3.29 and the B200 at \$5.69, with residual values inferred from rentals rising through 2026~\cite{ref:a16z_som,ref:silicon_data}. \emph{Register:} this is the strongest evidence on the other side of the economic-depreciation variable $\delta$ (Section~\ref{sec:commodity}), and it is why $\delta$ is a secondary variable rather than a range.

Two readings coexist. Rental prices rising while token prices fall means compute is scarce today; the paper's claim is about what happens when the 2027--28 cohorts energize. And \$2.63 per hour against a marginal cash cost of \$0.20--0.35 per hour is an 8--13$\times$ margin for the GPU owner --- the same ratio of price to floor the commodity-floor section finds at the token level, from the other end. The variable to watch is the H100 rental price through 2027; a decline toward \$1 is the floor arriving.

\item \textbf{``Backlogs have doubled year on year.''} Microsoft RPO, Google Cloud backlog and Amazon RPO together approach \$1.7T, up $\sim$150\% year on year~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} added as an observable. A backlog is a contract, not a payment: Oracle's \$664B converts over years and is over half one counterparty (Section~\ref{sec:observe}); Google's \$514B is not split between AI and the rest.

Backlog growth is evidence of demand for capacity, which the paper does not dispute; the carry test is about the price that capacity ultimately earns.

\item \textbf{``Hyperscaler ROIC is above the cost of borrowing.''} Bank of America puts hyperscaler return on invested capital near 20\% against a cost of debt of 4--5\%~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} that ROIC is earned on the existing businesses --- search, advertising, the installed cloud --- not on the AI cohorts, whose return is what this paper computes.

Borrowing at 4.5\% to fund assets whose incremental return is in question is the ordinary structure of a capital cycle, and the deck's own page on the railroads and telecoms (capex at 1.5--2\% of GDP at each cycle's peak) makes the comparison.

\item \textbf{``Data centers may be lowering your electricity bill.''} Watten, Bistline and Blanford find that each 10\% increase in state data-center capacity lowered average residential rates by about 0.4\% over 2019--24, by spreading the grid's fixed costs~\cite{ref:watten2026}. \emph{Register:} taken as stated, and it is an important correction to the ratepayer narrative.

It does not change the siting variable, which is calibrated on what communities \emph{do} (Data Center Watch's blocked and delayed projects), not on what rates \emph{did}; and the study predates the 2025--26 load growth and the Northern Virginia and Texas tariff increases. Both facts stand: rates fell on average through 2024, and opposition has risen since.

\item \textbf{``Enterprise AI spending has plenty of room to run.''} Only 20\% of organizations cite cost as a constraint; cloud reached $\sim$4\% of IT budgets in its second year and AI is below that; and the deck argues the addressable market is ``considerably larger than IT budgets''~\cite{ref:a16z_som}.

\emph{Register:} the last clause is the real disagreement, stated clearly. The paper's feasibility band is an IT-budget band. The deck's position is that AI is paid from labor budgets --- the \$30T global payroll --- not from the \$3.3T software-and-services pool.

Whether labor budgets actually convert to AI spend is Section~\ref{sec:slack}'s question, and the paper's answer (about a quarter of nominal savings reach cash, over a J-curve) is why it keeps the IT-budget band. If the deck is right, the band is wrong by a factor of several and the plateau pays easily; if the slack trap is right, the band holds. This is the single most consequential disagreement between the two documents and the register cannot settle it from 2026 data.

\item \textbf{``Adoption is wide but shallow; power users are pulling away.''} The top 1\% of users consume orders of magnitude more AI than the field, and the gap is widening~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} the same observation as MIT NANDA's 95\% and the Census Bureau's tenth of firms (Section~\ref{sec:slack}); the deck reads it as upside, the paper as the early J-curve. Both are consistent with the data; they differ on timing, which is the residual adoption factor.

\item \textbf{``Tokens up 600$\times$ in two years; frontier labs capture 70\% of spend on 35\% of tokens.''} On OpenRouter, Anthropic, Google and OpenAI take $\sim$69\% of spending on $\sim$35\% of tokens; all other labs 65\% of tokens for 31\% of spending; open models on China-based hyperscaler endpoints are under 0.3\% of model spend~\cite{ref:a16z_som,ref:openrouter}.

\emph{Register:} this is the cloud-capture share and the commodity floor measured from the demand side: two-thirds of tokens already trade at the cheap end, a frontier premium persists for frontier problems, and the premium tier is three labs. It raises confidence in $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ and in the cost-curve ordering, and it is the deck's best evidence for a durable frontier margin.

\item \textbf{``Jevons: compute prices rise as token prices fall.''} The deck's reading of rising H100 rentals alongside falling token prices is that efficiency gains are absorbed by demand~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} the paper agrees that this is what Jevons looks like while supply is short; the Bitcoin precedent (Section~\ref{sec:commodity}) is what it looks like after supply catches up. Which regime 2028 is in is the question the arms-race capex path answers one way and the adaptive path the other.

\item \textbf{``SaaS: prove-it, not pocalypse.''} Software revenue growth has slowed but stabilized; operating leverage is stable or improving; the sell-off has been selective (cyber and vertical over horizontal)~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} the seat-software growth variable $g_{\text{seat}}$ is a secondary variable for exactly this reason --- the fundamentals have not yet confirmed the repricing --- and Microsoft's +6--7\% seat growth is already in the incumbent case. The deck's own newspaper chart, in which stocks sold off five years before earnings collapsed, is the caution it offers against its own point.

\item \textbf{``Capex is at or near the peaks of cycles past.''} Lease-adjusted hyperscaler capex at $\sim$1.5\% of US GDP, against $\sim$2\% for the railroads and $\sim$1.5\% for telecoms at their peaks~\cite{ref:a16z_som}. \emph{Register:} the paper's own scale statement (2.5\% of GDP on a gross basis) is brought into line as 1.5--2.5\% depending on definition; the comparison to the two prior cycles that ended in impairment is the deck's, and the paper adopts it.
\end{enumerate}

\lead{Where a16z and the paper differ.} Taken together the deck and the paper agree on nearly every observable and disagree on two judgments: whether AI will be paid from labor budgets as well as IT budgets, and whether rising compute prices are the permanent state of a supply-constrained industry or the top of a cycle. The register names both as the places to look.

\section{The Sell-Side Hurdle: Goldman Sachs's Phase-2 ROIC Framework, September 2026}\label{sec:gs}
\claim{Goldman Sachs's hurdle --- 27 cents of revenue per dollar of capital --- matches the paper's 28.}

\inbrief{\item Goldman Sachs asks what revenue one cohort of spending must earn: \$1.42T over 2028--30.\item Per dollar of capital that is 27 cents a year; this paper's carry is 28. The hurdle is not in dispute.\item The two differ on scope --- one cohort or the whole stack --- and on running costs, which Goldman puts lower.}

On September 24, 2026, Goldman Sachs's Americas Technology team (Sheridan, Borges, Schneider and colleagues) published a 29-page note, \textit{Sizing the AI Economy Needed to Justify ROIC on Hyperscaler AI Capex}~\cite{ref:gs_roic2026}, whose question is this paper's question asked from the other side of the table: not ``what is the carry'' but ``what revenue would make the carry worth paying.'' The note is taken here the way Section~\ref{sec:a16z} took a16z's deck --- claim by claim, against the register.

It is the closest thing to a sell-side replication of the supply-side model that exists, and the two agree on the hurdle to within a few percent; where they differ is scope, one input, and what is assumed about demand.

\subsection{What the note computes}
