\claim{Goldman sizes the revenue one cohort of spending must earn: \$1.42T over 2028--30.}

The framework isolates one cohort: ``Phase 2,'' the AI-compute capex of six US hyperscalers (Alphabet, Microsoft, Amazon, Meta, Oracle and SpaceX) over calendar 2026--27, which the note puts at \$1.726T, taking 85--90\% of each company's total capex as AI-related (AWS segment capex for Amazon, the AI segment for SpaceX). That cohort is split 70\% ``compute'' (servers, chips, networking) depreciated straight-line over 5 years and 30\% ``shells'' (land, buildings, infrastructure) over 15 years.

\lead{Goldman's cost per gigawatt.} An all-in cost of \$42.4B per gigawatt (Epoch AI's year-end 2025 inputs inflated 10\%; servers \$24.1B, network \$12.6B, facilities \$5.4B) implies $\sim$41 GW added. Operating cost excluding depreciation is \$836M per GW-year (also from Epoch, plus 10\%), scaled by an operational ramp of 60\%, 80\% and 85\% in 2028, 2029 and 2030. The return threshold is a 15\% return on invested capital, defined as net operating profit after tax (NOPAT --- operating profit less a 21\% tax) in each of 2028--30 divided by the \emph{average annual} capex of the phase (\$863B).

\lead{The revenue a cohort must earn.} Grossing NOPAT up for tax and adding depreciation and operating cost gives the revenue the cohort must earn: \$465B, \$474B and \$476B in 2028, 2029 and 2030 --- \textbf{\$1.42T cumulative, or \$11.6B per GW-year} --- at an operating margin of $\sim$35\%.

A sensitivity table runs the ROIC threshold from 0\% to 30\% and the cost per GW from \$34B to \$51B: the three-year revenue requirement spans \$908B to \$1.89T, i.e.\ \$6.2--18.6B per GW-year.

\lead{Contracts, not forecasts.} The note then benchmarks the requirement against contracted backlog rather than against a demand forecast. For the three public cloud providers alone (Amazon, Microsoft, Alphabet; Phase-2 capex \$1.22T, $\sim$29 GW) the same framework requires \$1.00T of 2028--30 revenue, which is 59\% of their combined \$1.69T backlog at Q2 2026 (up 152\% year on year): 78\% of Alphabet's \$496B, 68\% of Amazon's \$514B and 40\% of Microsoft's \$678B.

\lead{Goldman's conclusion.} The note's conclusion is that near-term return compression is ``a natural consequence of the massive upfront investment cycle rather than evidence of structurally poor AI economics,'' and that the 15\% threshold is conservative against what the companies themselves describe: Amazon's $\sim$3-year breakeven on servers with a 5--6 year life, Oracle's ``high-20s'' steady-state ROIC on large projects, and SpaceX's stated one-year payback on terrestrial AI capacity.

\lead{A second build, from the chipmakers.} A separate build in the same note --- Nvidia, Broadcom, AMD, Marvell and MediaTek revenue per GW --- implies total AI infrastructure capex of \$861B, \$1.34T and \$2.0T in 2026, 2027 and 2028 (20, 35 and 57 GW), a wider set than the hyperscalers because it includes labs, sovereigns and enterprises.

\subsection{The same hurdle in the paper's units}
\claim{Restated per dollar, the bank's hurdle and the paper's carry are almost the same.}

The note's requirement can be restated per dollar of cohort capital and set beside the carry of Section~\ref{sec:supply}, which is also a cost per dollar of in-service gross. Table~\ref{tab:gs_reconcile} does this for the note's 2029 column and for the paper's 2028 plateau at the modes (Appendix~\ref{app:equations}, worked example: gross \$1.9T, carry \$540B).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{The Goldman Sachs Phase-2 requirement and the paper's carry, per dollar of capital per year}
\label{tab:gs_reconcile}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{L{3.0cm}L{1.6cm}L{1.6cm}X}
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{GS, 2029 (\$/\$ capex)} & \textbf{Paper, 2028 (\$/\$ gross)} & \textbf{Why they differ} \\
\midrule
Depreciation & 0.160 & 0.153 & GS: 70\% compute at 5 yr $+$ 30\% shell at 15 yr. Paper: 55\% silicon at 4.2 yr (harmonic across fleets) $+$ 45\% shell at 20 yr. The shorter life and the smaller silicon share offset. \\
Operating cost (excl.\ depreciation) & 0.020 & 0.067 & GS: Epoch's \$836M/GW-yr, essentially power and maintenance, at 60--85\% ramp. Paper: power 1.9\% $+$ staffing/maintenance 2.0\% $+$ property tax and insurance 1.0\% $+$ network 1.0\% $+$ other 0.6\% (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}). The one material gap; see below. \\
Return & 0.095 & 0.064 & GS: 15\% NOPAT on average annual capex (half the cohort), grossed up to pre-tax EBIT. Paper: CAPM cost of capital (9.45\%) on undepreciated net book ($\sim$68\% of gross). GS's return is a target; the paper's is the floor below which capital is destroyed. \\
\midrule
\textbf{Required revenue} & \textbf{0.274} & \textbf{0.284} & Within 4\%. On the same \$1.9T stock the GS framework would ask \$520B/yr; the paper's carry is \$540B. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\lead{What follows from Goldman's note.} Three things follow.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The hurdle is not in dispute.} A sell-side desk with every incentive to set it low and this paper with its ranges decomposed arrive at 27--28 cents of annual revenue per dollar of capital; the note's own sensitivity (\$6.2--18.6B per GW-year) brackets the paper's modes. Readers who distrust the paper's supply side can substitute Goldman's and change nothing in the result.
\item \textbf{One cohort or the whole stack.} The note's \$1.42T is the requirement of \emph{one} cohort and ignores, by construction, both the 2023--25 cohorts still on the books and the capex of 2028 and beyond; its authors say so. The paper's carry stacks the cohorts (that is what a vintage model is for), which is why its 2028 figure applies to \$1.9T of gross and its 2030 figure to $\sim$\$2.5T. Applying the note's own per-dollar requirement to the stacked stock gives the paper's result.
\item \textbf{The note's cohort is larger.} The cohort itself is larger in the note (\$1.73T) than in the paper (\$1.23T AI-attributable for 2026--27) for two scope reasons: the note counts 85--90\% of all capex as AI, where the paper nets a non-AI baseline that filings pin at $\sim$\$130B in 2023 growing 2--10\%/yr (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}); and the note adds SpaceX. Both are defensible; the register records the note's convention as the upper bound on the AI-attributable share.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Where the paper is harsher, and where it is not}
\claim{The two differ on scope --- one cohort or the whole stack --- and on one input, running costs.}

\inbrief{\item The one input that really differs from Goldman Sachs is running cost: 2\% of capital a year against the paper's 6.65\%.\item If Goldman is right, the 2030 gap falls from about \$420B to about \$270B; the sign and the 2027--29 loss stand.\item Backlog is a contract, not a payment, and management claims are graded as statements by interested parties.}

\textbf{Operating cost.} The one input on which the two frameworks differ materially is non-depreciation operating cost: 2\% of capital per year in the note (power and maintenance, from Epoch) against 6.65\% in the paper, whose decomposition adds staffing, property tax and insurance, network and ``other'' at 4.6\% of gross.

On the 2028 plateau stock (all builders) that is $\sim$\$105B/yr of carry; on the 2030 stock $\sim$\$155B. If the note's figure is right and the paper's is a double count --- property tax is often abated for data centers, and network is partly in the capex --- the 2030 plateau deficit falls from $\sim$\$420B to $\sim$\$270B and its z-score from $\sim$5.6 to $\sim$3.7 (\texttt{model/variants\_check.py}); the sign and the 2027--28 sunk deficit are unchanged.

\lead{Not changed silently.} The register now carries both anchors on $c_{\text{opex}}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}) and the input is flagged for a sourced operator cost per MW-year; by the update protocol's rule (Appendix~\ref{app:update}) adopting the lower anchor would be a material re-issue, so it is not done silently here.

\lead{Demand.} The note does not forecast the revenue; it shows what revenue is needed and observes that backlog covers it. The paper's two demand estimates put total infrastructure receipts --- from \emph{all} cohorts --- at $\sim$\$345B in 2028 and $\sim$\$480B in 2030 (Table~\ref{tab:bottomup}), i.e.\ 2030 receipts from the whole stock roughly equal to what the note requires from the 2026--27 cohort alone.

\lead{A backlog is not a payment.} The backlog benchmark is addressed in Section~\ref{sec:a16z} (item 4) and Section~\ref{sec:observe}: a backlog is a contract, not a payment; the three CSP backlogs include non-AI commitments; Microsoft's includes OpenAI's multi-year Azure commitment and Oracle's is over half OpenAI; and the note itself assumes no further backlog growth.

Converting 59\% of today's backlog over 2028--30 requires the counterparties to pay, which is the revenue-quality question of Section~\ref{sec:demand}, not a different answer to it.

\lead{Ramp and utilization.} The note's 60/80/85\% is the share of built capacity that is operational, a cohort ramp; the paper's energization lag and grid cap play the same role, and its inference utilization (40--50\% of nominal, Section~\ref{sec:commodity}) is a different quantity --- how hard operational capacity is run. The two are multiplicative, and the note's revenue per GW is stated at 100\% utilization of operational capacity.

\lead{Gigawatts.} The note's semiconductor build implies 57 GW of AI deployments in 2028 worldwide. The paper's grid energization cap is 22--40 GW/yr. One of the two is wrong, or 2028's silicon will be bought before it can be powered; the register records the note's figure as the high anchor on the cap and the 2028 deployment number as an observable to test in the Q4 2027 and 2028 utility filings.

\lead{Fungibility.} The note reports the operators' framing: Microsoft's ``fungible fleet,'' Amazon's modular retrofits, Alphabet shifting capacity between internal and external workloads. The paper does not dispute fungibility \emph{within} AI workloads --- training to inference, between geographies --- which is exactly what those statements claim. The dual-use fallacy of Section~\ref{sec:supply} is about AI halls hosting non-AI cloud, which none of the statements claim.

\lead{What the register takes.} Five observables (Table~\ref{tab:observe}): the Phase-2 requirement of \$1.42T and \$11.6B/GW-yr at 15\% ROIC; the \$42.4B/GW cost and its \$24.1/12.6/5.4B split; Epoch's \$836M/GW-yr operating cost; the 59\%-of-backlog conversion the three CSPs would need; and the 20/35/57 GW deployment path. One flagged input ($c_{\text{opex}}$, two anchors) and one high anchor (the grid cap).

\lead{Management claims, graded.} The note's management claims --- Amazon's 3-year server breakeven, Oracle's high-20s ROIC, SpaceX's one-year payback --- are recorded as grade-J (judgment) statements by interested parties, to be tested when segment disclosures permit. The note and the paper disagree on nothing arithmetical; they disagree on whether the cohorts after this one, and the ones before it, belong in the sum. The paper's answer is that a balance sheet does not get to choose.

\subsection{Four arguments from the first review round, not yet in the model}\label{sec:firstround}
\claim{Four mechanisms from the first review that are not yet in the model, kept so they are not lost.}

The first circulated draft was built in a long exchange whose working notes contained several mechanisms that did not survive into the paper's text, mostly because they lacked a source at the time. They are listed here so they are not lost, each with what it would change and what would put it into the register.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Take-or-pay power.} The paper's facility opex scales with load. A growing share of hyperscale power is contracted under long-dated take-or-pay power purchase agreements --- Microsoft's 20-year contract for the restarted Three Mile Island unit, Amazon's Susquehanna arrangement, Meta's 20-year Clinton contract~\cite{ref:ppa_press}\verify{} --- under which the payment is owed whether or not the capacity runs.

For the contracted share, power moves from opex into carry: a fixed liability that does not fall when utilization does. At 2\% of gross for power, a 30--50\% contracted share converts \$10--25B/yr of 2028 opex into a fixed charge. \emph{Register:} add a contracted-power share when a sourced figure exists; the effect is on the \emph{shape} of the downside (the carry no longer flexes down in a demand shortfall), not on the base case.
\item \textbf{The sovereign backstop.} If the 2030 plateau deficit is $\sim$\$420B against a carry of $\sim$\$890B, a state that wished to keep the stock running --- for defense, sovereignty or employment reasons --- would need to absorb nearly half of the carry. That is the scale of the implicit guarantee behind ``AI is strategic,'' and it is not small: more than a year of US federal R\&D. \emph{Register:} a what-if, not a variable; it is the size of the public-money question if the private one is answered the way this paper expects.
\item \textbf{Agentic loops as the Jevons mechanism.} The 600$\times$ growth in tokens on OpenRouter (Section~\ref{sec:a16z}) is mostly agents calling models many times per task. Tokens per task rising 10--100$\times$ as work moves from chat to agents is the mechanism by which Jevons could keep hyperscaler receipts growing after the per-token price collapses to the floor. \emph{Register:} already inside the residual adoption/capability factor; the observable is tokens per paid seat, which no operator yet discloses.
\item \textbf{Outcome-based procurement.} a16z's labor-budget argument (Section~\ref{sec:a16z}, item 7) has arithmetic: 2\% of the \$30T global knowledge payroll is \$600B/yr, three times the paper's 2030 IT-budget feasibility band. The paper's slack and leakage sections say why that 2\% is hard to extract and why, when it is, 10--50:1 of it stays with buyers and integrators. \emph{Register:} the feasibility band's upper edge is where this argument lives; it is already the axis of Figure~\ref{fig:probmap}.
\end{enumerate}
Two further notes from the same exchange --- a three-bloc comparison of five-year cost per megawatt (roughly \$42--50M in Europe, \$32--38M in the US, \$14--18M in China's western corridors) and a sketch of rest-of-world demand (India, ASEAN, Africa, Latin America) --- were derived without sources and are recorded here only as questions for the register\verify.

\section{Results: Break-Even Growth}\label{sec:results}
\claim{Revenue must grow about 57\% a year to break even by 2030; no growth rate rescues 2027--29.}

\inbrief{\item Counted sector by sector, receipts fall about \$420B a year short of the carry of all builders in 2030, five and a half standard deviations from break-even.\item AI can disrupt software and still fail to pay for its capital: the buyer's dollar moves, but only a fraction reaches the cloud.\item With a 35\% spending cut, the chance of profit is about 7\% in 2030 and 58\% by 2032.}

Each run draws $N=100{,}000$ samples of the parameters in Sections~\ref{sec:supply}--\ref{sec:demand} and computes the annual surplus or deficit $\Delta_t=D_t-H_t$ (positive is a surplus) for 2027--2030. Parameters are independent; since capex, energization and demand expectations are positively correlated in reality, the spread is understated and tail probabilities are indicative.

\subsection{Variance budget, undecomposed and decomposed}
\claim{Breaking the inputs down cuts the uncertainty from 286 to 104, and the sign survives.}

Equation~\ref{eq:var} in practice: Table~\ref{tab:varbudget} apportions the one-at-a-time swings into shares of total variance (half-swing squared over the sum), with the inputs treated first as single judgment ranges and then as the decompositions of Section~\ref{sec:decomp}. Undecomposed, two inputs --- the 2026 base and the cloud share --- would carry 82\% of the top-down uncertainty; decomposed, the root-sum-square is 104. What is left is the fleet-mix silicon life on the supply side and, on the demand side, the base and the cloud share, both already reduced to public components; their remaining spread is the labs' second-half 2026 bookings and Anthropic's undisclosed margin, which the next two quarters of filings will resolve.

\lead{The bottom-up variance.} On the bottom-up side, decomposing the advertising ceiling takes its contribution from half the sector variance to a tenth, leaving assistants and the common factor. The root-sum-square is less than half the linear sum throughout --- the quadrature discount --- and the tight factors contribute almost nothing even though there are many of them.

\subsection{Reconciling the two estimates}
\claim{The two estimates disagree by about \$255B of end-customer revenue in 2030; the gap in receipts against the carry is three and a half standard deviations.}

The top-down sweep says the plateau stock needs 57\%/yr sustained through 2030 (about \$1.09T of end-customer revenue; the siting friction defers carry but adds cost, and the two nearly cancel by 2030). The bottom-up chains deliver 33\% CAGR (about \$625B), with year-on-year growth falling from 57\% to 14\% as the large sectors saturate.

The two methods share only the 2026 base; the ceilings, ramps and capture shares are independent judgments, and the gap between them --- roughly \$480B of end-customer revenue or \$420B of infrastructure receipts in 2030 --- is several times the root-sum-square half-swing of the bottom-up estimate (Table~\ref{tab:varbudget}); expressed as a $z$-score of the difference, $\Delta_{2030}/\sigma_{\Delta}=-422/76\approx-5.6$ (Appendix~\ref{app:errors}).

\lead{A robust sign.} The Fermi standard is not certainty, but a gap larger than the propagated noise. Figure~\ref{fig:sector_tornado} shows that no single sector ceiling closes it; advertising uplift at its decomposed maximum (\$170B) no longer can, even with assistants at theirs (\$230B); Figure~\ref{fig:whatif2} maps the pair: no combination of the two ceilings within the plotted range closes the 2030 gap on its own, because ramps and capture shares bind as well. The concrete bull case is therefore not ``bigger ceilings'' but ``faster ramps and higher cloud capture at once'' --- the residual factor of Figure~\ref{fig:whatif1}.

\subsection{Can both be true? Cannibalization and the ``internally inconsistent'' objection}
\claim{Yes: AI can disrupt software and still fail to pay for its capital --- the buyer's dollar moves, but only a fraction reaches the cloud.}

Bank of America's note on the 2026 software repricing put the objection plainly: investors cannot believe both that AI capital expenditure fails to earn its return and that AI disrupts the whole software industry~\cite{ref:saaspocalypse_nx}. The paper's answer is that the two are one claim, and the mechanism is cannibalization. AI does not add a new spending pool to the enterprise budget; it enters an existing one. Three cases span the possibilities.

\lead{Additive or substitutive?} If AI spend is \emph{additive}, total software-plus-AI spending equals the old software pool plus the AI pool, and the incumbents keep theirs. If it is \emph{substitutive}, every AI dollar replaces a software dollar and the total is unchanged. The evidence points between: clients do pay more for software with AI in it --- Microsoft's July price increases are the proof --- but they buy fewer seats, and the seats they drop were the incumbents' highest-margin revenue.

\lead{Partly additive.} The total rises, but by less than the two pools added together: the feasibility band of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} is built exactly this way, with a reallocation share (12--32\% of the pool moving from seats and services to AI solutions) plus a small genuinely new component (2--7\%).

So AI revenue can reach $\sim$\$1T by 2030 while the software industry shrinks, because most of that trillion is the software industry's own money changing hands --- from seat-priced incumbents to labs, integrators and on-device silicon --- and only a minority of it reaches the infrastructure meter.

\lead{One flow, two consequences.} Both halves of the objection are consequences of the same flow: the buyer's dollar moves, the incumbent loses it, the infrastructure owner sees a fraction. There is no inconsistency; there is a leak, and the leak is the paper.

\subsection{The overlap gauge}\label{sec:overlap}
\claim{The chance that receipts exceed the carry, mapped over the two things the paper cannot pin down.}

\inbrief{\item The right question is not the sign but the probability that receipts exceed the carry.\item On a flat path that needs 2030 AI revenue near \$1.18T --- near the top of the feasible band, and only if the sector chains are about 90\% too low.\item With a 35\% spending cut the chance of profit is about 7\% in 2030 and 58\% by 2032.\item The map shows the result as a probability, not a prediction of loss.}

The question a Fermi analysis should end on is not ``what is the sign'' but ``with what probability can the two distributions overlap, and inside what feasible region.'' Figure~\ref{fig:overlap} overlays the bottom-up receipts and carry distributions year by year; Table~\ref{tab:pprofit} states P(receipts $>$ carry) for every path and year; and Figure~\ref{fig:probmap} maps that probability over the two residual axes with their feasibility cutoffs drawn in.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{f17_probmap.pdf}}
\caption{Probability of profit in 2030 over the two residual axes, by capex path. Solid contour: 50\%. Dashed line: the IT-budget hard cap on the assistant ceiling ($\sim$\$270B). Dotted curves bound the feasibility band: total 2030 AI revenue between \$920B and \$1.31T, the P10--P90 of the decomposed budget cutoff (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}); to the right of the band the demand cannot be financed from enterprise, advertising and consumer budgets. The dot is the paper's modes.}\label{fig:probmap}\end{figure}

\lead{The map in one picture.} The feasibility band is what keeps it honest. A single line at 8\% of total IT spend ($\sim$\$810B) would put the plateau's 50\% contour outside the feasible region; decomposing the cutoff into Gartner's software and services budgets, their growth, and an AI share (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}) gives a band of \$920B--\$1.31T --- almost unchanged in total even though the pool it comes from is being torn up, because the money moves from seats to solutions rather than disappearing (Section~\ref{sec:decomp}) --- and the plateau's 50\% contour --- residual factor $\sim$1.9, 2030 AI revenue $\sim$\$1.18T --- lies near its top: the carry of every builder can be financed only if almost everything goes right.

\lead{The honest statement.} On a plateau, the installed base of every builder is more likely than not to earn its 2030 carry only if the sector chains are $\sim$90\% too conservative in aggregate, which is financeable in only about a third of budget draws (the cutoff's P50 is $\sim$\$1.10T) and requires AI to reach about a third of an enterprise software and services pool that is no longer growing, up from $\sim$5\% in 2026 (Gartner's \$64B of AI platform and model spend against \$3.3T~\cite{ref:gartner_ai_platforms,ref:gartner_it_jul2026}) --- money that comes out of seat-priced software and consulting hours.

\lead{Other spending paths.} Under an arms race the contour is off the chart. Under rationalization the feasible and profitable regions overlap, but late: P(profit) is $\sim$7\% at the paper's modes in 2030 and $\sim$58\% in 2032; siting friction pulls it down because deferred cohorts and site costs land in 2029--30. The zeros in Table~\ref{tab:pprofit} for 2027--28 are not a modeling artifact: the 2025--27 cohorts are on the books and demand cannot double fast enough to meet them under any path.

\lead{Paid from labor budgets?} Three questions the feasibility band raises. The first is the deepest: a16z's \textit{State of Markets} argues that AI's addressable market is ``considerably larger than IT budgets'' because AI is paid from labor budgets~\cite{ref:a16z_som} --- the \$30T payroll, not the \$3.3T software-and-services pool.

If so, the band is wrong by a factor of several and the plateau pays easily. The paper keeps the IT-budget band because Section~\ref{sec:slack} finds that only about a quarter of nominal labor savings reach cash, and over a J-curve; but this is the disagreement on which the bull case turns, and the register cannot settle it from 2026 data.

\lead{Additional or displacing?} Two further questions the band raises.
\begin{itemize}
\item First, is AI spending \emph{additional} to IT budgets or does it \emph{displace} other software and services? Gartner's 2026 forecast has total IT spending growing 14\%, most of it AI-driven, so the near-term answer is mostly additional; but the band's AI share is a share of the whole software-and-services pool, so if AI displaces seat-based software and consulting hours one-for-one the cap on AI is the same and the hyperscalers' non-AI cloud revenue shrinks instead --- Microsoft can gain AI revenue while losing on conventional Office.
\item Second, AI's own efficiency will shrink the services budget it draws on: if AI removes \$200--400B of the \$1.87T services pool by 2030, the band's upper edge falls by roughly that share of the AI cut. Both effects narrow the feasible region; neither is in the base case, which is therefore generous to the build-out on this point.
\end{itemize} \textbf{No growth rate clears 2027--28.} No plausible $g$ clears 2027 or 2028: the 2025--27 cohorts are a sunk deficit of \$200--300B/yr. Under Plateau, $g\approx57\%$ sustained for four years breaks even in 2030 alone and $\sim$74\% recovers the 2027--29 hole cumulatively. The tornado (Figure~\ref{fig:tornado}) says what a Fermi analyst wants to know: the 2026 revenue base and the infrastructure share dominate everything on the supply side; silicon life, opex and the non-AI baseline are second-order; cost of capital and shell life barely register.

 The distribution at $g=57\%$ (Figure~\ref{fig:hist}) is centered near zero --- the coin-toss neighborhood by construction. The bottom-up estimate says the sectors do not reach that neighborhood.

\noindent\lead{Where the data stand.} Frontier-lab run-rates rose roughly 3.5$\times$ (Anthropic $\sim$7$\times$, OpenAI $\sim$2$\times$) between end-2025 and August 2026~\cite{ref:techcrunch_anthropic,ref:axios_anthropic,ref:epoch_revenue}. Both companies have told investors to expect slower growth in 2026 than trend --- roughly 2.2$\times$ for OpenAI and up to 4$\times$ for Anthropic~\cite{ref:epoch_revenue_x}.

Even those guided rates exceed $g^{*}$. The labs' figures are the only high-growth disclosures available; Microsoft's consumer assistant has by most accounts disappointed, and Amazon's and Meta's AI revenue is not separately reported, so ``ahead of the threshold'' rests on two companies.

\lead{The paper's position.} The build-out pays if the labs' growth halves and then halves again but stays above $\sim$50\% through 2030. The fifteen sector chains put it nearer 14\% by 2030 because seats, IT budgets and advertising yields run out before compute does; Sections~\ref{sec:slack}--\ref{sec:lastmile} are the mechanisms behind those ceilings; and Section~\ref{sec:falsify} says what would move the distribution.

\claim{The GPU renters and their lenders are most exposed; buyers, integrators and own-chip clouds gain.}

\section{Timeline}
\claim{If the paper is right: slowing lab growth and write-downs in 2027--28, a spending retreat in 2028--29, a cheaper equilibrium after.}

\inbrief{\item 2026--27: startup failures, scrutiny of investor-funded cloud revenue, a first wave of write-downs.\item 2028--29: spending retreats as finance chiefs cut budgets.\item After 2030: a cheaper, more spread-out equilibrium --- not the end of AI.}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{2026--27:} startup-cohort failure crosses $\sim$80\%; SEC and auditor scrutiny of related-party cloud revenue; first \$50B+ impairment wave across hyperscalers and neo-clouds; secondary GPU spot prices fall by half or more.
    \item \textbf{2028--29:} human-text data wall binds for frontier training~\cite{ref:epoch_data2024}; CFOs cut experimental AI budgets; Big-5 capex retreats 30\%+ from the 2026--27 peak; neo-cloud restructurings; OCI renegotiates unfulfilled contracts.
    \item \textbf{2030:} 100+ TOPS local silicon standard; open-weight small models the enterprise default; frontier intelligence a low-margin utility; software margin concentrated in domain-specific integrators.
\end{itemize}

\section{What Would Change the Distribution}\label{sec:falsify}
\claim{Two observations would overturn the result on their own --- above all, 2028 AI revenue above \$400B.}

\inbrief{\item AI revenue above \$400B in calendar 2028 would overturn the most likely outcome.\item Interim marker: 2027 revenue near \$330B is the paper's path; above \$350B favours the build-out.\item Paid use of office assistants is the sector to watch.}

The paper's result is a distribution, not a yes or no, so the tests below are stated as observations that would move the distribution decisively rather than as binary refutations. Two are sufficient on their own:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Revenue.} If calendar-2028 end-customer AI revenue across all channels exceeds \$400B --- i.e.\ $g\ge50\%$ has held for two more years from the 2026 base, measured on the same seven-component basis as Table~\ref{tab:basedecomp} --- the plateau path is on track and the paper's most likely outcome was wrong.

    Interim markers: calendar-2027 revenue near \$330B is the bottom-up path; above \$350B favors the build-out; the sector to watch is office assistants and agents, where the bottom-up ceiling (\$150B) and the IT-budget cap (\$270B) leave the most room to differ.
    \item \textbf{Impairment and retreat.} If by end-2030 cumulative AI-asset impairments across hyperscalers and neo-clouds under ASC 360-10 are below \$100B \emph{and} aggregate Big-5 capex has not fallen 30\% from its peak, the paper's most likely outcome was wrong.
\end{enumerate}
Other observables will move the distribution as they arrive (Appendix~\ref{app:update} gives the calendar and the re-issue rule), in rough order of leverage: the labs' calendar-2026 bookings and gross margins (the base and $\theta$); Microsoft's next paid-seat disclosure (the assistant ceiling); Data Center Watch's quarterly count (the deferral fraction); the operators' year-end useful-life notes (silicon life); Gartner's 2027 AI-share revision (the feasibility band).

Softer expectations that do not decide the case, because their attribution is weak: BLS professional-services employment shows under 1.5\% net AI-attributable displacement; metered frontier API prices fall more than 80\% from 2026 while integrator revenue grows.

\lead{If the paper is right: the sequence.} 2027--28, lab growth decelerates below 2$\times$, the 2024--25 startup cohort thins, auditors scrutinize investor-funded compute contracts, first \$50B+ impairment wave; 2028--29, CFOs cut experimental budgets, capex retreats toward the \$450--550B the realized $g$ can carry, neo-cloud restructurings; 2030, 100+ TOPS local silicon is standard, open-weight small models the enterprise default, software margin with domain integrators.

\section{Model Specification}
All parameters triangular Triang(min, mode, max); \$B.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Capex history $I_{2023..2026}=161, 260, 448, 780$~\cite{ref:epoch_capex,ref:mlq2026}; forward paths Plateau (780 flat), Arms race (1000 then 1100), Rationalize (550 then 500), each held flat to 2035; and an Adaptive path in which next year's capex scales with last year's ratio of receipts to carry, damped (exponent 0.5) and bounded to \$400B--\$1.1T (Appendix~\ref{app:horizon}).

    No crystal ball chooses among the first three: the plateau is the base because it is the operators' stated 2026 level held constant; the arms race is their 2027 trajectory continued; the cut is what an impairment test would force.

    The adaptive path is what a rational operator would do with the same information the model has. Non-AI baseline $N_c=(161-A_{2023})(1+g_N)^{c-2023}$, $A_{2023}\sim$Triang(20, 30, 40), $g_N\sim$Triang(0.02, 0.06, 0.10). Grid energization cap Triang(1150, 1400, 1680) \$B/yr; siting deferral $f_{\text{delay}}\sim$Triang(0.10, 0.20, 0.35) from 2026; deferred capex carries $r$ only. Shell cost uplift $\kappa_{\text{site}}\sim$Triang(1.02, 1.05, 1.09); opex uplift $u\sim$Triang(0, 0.03, 0.06).
    \item Cohorts: $\phi\sim$Triang(0.45, 0.52, 0.60); silicon life $=1/(f_{\text{tr}}/L_{\text{tr}}+(1-f_{\text{tr}})/L_{\text{inf}})$ with $f_{\text{tr}}\sim$Triang(0.35, 0.45, 0.55), $L_{\text{tr}}\sim$Triang(2.5, 3.5, 4.5), $L_{\text{inf}}\sim$Triang(4, 5, 6); $L_{\text{sh}}\sim$Triang(15, 20, 25); $\ell_{\text{si}}\sim$Triang(0.5, 0.7, 0.9), $\ell_{\text{sh}}\sim$Triang(0.2, 0.4, 0.6); straight-line, mid-year.
    \item Carry: $c_{\text{opex}}\sim$Triang(0.059, 0.0665, 0.074), decomposed; $r\sim$Triang(0.090, 0.0945, 0.099), CAPM; $H_t$ per Eq.~\ref{eq:Ht}.
    \item Demand: $D_{2026}\sim$Triang(161, 179, 198) and $\theta\sim$Triang(0.77, 0.815, 0.86), both from the decompositions of Section~\ref{sec:decomp}; $D_t=\theta D_{2026}(1+g)^{t-2026}$, $g$ swept.
    \item Bottom-up demand: fifteen sectors per Table~\ref{tab:sectors}; logistic ramps; common factors on ceilings Triang(0.7, 1.0, 1.4) and ramps Triang(0.7, 1.0, 1.3); circular deduction Triang(8, 12, 18); $\gamma_{\text{sov}}$ applied to sovereign-exposed sectors.
    \item Secondary variables added at v25: economic depreciation rate $\delta\sim$Triang(0.5, 0.7, 0.9); inference utilization Triang(0.40, 0.45, 0.50) with a doubling case; token price index and marginal cash cost per million tokens (Section~\ref{sec:commodity}).
    \item Limitations: top-down parameters independent (understates spread); no demand saturation (favors the bull at high $g$); no SPV debt-service cliffs (favors the bull); fixed capex paths except the adaptive path of Appendix~\ref{app:horizon}; investor-funded lab compute counted as receipts.
\end{itemize}


\section{Beyond 2030: The Horizon to 2035 and a Rational Operator}\label{app:horizon}
Ending at 2030 invites the objection that the deficit is a timing artifact: the stock is built ahead of demand, and by 2032 the demand has arrived. This appendix extends the same model to 2035. After 2030 the sector ceilings grow with the budgets that set them --- IT spending, advertising and users --- at a rate $g_C\sim$Triang(6, 10, 14)\%/yr, and the logistic ramps continue; capex paths are held at their 2028--30 levels.

A fourth, \emph{adaptive}, path lets capex respond: each year's capex is last year's scaled by the ratio of receipts to carry, damped by a square root and bounded to \$400B--\$1.1T --- the behavior of an operator who reads the same numbers this paper does and neither ignores nor panics at them.

\caption{Annual and cumulative surplus or deficit to 2035 by capex path (medians). A plateau's annual deficit narrows only to about \$265B by 2035 and never turns to profit; the adaptive path is profitable from 2032 but is still about \$140B short cumulatively in 2035.}\label{fig:horizon}\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\figbox{\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{f20_adaptive.pdf}}
\caption{The adaptive path. Big-5 capex falls to $\sim$\$400B by 2030--32 as receipts lag carry (the other builders scale with it), then re-expands as receipts overtake it; the stock is right-sized to demand with a two-year lag.}\label{fig:adaptive}\end{figure}

Three readings.
\begin{itemize}
\item First, the plateau does not recover by 2035: infrastructure receipts grow from \$480B to \$910B, but the plateau's carry grows too --- replacement of short-lived silicon plus the shell cohorts still depreciating --- from \$890B to \$1.16T, so the annual gap is still about \$265B at the end of the horizon and the cumulative deficit of $\sim$\$3T is not repaid. ``By 2035 it all comes back'' is true for the technology and false for the plateau balance sheet.
\item Second, the arms race never closes.
\item Third, a 35\% cut or an adaptive operator turns profitable in 2032 and is close to even cumulatively by 2035 ($-$\$140--190B before the cost of money, $-$\$700--800B with it), at a stock that has fallen to roughly \$400--500B/yr of Big-5 capex --- close to what Section~\ref{sec:results} identified as the level realized demand can carry.
\end{itemize}

The adaptive path also answers a question the fixed paths cannot: how does the industry stay on the rails? By letting the operators' own recoverability tests do their work a year at a time, rather than by any single actor's failure.

\paragraph{Inflation over the longer horizon.} The further out the horizon, the more inflation matters, so it is stated explicitly. Receipts are in today's prices: no inflation uplift is given to revenue, because contracts are fixed in dollars and AI unit prices are falling. The carry charges a nominal 9.45\% on the money, which already contains expected inflation, so the annual and cumulative figures in Table~\ref{tab:horizon} stand as they are, and so does the finding that no path is repaid with interest by 2035. What inflation changes is how much the owners keep: in today's money the plateau's cumulative hole with interest is about \$3.5T in 2035 rather than \$4.5T, and its average return is about $-$6.5\% a year rather than $-$4\% --- smaller dollars, a larger loss of purchasing power. One effect would make the result worse and is left out of the base for lack of a source: power, labor and site costs rising with prices while fixed-price contracts do not ($\sim$\$50B a year by 2035 on the plateau). The step-by-step reasoning is in ``How long investors wait'' (p.~\pageref{ag:cum}); the formulas, term by term, in Appendix~\ref{app:equations}.

Growth $g$ is in this sense not an exogenous unknown but the outcome of decisions the operators will make with the same numbers, which is why the paper reports distributions and thresholds rather than a forecast.

\section{Sector Ceiling Derivations}\label{app:sectors}
Each ceiling is a Fermi chain to 2030, as first derived. Where Section~\ref{sec:decomp} later decomposed a ceiling further (assistants, advertising, consumer), the decomposed range in Table~\ref{tab:sectors} supersedes the chain below.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Office assistants / agents.} 350M knowledge-worker seats $\times$ 35--55\% adoption $\times$ \$25--35/seat/month $\times$ 12 $=$ \$37--83B of seat revenue; agentic and workflow consumption at 1.5--3$\times$ seat revenue $=$ \$55--250B; ceiling capped by an AI share of 3--5\% of $\sim$\$5.5T global IT spend $=$ \$165--275B. Mode \$200B. $\theta=0.6$: routine tasks migrate to on-device NPUs and private small models (Section~\ref{sec:bottomup}).
    \item \textbf{Advertising uplift.} Meta and Google advertising $\sim$\$500B in 2026; AI ranking, creative and measurement lift yield by 10--20\% $=$ \$50--100B; AI-native conversational and generative formats \$30--150B. Mode \$150B. $\theta=1.0$: the infrastructure owner is the advertiser's platform.
    \item \textbf{Consumer subscriptions.} $\sim$1B monthly users $\times$ 5--10\% paid $\times$ \$20--25/month $\times$ 12 $=$ \$12--30B; free-tier advertising at \$30--80/user-year on 300--600M ad-eligible users $=$ \$10--50B; premium tiers and devices remainder. Mode \$80B.
    \item \textbf{Coding tools.} 29M developers~\cite{ref:octoverse} $\times$ 50--70\% paid $\times$ (\$20--200/month seat + \$1--3k/yr agent tokens) $=$ \$35--95B. Mode \$60B. Non-recurring: refactoring impulse $\alpha e^{-\lambda t}$ with maintenance tail $\beta\sim$Triang(0.05, 0.08, 0.12) of build tokens.
    \item \textbf{Search \& internal use.} Google search $\sim$\$250B; AI overviews and modes defend share rather than expand it; incremental monetizable AI search 5--15\% $=$ \$12--38B; hyperscaler internal productivity, fraud and recommendation uplift \$15--40B. Mode \$50B.
    \item \textbf{Financial services.} Banking and insurance IT and operations $\sim$\$1.5T; AI share 2--4\% $=$ \$30--60B, constrained by model-risk governance (SR~11-7, FINRA) and on-premise deployment ($\theta=0.75$). Mode \$40B.
    \item \textbf{Customer service / CX.} \$350B contact-center labor; 10--15\% displaced by 2030 $=$ \$35--55B of savings; vendors and integrators price at 50--80\% of savings $=$ \$25--45B. $\theta=0.5$: speech and RAG run on small models; 10--35:1 token ratio.
    \item \textbf{Public sector / defense.} Government IT $\sim$\$700B; AI share 4--8\% $=$ \$28--56B. Sovereign carve-out $\gamma$ applies.
    \item \textbf{Healthcare / clinical.} 1.1M US physicians $\times$ 60--80\% ambient-scribe adoption $\times$ \$400--600/month $=$ \$3--6B; global multiple 2--3$\times$; prior-authorization, coding, imaging triage \$10--25B. Mode \$35B. Market reports place clinical documentation at \$5B in 2026~\cite{ref:healthcare_rm}.
    \item \textbf{Autonomous fleets.} Autonomous-driving and fleet software forecasts of \$8B (2026) to \$32B (early 2030s)~\cite{ref:av_fbi}; 5--7 year chassis cycles; most inference on-vehicle ($\theta=0.45$).
    \item \textbf{Generative video / gaming / media.} Cloud GPU cost $\sim$\$0.30/user-hour against consumer willingness to pay; live generative content 100--300M users $\times$ \$5--10/month $=$ \$6--36B~\cite{ref:newzoo}.
    \item \textbf{Legal \& compliance.} \$950B legal services; software 2--3\% $=$ \$19--28B; verification rules and hallucination liability cap adoption. Mode \$20B.
    \item \textbf{Science / R\&D.} Pharma R\&D $\sim$\$300B $\times$ 4--8\% $=$ \$12--24B; materials, climate, engineering simulation \$5--20B. Mode \$25B.
    \item \textbf{Factory / robotics software.} Operational robot stock $\sim$4--5M~\cite{ref:ifr} $\times$ \$2--4k/yr software $=$ \$8--20B; bounded by Asian wage floors (\$1.05--1.60/hr) below robot TCO ($\sim$\$2.9/hr); inference at the edge ($\theta=0.45$).
    \item \textbf{Eldercare / assistive.} 160M OECD seniors $\times$ 15--25\% smart-home penetration $\times$ \$100--200/yr $=$ \$2--8B; 85\% on-device for privacy ($\theta=0.15$). 90\% of eldercare cost is physical labor software cannot displace.
    \item \textbf{Edge siphon (cross-cutting).} $350\text{M}\times15\times250\times500=6.6\times10^{14}$ routine tokens/yr; at \$0.10--0.50/M $=$ \$66--330B of potential cloud revenue; 60--85\% executed locally $\Rightarrow$ \$40--280B/yr never billed. This is the physical basis for $\theta<1$ in the office assistant, CX, robotics and eldercare rows.
    \item \textbf{Spatial video tokens (embodied AI).} $4\text{ cameras}\times30\text{ fps}\times3600\text{ s}\times62.5\text{ tokens/frame}=2.7\times10^{7}$ tokens per camera-hour, $\sim$5.4$\times10^{8}$ per robot per 20-hour day; favors \$0.02--0.035/kWh corridors over cheap chips.
\end{itemize}


\section{Error Propagation Across Chains}\label{app:errors}
The paper combines estimates in three ways, and each has its own propagation rule. All three are checked below against the Monte Carlo, which does not assume any of them.

\subsection{Rule 1: within a chain, factors multiply, log-variances add}
A single chain is a product, $Y=\prod_i X_i$. With independent factors,
\begin{equation}
\sigma^{2}_{\ln Y}=\sum_i\sigma^{2}_{\ln X_i},
\end{equation}
so \emph{relative} errors add in quadrature and the loose factors dominate. For a triangular input with min $a$ and max $c$, $\sigma_{\ln X}\approx\ln(c/a)/4.9$. In this paper every input is tighter than a factor of two: the supply-side inputs have $\sigma_{\ln}$ between 0.06 (cost of capital) and 0.17 (energization lag); the sector ceilings have 0.15--0.25. The chain is where individual expertise enters, and where a referee should attack one factor at a time.

\subsection{Rule 2: across chains that are summed, absolute variances add --- plus the common factor}
Sectors are added, not multiplied: $D=\sum_s R_s$. For independent sectors,
\begin{equation}
\sigma^{2}_{D}=\sum_s\sigma^{2}_{R_s}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\sigma^{2}_{\ln D}\approx\sum_s w_s^{2}\,\sigma^{2}_{\ln R_s},\quad w_s=\frac{R_s}{D},
\label{eq:sumrule}
\end{equation}
so the relative error of the sum is the \emph{share-weighted} root-sum-square of the sector relative errors. Because the shares $w_s$ are squared, a sector with 5\% of the total contributes 1/25 as much variance as one with 25\%, even at equal relative uncertainty; and because there are many sectors, the idiosyncratic error of the sum is far smaller than that of any one sector. This is the averaging that rewards detail.

What does not average is anything the sectors share. If a common factor $M$ (the $M_C$ of Section~\ref{sec:bottomup}) multiplies every ceiling --- macro adoption, a recession, a regulatory shock --- then $D=M\sum_s R_s'$ and
\begin{equation}
\sigma^{2}_{\ln D}\approx\sigma^{2}_{\ln M}+\sum_s w_s^{2}\,\sigma^{2}_{\ln R_s'}.
\label{eq:common}
\end{equation}
The common factor adds its full variance once, unweighted, and cannot be diversified away. In practice it dominates: the residual uncertainty of a well-decomposed demand estimate is almost entirely the shared factor, not the sector details.

\subsection{Rule 3: supply minus demand, absolute variances add}
The result is a difference, $\Delta=\text{Receipts}-H$. Supply and demand share no parameters in this model, so
\begin{equation}
\sigma^{2}_{\Delta}=\sigma^{2}_{\text{Receipts}}+\sigma^{2}_{H}\qquad(\text{plus }-2\,\text{Cov if correlated}),
\end{equation}
and the strength of the conclusion is the $z$-score $\Delta/\sigma_{\Delta}$. Two things follow. First, because the uncertainties add rather than the medians, a gap must exceed roughly $2\sigma_{\Delta}$ before the sign is a Fermi-robust result. Second, if capex and demand were positively correlated in reality (nobody spends into flat demand), the covariance term would \emph{reduce} $\sigma_{\Delta}$ and sharpen the sign; we do not take that credit.

\subsection{Numerical check, 2030, Plateau}

\centering\small

\label{tab:propcheck}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}

\midrule

\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Rule 2 with common ceiling factor, $\sigma_{\ln M}=0.14$}} \\
$\sigma_{\ln D}=\sqrt{0.0026+0.0195}$ & 0.149 & 0.133$^{\dagger}$ \\
\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Rule 1, supply chain}} \\
$\sigma_{\ln H_{2030}}$ & --- & 0.047 \\
\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Rule 3, difference}} \\
$\sigma_{\text{Receipts}}$, $\sigma_{H}$ (\$B) & 65, 34 & 65, 34 \\
$\sigma_{\Delta}=\sqrt{65^{2}+34^{2}}$ (\$B) & 73 & 73 (Cov $\approx-4$) \\
Median $\Delta_{2030}$ (\$B) & & $-259$ \\
$z=\Delta/\sigma_{\Delta}$ & & $-3.5$ \\
P($\Delta_{2030}<0$) & & 0.99 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\\[2pt]{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Slightly below prediction because the logistic has not fully reached the ceiling by 2030, so the common ceiling factor passes through at less than unit elasticity.}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Sector contributions to Eq.~\ref{eq:sumrule}, 2030 (idiosyncratic, common factor removed)}
\label{tab:sectorvar}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Sector} & \textbf{Share $w_s$} & $\sigma_{\ln R_s}$ & $w_s^{2}\sigma^{2}_{\ln R_s}$ \\
\midrule
Office assistants / agents & 0.21 & 0.16 & 0.0011 \\
Advertising uplift & 0.18 & 0.11 & 0.0004 \\
Consumer subscriptions & 0.11 & 0.16 & 0.0003 \\
Coding tools & 0.09 & 0.16 & 0.0002 \\
Search \& internal use & 0.07 & 0.18 & 0.0001 \\
Customer service / CX & 0.05 & 0.17 & 0.0001 \\
Financial services & 0.05 & 0.17 & 0.0001 \\
Remaining eight sectors & 0.01--0.04 & 0.15--0.25 & 0.0002 total \\
\midrule
Sum & 1.00 & & 0.0026 $\Rightarrow\sigma_{\ln D}=0.051$ \\
