\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The lesson of the table is the one the method section promised. Fifteen sectors, each uncertain by 11--25\%, sum to an idiosyncratic uncertainty of 5.1\%; assistants carry 40\% of that now that the advertising and consumer ceilings are decomposed. What remains --- three-quarters of the total demand variance --- is the common adoption factor at 14\%, which sector detail cannot reduce but which Section~\ref{sec:decomp} splits into an IT-budget part (5\%), a price-deflation part (8\%) and a residual capability part (10\%).

The supply side, a chain of seventeen tight inputs, is uncertain by 5\%. Subtracting, the 2030 gap of \$422B against $\sigma_{\Delta}=\$76$B is a 5.6$\sigma$ result (for the five largest builders alone, \$259B against \$73B, 3.5$\sigma$): robust by the Fermi standard, not a certainty, and its residual doubt lives in the residual capability factor and the assistant ceiling. Those are the two axes of Figure~\ref{fig:probmap}.

\section{Other Derivations}
\subsection{Carry at the modes, 2028 Plateau}
\emph{A hand check with round-number inputs from the first draft (opex 8\%, return 10.5\%, silicon life 4 years); the model's current median is \$535B for the five largest alone and \$645B with every builder (Table~\ref{tab:carry}).} AI cohorts in service: 2023: 0; 2024: 95; 2025: 283; 2026: 615; 2027: 615; 2028: 308 (half energized). Gross $\approx$1{,}915. Silicon 55\%: depreciation $1{,}053/4=263$; shell 45\%: $862/20=43$. Opex $0.08\times1{,}915=153$. Net book $\approx$1{,}280; return $0.105\times1{,}280=134$. $H_{2028}\approx590$ on these first-draft inputs.
\subsection{Enterprise benefit requirement, 2028}
$0.7\times535\approx375$; $\times(1+1.5)\approx940$; $\times1.2\approx1{,}125$. Payroll shares: $1.1/30\approx4\%$ global; $1.1/9\approx12\%$ US-alone.
\subsection{Slack dissipation}
$\tau(1-0.65)(1-0.20)=0.28\,\tau$; at $\tau=0.15$: 4.2\% of payroll.
\subsection{Contact-center ratio}
Labor \$350B; displacement 5--10\% $=$ \$17--35B; tokens \$1--2B; ratio 10--35:1.
\subsection{Break-even revenue levels}
Plateau 2030 at $g^{*}=57\%$: $179\times1.57^{4}\approx1{,}090$; at $\theta=0.815$, infrastructure receipts $\approx885$, against $H_{2030}\approx890$.

\newpage
\section{Every Equation, Term by Term}\label{app:equations}
Each numbered equation in the paper, restated and explained one symbol at a time, with units and a worked number where one helps. Symbols follow the glossary table (Appendix~\ref{app:glossary}); a few notational conventions come first.

\subsection*{Conventions}
Lower-case $\ell$ is an energization lag (a fraction); upper-case $L$ is a life in years --- they are different quantities and the letters are deliberately different. Lower-case $k_s$ is a sector's logistic ramp rate (per year); $k$ in the Arrhenius and Black equations is Boltzmann's constant --- the only place a letter is reused, and it is flagged where it occurs. $f_{\text{tr}}$ is the training share of the silicon fleet; $f_{\text{delay}}$ is the siting deferral. $M_C$ and $M_k$ are the common factors; $m$ is the enterprise benefit margin. $\omega(e)$ is an edge weight in the circular-flow graph; $W$ is the payroll pool; $w_s$ is a sector share.

Triang($a$, $b$, $c$) always means a triangular distribution with minimum $a$, mode $b$, maximum $c$. Money is in billions of US dollars per year unless stated; rates are per year; shares are fractions between 0 and 1.

\subsection*{Equation~\ref{eq:var}: how uncertainties combine in a product}
\[\sigma^{2}_{\ln Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}_{\ln X_i}.\]
The estimate, $Y$, is a quantity computed as a product of $n$ factors $X_1\cdots X_n$ (a Fermi chain). $\ln$ is the natural logarithm; taking logs turns the product into a sum, $\ln Y=\sum\ln X_i$. $\sigma_{\ln X_i}$ is the standard deviation of $\ln X_i$, which for modest uncertainties is the same as the \emph{relative} uncertainty of $X_i$ (a $\sigma_{\ln}$ of 0.10 means ``about $\pm$10\%'').

For independent factors, variances add: the relative uncertainty of the product is the square root of the sum of the squares of the factors' relative uncertainties. Worked: three factors at 0.10, 0.15 and 0.30 give $\sqrt{0.01+0.0225+0.09}=\sqrt{0.1225}=0.35$, not $0.55$; the loosest factor dominates.

\subsection*{Equation~\ref{eq:Ht}: the annual carry of the installed base}
\[H_t=\sum_{c\le t}\sum_{j\in\{\text{si},\text{sh}\}}\left[\frac{G_{c,j}}{L_j}+c_{\text{opex}}\,G_{c,j}+r\,G_{c,j}\!\left(1-\frac{t-c+\tfrac12}{L_j}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}\!\left[t-c+\tfrac12<L_j\right]+\;r\,\text{Deferred}_t.\]
The carry, $H_t$, is the yearly cost of holding the installed base in year $t$ (\$B). The outer sum runs over every cohort year $c$ up to and including $t$; the inner sum over the two components $j$ of each cohort: silicon (si) and shell (sh). $G_{c,j}$ is the in-service gross value of that component of that cohort (\$B): AI-attributable capex $A_c$ times the component's share ($\phi$ for silicon, $1-\phi$ for shell), times the energized fraction ($\ell_j$ in the spend year, 1 afterwards), times the site cost uplift $\kappa_{\text{site}}$ for shell.

Inside the bracket, three costs: \emph{depreciation} $G_{c,j}/L_j$, straight-line over the component's life $L_j$; \emph{facility opex} $c_{\text{opex}}G_{c,j}$ (times $(1+u)$ where dry cooling applies); and \emph{return on net book} $r$ times what is left undepreciated, where $t-c+\tfrac12$ is the cohort's age at mid-year and $1-\text{age}/L_j$ is the undepreciated fraction. The indicator $\mathbf{1}[\cdot]$ is 1 while the cohort is younger than its life and 0 after it has fully depreciated, so retired cohorts drop out.

The final term charges the cost of capital on capital that is deferred (by the grid cap or siting) and not yet earning. Worked, 2028 plateau at the modes: in-service AI gross about \$1.9T, of which silicon \$1.05T $/4.2$ yr $=$ \$250B of depreciation and shell \$0.86T $/20$ yr $=$ \$43B; opex $0.0665\times1.9\text{T}=\$126$B; return $0.0945\times$ net book $\sim$\$1.3T $=\$123$B; total $\approx$ \$540B, matching Table~\ref{tab:carry}.

\subsection*{Effective silicon life (harmonic mean)}
\[L_{\text{si}}=\frac{1}{f_{\text{tr}}/L_{\text{tr}}+(1-f_{\text{tr}})/L_{\text{inf}}}.\]
Because depreciation is $1/L$ per year, a fleet's average depreciation rate is the share-weighted average of $1/L$, and the effective life is its reciprocal. At $f_{\text{tr}}=0.45$, $L_{\text{tr}}=3.5$, $L_{\text{inf}}=5$: $0.45/3.5+0.55/5=0.129+0.110=0.239$, so $L_{\text{si}}=4.2$ years.

\subsection*{The top-down demand path}
\[D_t=\theta\,D_{t_0}(1+g)^{t-t_0},\qquad t_0=2026.\]
The 2026 revenue base, $D_{t_0}$, is calendar-2026 end-customer AI revenue (\$B); $g$ the assumed constant annual growth (0.57 means 57\%/yr); $(1+g)^{t-t_0}$ compounds it over $t-t_0$ years; $\theta$ converts end-customer revenue to infrastructure receipts. Worked: $179\times1.49^{4}=880$ end-customer, $\times0.815=717$ receipts in 2030. The break-even $g^{*}$ is the $g$ at which this equals $H_t$; it is found by bisection in the code.

\subsection*{The logistic sector curve}
\[R_s(t)=\frac{C_s}{1+\left(C_s/R_{s,0}-1\right)e^{-k_s(t-t_0)}}.\]
At $t=t_0$ the exponential is 1 and the expression reduces to $C_s/(C_s/R_{s,0})=R_{s,0}$: the curve starts at the 2026 value. As $t$ grows the exponential shrinks toward 0 and $R_s(t)\to C_s$: it approaches the ceiling. The bracket $(C_s/R_{s,0}-1)$ sets how far below the ceiling the sector starts; $k_s$ sets how fast the gap closes --- half the remaining distance is covered in about $\ln 2/k_s$ years. Worked, office assistants: $R_{s,0}=30$, $C_s=150$, $k_s=0.8$: in 2030, $e^{-3.2}=0.041$, bracket $=4$, denominator $=1.16$, $R_s=129$.

The sector's growth rate is the slope of this curve divided by its value, which falls from about 60\%/yr in 2027 to about 12\%/yr in 2030. Sums: $D^{\text{BU}}_t=\sum_s R_s(t)$ and $\text{Receipts}_t=\sum_s\theta_sR_s(t)$, with common factors $M_C$ multiplying every $C_s$ and $M_k$ every $k_s$, and sovereign-exposed sectors multiplied by $(1-\gamma_{\text{sov}})(1-g_{\text{EU}}\cdot\text{ramp})$.

\subsection*{The annual surplus or deficit}
\[\Delta_t=\text{Receipts}_t-F_{\text{circ}}-\kappa\,H_t.\]
Receipts less round-trip revenue $F_{\text{circ}}$ (\$B) less the carry, with $\kappa$ the sovereignty capital multiplier (1 in the base case, 1.2 in the overlay). Positive is a surplus. P(profit) is the fraction of Monte Carlo trials in which $\Delta_t>0$; the $z$-score is $\text{median}(\Delta_t)/\text{sd}(\Delta_t)$.

\subsection*{Realized enterprise benefit}
\[B_{\text{realized}}=W\,\tau\,(1-\sigma_{\text{slack}})(1-\nu_{\text{verify}})-C_{\text{severance}}.\]
The payroll pool, $W$, is the wage bill AI could act on (\$T); $\tau$ the nominal fraction of work time AI saves; $(1-\sigma_{\text{slack}})$ the fraction of that saving not absorbed as slack; $(1-\nu_{\text{verify}})$ the fraction not consumed by verification; $C_{\text{severance}}$ one-time separation costs. Worked at the modes: $\tau\times0.35\times0.80=0.28\,\tau$; with $\tau=0.15$, 4.2\% of payroll --- \$1.26T on the \$30T global pool before severance.

\subsection*{The enterprise benefit requirement}
\[B_{\text{req}}=S_{\text{ent}}(1+m),\qquad S_{\text{ent}}=H_{\text{ent}}(1+\mu_{\text{integ}}),\qquad H_{\text{ent}}=f_{\text{ent}}H_t.\]
The enterprise share, $f_{\text{ent}}$ ($\approx$0.7), is the part of the carry that enterprises must fund; $\mu_{\text{integ}}$ the integration spend per dollar of software and tokens; $m$ the required margin over cost. Worked, 2028: $0.7\times645=452$; $\times2.5=1{,}129$; $\times1.2\approx1{,}355$, i.e.\ $\sim$\$1.35T.

\subsection*{Propagation across summed chains (Appendix~\ref{app:errors})}
\[\sigma^{2}_{\ln D}\approx\sigma^{2}_{\ln M}+\sum_s w_s^{2}\,\sigma^{2}_{\ln R_s}.\]
Total demand, $D=M\sum_sR_s$, is a sum of sectors with a common multiplier $M$ ($=M_C$ here). Sector $s$'s share is $w_s=R_s/D$. The relative variance of a sum of independent terms is the share-weighted (squared) sum of their relative variances --- small sectors contribute almost nothing --- plus the common factor's variance, which enters once, unweighted, because it moves every term together. Worked: fifteen sectors at 0.11--0.25 with the shares of Table~\ref{tab:sectorvar} sum to 0.0026 ($\sigma=0.051$); adding $\sigma_{\ln M}=0.14$ gives $\sqrt{0.0026+0.0195}=0.149$.

\subsection*{Propagation across the difference}
\[\sigma^{2}_{\Delta}=\sigma^{2}_{\text{Receipts}}+\sigma^{2}_{H}-2\,\text{Cov}(\text{Receipts},H).\]
Absolute (not relative) variances add for a difference; the covariance is near zero here because supply and demand share no inputs. Worked: $\sqrt{65^2+34^2}=73$; $z=-259/73=-3.5$.

\subsection*{The incumbent's revenue ratio}
\[\frac{\text{Rev}_{2030}}{\text{Rev}_{2026}}=(1+g_{\text{seat}})^{4}\,(1-\text{EU})\,\bigl[1-\epsilon\bigl((1+p)^{4}-1\bigr)\bigr]\,(1+p)^{4}.\]
Seats fall with the agent-driven trend $(1+g_{\text{seat}})^4$, the sovereignty exit $(1-\text{EU})$, and the price-driven loss $\epsilon$ times the cumulative price rise; price rises by $(1+p)^4$. Worked at the modes: $0.97^4=0.885$; $\times0.92=0.814$; price rise $1.08^4-1=0.36$, seat loss $0.35\times0.36=0.126$, factor $0.874$; $\times1.36$: ratio $=0.814\times0.874\times1.36=0.97$ --- revenue about flat.

\subsection*{Arrhenius acceleration}
\[AF=\exp\!\left[\frac{E_a}{k}\left(\frac{1}{T_1}-\frac{1}{T_2}\right)\right].\]
The acceleration factor, $AF$, is the factor by which a temperature-driven failure rate rises from absolute temperature $T_1$ to $T_2$ (kelvin); $E_a$ the activation energy in electron-volts; $k$ Boltzmann's constant, $8.617\times10^{-5}$ eV/K. Worked: $E_a=0.7$ eV, $T_1=333$ K (60$^{\circ}$C), $T_2=343$ K (70$^{\circ}$C): $\frac{0.7}{8.617\times10^{-5}}(1/333-1/343)=8124\times8.76\times10^{-5}=0.71$; $AF=e^{0.71}=2.0$. Failure rate doubles per 10$^{\circ}$C in this range.

\subsection*{Black's equation}
\[\text{MTTF}\propto J^{-n}\,e^{E_a/kT}.\]
Mean time to failure by electromigration falls with current density $J$ raised to a power $n$ (typically about 2) and rises with the same Arrhenius exponential; high current and high temperature together shorten life.

\subsection*{Cost of capital (CAPM and WACC)}
\[r_e=r_f+\beta\,\text{ERP},\qquad r=\frac{E}{V}r_e+\frac{D}{V}r_d(1-T_{\text{tax}}).\]
Cost of equity: risk-free rate plus beta times the equity risk premium. WACC: equity weight times cost of equity plus debt weight times after-tax cost of debt. Worked: $4.3+1.2\times5.0=10.3\%$; $0.82\times10.3+0.18\times6.5\times0.79=8.45+0.92=9.4\%$.

\subsection*{Real return and amounts in today's money (v28)}
\[\rho_{\text{real}}=\frac{1+\rho}{1+\pi}-1,\qquad X^{\text{2026\$}}_t=\frac{X_t}{(1+\pi)^{t-2026}}.\]
$\rho$ is a return in dollars of each year (nominal), for example the average return to date in Table~\ref{tab:roiyear}; $\pi$ is inflation per year (register entry \texttt{inflation}, 2.0 / 2.6 / 3.7\%); $\rho_{\text{real}}$ is the same return in today's purchasing power. $X_t$ is any dollar amount in year $t$ (a cumulative deficit, a contract payment); $(1+\pi)^{t-2026}$ is the price level relative to 2026; $X^{\text{2026\$}}_t$ is the amount in 2026 dollars. Worked: $\rho=9.45\%$, $\pi=2.6\%$ gives $1.0945/1.026-1=6.7\%$; \$4.47T owed in 2035 is $4.47/1.026^{9}\approx\$3.55$T in 2026 dollars (the Monte Carlo median, \$3.50T, uses the drawn inflation). Because the divisor is positive, a sign --- and therefore a payback year --- never changes; the WACC $r$ is nominal and is not deflated again.

\subsection*{Internal rate of return}
\[\sum_{t=0}^{n}\frac{F_t}{(1+d)^{t}}=0.\]
The project's cash flows, $F_t$, are its money in and out (negative outlay at $t=0$, positive returns after); $d$ is the discount rate. The IRR is the $d$ that makes the discounted flows sum to zero. A hurdle rate is the minimum IRR a firm accepts.

\section{Every Input, Appraised}\label{app:inputs}
Fermi's discipline is to do one's best on each number and to show the work so that anyone with better information can replace it. This appendix does that for every input in the register. For each: what is actually known and from whom; the range chosen and why; how far the source can be trusted, including where a company has a reason to shade the figure; what would improve it; and, where we have a view, what we think is likely to happen.

Ranges are triangular (min / mode / max) unless stated. The register (\texttt{parameters.json}) is the place to substitute a different opinion; the rebuild script shows what it does.

A general caution that applies throughout, and a request. Corporate disclosures in this area are made by parties with a stake in the answer: hyperscalers choose accounting lives; labs choose run-rate months and revenue bases; forecasters sell the forecasts they publish; opposition groups count victories.

None of that makes a figure false or implies bad faith, and nothing below is a claim about any company's conduct; it is the ordinary observation that a reader should ask ``who benefits from this number being large (or small)?'' and weight accordingly.

Where we note an incentive, that is what it is --- an incentive, not an accusation. The request follows from it: the companies concerned could remove most of this appendix by disclosing a few numbers they already have --- paid seats and their trend, AI revenue on a stated basis, compute cost as a share of lab revenue, accelerator lives in service.

Better guidance would cost them little and would replace inference with fact in every analysis of this kind, including those of their own lenders and auditors.

\subsection*{Supply side}

\paragraph{Capex history, $I_c$ (161, 260, 448, 780).} Known well. 2023--25 are Epoch AI's reconstruction from XBRL filings (cash PP\&E plus new finance leases), which is the best available basis and reconciles with company statements. 2026 is guidance, and guidance has been revised upward all year; companies have an incentive to guide high (signals commitment to investors and to each other) and then spend it. Improve with: the October actuals and the Q4 10-K cash-flow statements; note whether finance leases are growing faster than cash capex, which moves cost off the capex line. Likely: 2026 lands within \$40B of guidance; the October calls gave the first signal on 2027: every company that gave a direction pointed up (What Is New in Version 28).

\paragraph{AI capex in 2023, $A_{2023}$ (20 / 30 / 40).} Inferred, not disclosed. Total capex was flat in 2023 while H100 purchases began; Nvidia's data-center revenue that year (\$47B) and the Big-5's share of it bound the figure. The range is honest about the inference. Improve with: any company-level statement about 2023 AI spend. Likely: near the mode; it matters mainly because it pins the baseline.

\paragraph{Non-AI baseline growth, $g_N$ (2 / 6 / 10 \%/yr).} Judgment. Fulfillment capex is cyclical, ordinary cloud refresh tracks non-AI cloud revenue ($\sim$15\%/yr) at a lower capital intensity. Improve with: Amazon's segment capex, Microsoft's and Alphabet's statements about non-AI infrastructure. Likely: 4--8\%; the low end if capital is being diverted to AI.

\paragraph{Silicon share, $\phi$ (0.45 / 0.52 / 0.60).} Anchored on Nvidia's data-center revenue against AI-attributable capex, plus custom silicon, memory and networking. The trap is dividing by total capex rather than AI capex. Companies do not disclose the split; Nvidia's share of Big-5 purchases is estimated. Improve with: any hyperscaler statement of ``servers and equipment'' versus ``land and buildings'' in the PP\&E note (Meta and Microsoft give this). Likely: the mode; rising if custom silicon displaces shell-heavy new builds, falling if power infrastructure costs keep rising.

\paragraph{Training share of fleet, $f$ (0.35 / 0.45 / 0.55).} Judgment. Frontier training clusters are a minority of installed accelerators by count and a larger share by capital. No company discloses it. Improve with: statements about inference versus training capacity (Microsoft has made them qualitatively). Likely: falling over time as inference scales.

\paragraph{Training-cluster life, $L_{\text{tr}}$ (2.5 / 3.5 / 4.5 yr).} Physics and field data, not accounting. Arrhenius acceleration, the 24/7 duty cycle, Meta's reported failure rates, and the economics of the power slot argue for the short end; A100s still rented argue for the long.

The companies' accounting lives (5--6 years) are longer than this, and the companies have an incentive to keep them long (each year added to useful life reduces reported depreciation by billions). Amazon's 2025 shortening to five years, ``due to the pace of AI,'' is the one disclosure that cuts the other way and is the most credible datum here.

Improve with: the December 10-K useful-life notes; any field reliability data; secondary-market prices for H100s. Likely: the mode; an impairment or life shortening at one operator would move the others.

\paragraph{Inference-fleet life, $L_{\text{inf}}$ (4 / 5 / 6 yr).} Bounded above by the accounting lives (a company cannot credibly claim its assets last longer than it depreciates them) and below by the training life. Same corporate incentive as above. Likely: the mode.

\paragraph{Shell life, $L_{\text{sh}}$ (15 / 20 / 25 yr).} Industry norm; low leverage on the result. Improve only if evidence emerges that AI halls are being rebuilt for new density standards faster than that.

\paragraph{Energization lags, $\ell_{\text{si}}$ (0.5 / 0.7 / 0.9), $\ell_{\text{sh}}$ (0.2 / 0.4 / 0.6).} Judgment from build times: silicon racks within one to three quarters, shell takes twelve to twenty-four months. Low leverage after decomposition. Improve with: any disclosed construction-in-progress balances (the PP\&E note shows ``construction in progress,'' which is exactly un-energized capital).

\paragraph{Facility opex share, $c_{\text{opex}}$ (5.9 / 6.65 / 7.4 \% of gross).} Decomposed from PUE, tariff, utilization, capex per MW, staffing, tax, network. The tariff and PUE are public; staffing and maintenance are judgment. Improve with: any operator's disclosed cost per MW-year; colocation providers' (Equinix, Digital Realty) unit costs are the nearest public proxy.

Likely: rising, as tariffs rise and dry cooling spreads. \emph{Second anchor (v27):} Goldman Sachs, citing Epoch AI, uses \$836M per GW-year of non-depreciation operating cost, about 2\% of capital --- the paper's power line alone.

The 4.6\% gap (staffing, property tax and insurance, network, other) is the one input on which the two supply-side frameworks differ materially (Section~\ref{sec:gs}, Table~\ref{tab:gs_reconcile}); it is worth \$85--115B/yr of carry in 2028--30. Flagged: adopting the lower anchor would be a material re-issue under Appendix~\ref{app:update} and awaits a sourced operator figure.

\paragraph{Cooling opex uplift, $u$ (0 / 3 / 6 \%).} Engineering estimate of dry versus evaporative cooling. Improve with: the fraction of new capacity in water-constrained jurisdictions. Likely: the mode, rising with siting friction.

\paragraph{WACC, $r$ (9.0 / 9.45 / 9.9 \%).} CAPM with Damodaran inputs. The one input that is honestly well known; changes with rates. Improve with: the January update.

\paragraph{Inflation, $\pi$ (2.0 / 2.6 / 3.7 \%/yr; new in v28, presentation only).} Minimum: the Federal Reserve's longer-run 2\% goal; mode: September 2026 core CPI, 2.6\%; maximum: September headline CPI, 3.7\%~\cite{ref:fed_goal,ref:bls_cpi_sep26}. Used only to restate returns and deficits in today's money. It is not applied to receipts (contracts are fixed in dollars, AI unit prices fall, ceilings use today's prices) and not to the payback test, because the WACC is already nominal --- applying it there would count inflation twice. Improve with: the monthly CPI release; escalation clauses in power and colocation contracts, which would let facility opex rise with prices in the base case.

\paragraph{Grid energization cap (1150 / 1400 / 1680 \$B/yr).} 22--40 GW/yr of connectable new AI load, globally, at \$38--55M/MW. Weakly sourced: assembled from utility and queue reports, not a single dataset. Does not bind on a plateau, so its looseness does not matter unless capex exceeds $\sim$\$1.3T.

Improve with: a consolidated GW-per-year connection figure from the grid operators. \emph{High anchor (v27):} Goldman Sachs's semiconductor build implies 20, 35 and 57 GW of AI deployments worldwide in 2026, 2027 and 2028~\cite{ref:gs_roic2026}; the 2028 figure is above this range's top and is the observable to test against utility energization data.

\paragraph{Siting deferral, $f_{\text{delay}}$ (10 / 20 / 35 \%).} Calibrated on Data Center Watch's \$130B per quarter of blocked or delayed projects against a pipeline of order \$1T. Data Center Watch tracks opposition outcomes and has an incentive to count broadly; its figures are project values, not annual spend, and most delayed projects eventually proceed elsewhere.

The range discounts for both. Improve with: an independent count; the fraction of delayed projects that were ultimately built. Likely: rising through 2027 as moratoria spread, then falling as developers move to jurisdictions that want them.

\paragraph{Site cost uplift, $\kappa_{\text{site}}$ (1.02 / 1.05 / 1.09).} Judgment: community-benefit agreements, water mitigation, private generation, relocation premia. Improve with: disclosed terms of any large community-benefit agreement.

\paragraph{Sovereignty capital multiplier, $\kappa_{\text{sov}}$ (1.1 / 1.2 / 1.3; overlay only).} Engineering judgment about utilization and scale of smaller in-jurisdiction facilities. No regulator publishes it. Kept out of the base case for that reason.

\subsection*{Demand side}

\paragraph{The 2026 base, $D_{2026}$ (161 / 179 / 198).} Seven components. Anthropic's first half is now filed (S-1); OpenAI's figures, and Anthropic's third quarter, still rest on figures the companies chose to disclose, in the months they chose to disclose them, on a basis (gross, for Anthropic) that flatters the figure; both companies were raising capital at the time. Run-rates overstate calendar bookings. The hyperscaler component depends on what Microsoft counts in its ``AI business,'' a basis it has not fully defined.

The advertising component is the least observable; Meta and Google attribute growth to AI on their calls without publishing the decomposition. The overlap correction is our own. The incentive on every component points upward. Improve with: January's 10-Ks (audited), an OpenAI S-1, and Anthropic's amended S-1 with third-quarter figures.

\emph{v28:} Anthropic's S-1 now gives an audited first half (\$16.3B), within the v27 range; the September press report of a \$100B+ exit run-rate, which had raised the register to \$187B, is not supported by it. Likely: calendar-2026 bookings near the mode; the run-rate figures will look generous in retrospect.

\paragraph{Cloud-capture share, $\theta$ (0.77 / 0.815 / 0.86), $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ ($\sim$0.635).} OpenAI's reported margin and Azure spend are leaked, unaudited figures; Anthropic's margin is undisclosed. The labs, raising capital, have the ordinary incentive to present improving margins. Improve with: the S-1; any audited lab financials.

Likely: $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ falls over time as the labs' margins improve --- which is good for the labs and bad for the infrastructure owners, and is the paper's central value-capture argument in one number.

\paragraph{Circular flows, $F_{\text{circ}}$ (8 / 12 / 18).} From related-party notes and press. Small; kept for completeness and because readers will look for it. Improve with: the 10-K notes.

\paragraph{Sector bases, $R_{s,0}$.} Sum to about \$205B, above the \$179B channel count because sectors count spend the channel count nets out; not independent evidence. Individual sector bases are judgment allocations. Improve with: any vendor's sector revenue disclosure.

\paragraph{Sector ceilings, $C_s$.} The paper's most consequential judgments. Each is a chain (Appendix~\ref{app:sectors}). The two that matter: office assistants (100 / 150 / 230), anchored on Microsoft's disclosed 20M paid seats --- Microsoft discloses seat counts at its discretion and has not committed to a regular series --- and on an IT-budget hard cap; and advertising (105 / 136 / 170), anchored on Meta's disclosed lifts --- disclosed by a company with an interest in AI being credited for ad growth.

Both have an upward incentive at the source and the ranges allow for it. The remaining thirteen are smaller and their errors average. Improve with: Copilot seats each quarter; Gartner's application-software category data; Meta's and Google's ad-revenue decomposition if ever given. Likely: assistants at or below the mode unless agents are trusted with whole workflows; advertising near the mode.

\paragraph{Ramp rates, $k_s$.} Judgment about time-to-saturation by sector, calibrated so that 2027 growth matches the labs' guided deceleration. Improve with: 2027 actuals.

\paragraph{Sector cloud shares, $\theta_s$.} Judgment about on-device, on-premise and integrator leakage by sector. The edge-siphon chain and the last-mile evidence support the shape; the levels are estimates. Improve with: any data on the share of enterprise inference running outside hyperscaler clouds (surveys exist; none are authoritative).

\paragraph{Common factors, $M_C$ (0.7 / 1.0 / 1.4), $M_k$ (0.7 / 1.0 / 1.3).} Judgment about how much macro and capability background the sectors share. This is where the residual doubt lives and it is stated as such. Improve only by experience.

\paragraph{Post-2030 ceiling growth, $g_C$ (6 / 10 / 14 \%/yr).} Generic; deliberately generous to the build-out. Should be tied to the torn-up budget pool (seat deflation, services shrinkage) in a later version, which would lower it.

\paragraph{European displacement, $g_{\text{EU}}$ (3 / 5 / 8 \% of sovereign-exposed demand by 2030).} From the sovereignty survey (a vendor of private email, with a stake in the answer), France's mandate, and the German state exits. Probably conservative given a national government has now moved. Improve with: procurement announcements; the 2027 survey. Likely: above the mode.

\paragraph{Sovereignty carve-out, $\gamma_{\text{sov}}$ (15 / 25 / 35 \%; overlay only).} Judgment; kept as an overlay.

\paragraph{Residual adoption factor (swept, not ranged).} The one input the paper refuses to assign a range to, because no 2026 data bear on it. It is whether agents become trustworthy with whole workflows and how fast enterprises reorganize around them. Our view, for what it is worth: the sector chains already assume substantial adoption; a residual factor of 1.5 on top of them --- what the plateau needs --- would mean the largest and fastest enterprise software adoption in history. Possible; not the base case.

\subsection*{Feasibility band}

\paragraph{Software and services pools (1440, 1870 \$B).} Gartner, whose forecasts moved 3.4 points on the same year within five months and who sells the forecasts. The levels are more reliable than the growth rates. Improve with: Gartner's next update; cross-check against IDC.

\paragraph{Seat share of software (0.50 / 0.55 / 0.60).} Judgment. Improve with: Gartner's application-versus-infrastructure split.

\paragraph{Seat-SaaS growth, $g_{\text{seat}}$ ($-6$ / 0 / $+5$ \%/yr; secondary).} The market has repriced the sector on the assumption of decline; Atlassian's seat decline is the first hard datum; vendors have an incentive to report consumption metrics in place of seat counts. Improve with: quarterly seat disclosures from the vendors that still give them. Likely: negative; the question is how negative.

\paragraph{Reallocation to AI (12 / 20 / 32 \%; secondary).} Pure judgment: how much of the seat and services money becomes AI-solution spend rather than savings returned to the CFO. The most improvable number in the paper with the least data. Improve with: any vendor's disclosure of AI-consumption revenue replacing seat revenue (Salesforce, ServiceNow, SAP); Gartner's 2027 category data. Likely: the mode, with the money arriving later than the seat losses.

\paragraph{Infrastructure software and services growth (4/8/12; $-2$/3/7 \%/yr).} Judgment; services could shrink faster if AI removes consulting hours quickly.

\paragraph{European pool displacement (10 / 20 / 35 \%).} As for $g_{\text{EU}}$; applied to the pool rather than to demand.

\paragraph{New AI spend (2 / 4 / 7 \%).} Judgment; small.

\paragraph{Advertising and consumer caps (140/170/200; 80/105/130).} From the decomposed ceilings; same sources and biases as above.

\subsection*{Incumbent case}

\paragraph{Microsoft 365 seats and price (420 / 450 / 480 M; \$19 / 21 / 23).} Widely quoted; not verified against a filing; Microsoft quotes seats selectively. The illustration hangs on these two numbers and they are flagged; a single disclosed seat series would settle it. Improve with: the 10-K segment revenue and the last disclosed seat count.

\paragraph{Price growth, $p$ (5 / 8 / 12 \%/yr).} From the announced July 2026 increases. Microsoft's incentive is to raise price to offset seats; it has done so.

\paragraph{Seat elasticity, $\epsilon$ (0.15 / 0.35 / 0.60; secondary).} Judgment; enterprise software has historically been sticky, agents and sovereignty exits make it less so. Improve with: renewal data, if any vendor ever discloses it.

\paragraph{Agent seat change, EU exit, attach.} Judgment ranges consistent with the sector chains; the seat trend is now anchored on Microsoft's disclosed +6--7\% commercial seat growth through FY26, from which the model assumes deceleration. Attach (12 / 22 / 35 \% by 2030, from $\sim$6.5\%) is the number that decides the illustration and the one a regular disclosure would settle.

\subsection*{Water and thermal}
\paragraph{WUE, evaporative share, $E_a$.} Engineering ranges from public sources (ASHRAE, JEDEC, operator sustainability reports --- which have an incentive to report improving WUE). Used in the text, not in the carry.

\subsection*{What we think is likely, in one paragraph}
The supply-side numbers will be settled by filings within two quarters and are unlikely to move the carry by more than 10\%. The demand-side base will come in near its mode once audited figures replace run-rates, and $\theta$ will drift down as the labs' margins improve.

The sector ceilings will hold except for office assistants, where the answer depends on agent adoption that no one can see from here, and advertising, where Meta and Google will keep attributing growth to AI whether or not the attribution is clean.

The budget pool will be smaller and more reallocated than the base case assumes, hurting seat-priced incumbents more than the aggregate. Siting friction will peak in 2027. The result --- a sunk deficit in 2027--28 under every path, a plateau that pays only with adoption far above what is visible, and a rational operator who cuts to \$400--500B and turns a yearly profit in 2032 without earning back the losses by 2035 --- is more likely to be moved by a capex decision than by any demand surprise. That decision is the thing to watch; the October calls pointed spending up, not down.

\section{Update Protocol: Keeping the Estimate Live}\label{app:update}
Every number in this paper is a function of a register of inputs, each with a source, a source date, the date its source next publishes, and the model parameter it feeds. The register (\texttt{parameters.json}), the model, and a rebuild script are deposited with the paper. Updating is mechanical: change the input, run the rebuild, read the delta.

\subsection*{The register}
Fifty-three entries in five groups --- supply (capex history, silicon share, lives, opex, WACC, inflation, lags, grid and siting), demand (the 2026 base and its components, cloud-capture share, sector ceilings, European displacement, common factors), feasibility (software and services pools, seat share, seat growth, reallocation), the incumbent worked case (seats, price, attach, elasticity), and the re-issue rule.

Entries marked \emph{secondary} are swept in their own figures rather than trusted at a range. Two commitments follow from the register: nothing enters the model except through it, so the provenance trail is complete; and any reader can substitute their own value and rebuild.

\subsection*{The re-issue rule}
After each rebuild the script reports the change in three summary statistics against the previous run: the 2030 plateau $z$-score, the 2030 plateau break-even growth $g^{*}$, and P(profit, 2030) under rationalization. A change is \emph{minor} if $|\Delta z|<0.5$, $|\Delta g^{*}|<5$ points and $|\Delta\text{P}|<15$ points: tables are updated, the version number is incremented, and no announcement is made.

A change is \emph{material} if any threshold is crossed, or if any registered 2026 observable arrives outside its P10--P90 range: the paper is re-issued with a delta log --- a table of every input that moved, its old and new range, its source, and the effect on the three statistics.

Cadence is set by the calendar: a rebuild after each Big-5 earnings week and after each Nvidia report, and a material re-issue whenever the rule fires.

\subsection*{A dry run}
To test the loop before it is needed: (written before the October calls on the Big-5-only count, kept as a worked example) suppose the calls had put 2026 capex at \$800B and calendar-2026 AI revenue at \$195B rather than \$179B. Rebuilding gives $g^{*}_{2030}=46\%$ (from 49\%), $z=-3.57$ (from $-3.54$) and P(profit, rationalize, 2030) two points lower: all three inside the minor thresholds.

The higher base helps the demand side, the higher capex hurts the supply side, and they nearly cancel. A disclosure that Copilot paid seats had reached 40M, by contrast, would move the assistant ceiling's floor and be reported as material.

Figures are regenerated from the v28 register. The v28 rebuild reproduces the results tables of the body (Tables~\ref{tab:breakeven} and~\ref{tab:bu_results}) to the precision quoted: those tables were computed on a \$179B base, while v27's abstract quoted a register run on the superseded \$187B base (\$255B deficit, $g^{*}=48\%$). Version 28 brings the register, the abstract and the tables into line. Version 28 then adds the builders outside the Big-5 (SpaceX/xAI, CoreWeave, Nebius, IREN, Applied Digital) to the carry; every table and figure is rebuilt on that full count, and the Big-5-only figures are quoted alongside where they help the comparison.

\subsection*{Commitment for this release}
Version 28 (29 October 2026) is the post-earnings rebuild: it carries the delta log above, the rebuilt tables and figures, the statement of what the calls did to the summary statistics, and the material change from counting every builder (What Is New in Version 28). Version 28.1 will add Nvidia (19 November), Oracle (December) and Anthropic's amended S-1 with third-quarter figures, whether or not the re-issue rule fires.

History: v26 (30 September) was the pre-earnings estimate; v27 (15 October) added the Goldman Sachs reconciliation. Register changes made between 29 and 30 September (the OpenAI and Anthropic September disclosures, the batch share, the effective-price tier) rebuilt as minor under the rule: $z$ $+0.07$, $g^{*}_{2030}$ $-1$ point, P(profit, rationalize, 2030) $+2$ points.

\subsection*{Formats}
The paper is released as LaTeX source and PDF, deposited on Zenodo with the register, model and figure scripts; the PDF is the reading copy and the source is the object of record. Word and other office formats are produced only for reviewers' comments and are not released.

\subsection*{What is not automated}
Judgment ranges --- the common factors, the residual adoption factor, the reallocation share, elasticities --- do not update from a feed and are re-examined only when a disclosure bears on them. The re-issue rule guards against the two failure modes an updated model is prone to: over-reacting to one quarter, and quietly drifting through many small changes that add up to a different paper.

\section{Glossary}\label{app:glossary}
Terms are grouped by discipline and listed alphabetically within each group; a table lists every model variable and parameter with its symbol, register name, units, base range and meaning; and a final section explains the phrases and idioms the paper relies on. Where a term is used with a paper-specific meaning, that meaning is given.

\subsection*{Paper-specific terms and model symbols}
\begin{description}\setlength{\itemsep}{1pt}
\item[AI-attributable capex ($A_c$)] Big-5 capex in year $c$ minus a non-AI baseline (fulfillment, ordinary cloud refresh). Only this part enters the carry.
\item[Big-5] Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Oracle --- the five whose capex Epoch AI tracks and whose infrastructure hosts most frontier AI.
\item[Break-even growth ($g^{*}$)] In percent per year (0.57 $=$ 57\%/yr). The constant annual growth rate of end-customer revenue from 2026 at which infrastructure receipts equal carry in a given year. Held constant by construction so that one number can be tested; the bottom-up growth is not constant (it decays).
\item[Carry ($H_t$)] The annual cost of owning the installed base in year $t$: depreciation plus facility opex plus a return on net book value. The revenue the base must earn just to stand still.
\item[Ceiling ($C_s$)] In \$B. A sector's maximum 2030 end-customer revenue, set by a physical or budgetary constraint (seats, IT budgets, ad yields, users). Given as a triangular range; the logistic never quite reaches it.
\item[Cloud-capture share ($\theta_s$; $\theta$)] Dimensionless, 0--1. The fraction of sector $s$'s end-customer revenue that becomes infrastructure receipts, after the lab's margin, on-device execution, on-premise deployment and integrator capture. $\theta$ without a subscript is the revenue-weighted average; $\theta_{\text{lab}}$ is the same quantity for the frontier-lab channel alone, i.e.\ the labs' compute cost as a share of their revenue.
\item[Cohort] Capital spent in one calendar year, tracked as a unit through its own depreciation life. A \emph{vintage model} sums the cohorts alive in each year.
\item[Common factor] A multiplier drawn once per Monte Carlo trial and applied to every sector's ceiling (and a second to every ramp), representing the macroeconomic and technological background all sectors share --- recession, rates, regulation, or a capability step. Without it, fifteen independent sectors would average to a falsely narrow total.
\item[End-customer revenue ($D_t$; $R_s(t)$)] What buyers --- enterprises, consumers, advertisers --- pay in year $t$ for AI products and services, in \$B. $D_t$ is the total; $R_s(t)$ is one sector's part of it, and $D_t=\sum_s R_s(t)$. It is counted \emph{before} the frontier labs take their margin and before the part that never reaches a cloud (leakage) is removed. See Figure~\ref{fig:flow}.
\item[Energization] Connecting built capacity to grid power so it can run. The \emph{lag} ($\ell$) is the share of a cohort energized in its spend year; the rest follows a year later.
\item[Feasibility cutoff / band] In \$B. The most that could be spent on AI in 2030 out of the enterprise software and services pool --- itself decomposed into seat-priced software, infrastructure software, and services, each with its own growth, less the share displaced from US vendors --- plus advertising and consumer caps. The cutoff is uncertain, so it is drawn as a band from its P10 to its P90 (\$920B--\$1.31T); its P50 is $\sim$\$1.10T.
\item[Fermi chain] A quantity estimated as a product of several separately-bounded factors, in the manner of Fermi's piano-tuner estimate. In this paper each factor is bounded to tens of percent, not to an order of magnitude; the Fermi label refers to the method of decomposition and propagation, not to the precision.
\item[Grid energization cap] Global capacity to connect new AI load each year, in GW/yr; capex above it is deferred.
\item[Infrastructure receipts (also ``receipts'', ``cloud receipts'')] The part of end-customer revenue that reaches the owners of the data centers, in \$B: $\text{Receipts}_t=\sum_s\theta_s R_s(t)$. This, not end-customer revenue, is what is compared with the carry.
\item[Last-mile operator] An external firm that delivers AI outcomes to an enterprise on gain-share pricing, typically on small open-weight models.
\item[Logistic] The S-shaped saturation curve $R(t)=C/[1+(C/R_0-1)e^{-k(t-t_0)}]$ with $t_0=2026$ and $R_0$ the 2026 value: growth that decays as the ceiling is approached.
\item[Plateau / Arms race / Rationalize / Adaptive] The forward capex paths: flat at the 2026 level of \$780B; the 2027 trajectory continued at \$1.0--1.1T (an \emph{arms race}: each operator building because the others are); a 35\% cut to \$500--550B; and an adaptive path in which capex tracks realized receipts (Appendix~\ref{app:horizon}).
\item[Ramp ($k_s$)] In units of per year. The rate parameter of the logistic curve; a sector covers half the remaining distance to its ceiling in about $\ln2/k_s$ years (0.9 years at $k=0.8$). It is not a growth rate and not ``$1+k$''.
\item[Residual adoption/capability factor] Dimensionless multiplier on 2030 receipts (1.0 $=$ the sector chains as estimated). It stands for what the chains cannot see from 2026: the pilots and seat counts visible today let one see roughly four years ahead, but not whether a capability step makes agents trustworthy with whole workflows. Because it cannot be estimated, it is swept as a what-if axis rather than assigned a range.
\item[Shell] Buildings, power distribution, cooling plant --- the long-lived (15--25 yr) part of a data center.
\item[Silicon share ($\phi$)] Fraction of AI-attributable capex that is accelerators, memory, networking and servers, as opposed to shell, power and cooling.
\item[Site cost uplift ($\kappa_{\text{site}}$)] Multiplier on shell cost for community-benefit agreements, water mitigation, dry cooling, private generation, relocation.
\item[Siting / consent deferral ($f_{\text{delay}}$)] Share of a cohort delayed a year by permitting, litigation, moratoria or relocation.
\item[Slack trap] The paper's term for productivity gains absorbed as organizational slack (verification, output inflation, fractional time savings) rather than realized as cash flow.
\item[Sovereignty overlay ($\kappa_{\text{sov}}$, $\gamma_{\text{sov}}$)] Capital duplication multiplier and demand carve-out from data-residency and procurement rules.
\item[Top-down vs bottom-up] Two independent demand estimates: a base grown at $g$; and fifteen sector chains summed. They share only the 2026 base.
\end{description}

Ranges are those of the current version; the register is authoritative.
